Kenneth Strongman wrote in the March 1970 issues of Science Journal: ÔAt the time we thought ourselves justified in concluding that a dominance structure of submission from eye contact exists and that this tends to approach hierarchy, particularly when the focus is on initial eye contact. Our reason for considering it to be concerned with dominance was based on a statement made by Argyle and Dean, who suggested that if A wants to dominate B he stares at him appropriately; B can accept this with a submissive expression or by looking away, or can challenge and outstare. However, S. E. Poppleton, a research student at Exeter, has since shown that the relationship between eye-glance submission hierarchies and an independent measure of dominance (provided by CatellÕs 16PF personality inventory) is an inverse one. Thus he who looks away first is the more dominant.Õ 
One might contrast this with other reports, like that of an experiment at Stanford University where it was found that drivers who had been stared at left traffic lights appreciably faster. Such disagreements indicate the difficulty of actually perceiving status transactions. In my view, breaking eye contact can be high status so long as you donÕt immediately glance back for a fraction of a second. If you ignore someone your status rises, if you feel impelled to look back then it falls. ItÕs as if the proper state of human beings is high, but that we modify ourselves to avoid conflicts. Posture experts (like Mathias Alexander) teach high-status postures as ÔcorrectÕ. ItÕs only to be expected that status is established not by staring, but by the reaction to staring. Thus dark glasses raise status because we canÕt see the submission of the eyes. 
I minimise Ôstatus resistanceÕ from my students by getting them to experience various status sensations before I discuss the implications, or even introduce the term. I might ask them to say something nice to the person beside them, and then to say something nasty. This releases a lot of laughter, and they are surprised to find that they often achieve the wrong effect. (Some people never really say anything nice, and others never say anything really nasty, but they wonÕt realise this.) 
I ask a group to mill about and say ÔhalloÕ to each other. They feel very awkward, because the situation isnÕt real. They donÕt know what status they should be playing. I then get some of the group to hold all eye contacts for a couple of seconds, while the others try to make and then break eye contacts and then immediately glance back for a moment. The group suddenly looks more like a ÔrealÕ group, in that some people become dominant, and others submissive. Those who hold eye contacts report that they feel powerfulÑ and actually look powerful. Those who break eye contact and glance back ÔfeelÕ feeble, and look it. The students like doing this, and are interested, and puzzled by the strength of the sensations. 
I might then begin to insert a tentative ÔerÕ at the beginning of each of my sentences, and ask the group if they detect any change in me. They say that I look ÔhelplessÕ and ÔweakÕ but they canÕt, interestingly enough, say what IÕm doing thatÕs different. I donÕt normally begin every sentence with ÔerÕ, so it should be very obvious. Then I move the ÔerÕ into the middle of sentences, and they say that they perceive me as becoming a little stronger. If I make the ÔerÕ longer, and move it back to the beginning of sentences, then they say I look more important, more confident. When I explain what I am doing, and let them experiment, theyÕre amazed at the different feelings the length and displacement of the ÔersÕ give them. They are also surprised that itÕs difficult to get some people to use a short ÔerÕ. There wouldnÕt seem to be any problem in putting an ÔerÕ lasting a fraction of a second at the beginning of each sentence, but many people unconsciously resist. They say ÔumÕ, or they elongate the sound. These are people who cling to their self-importance. The short ÔerÕ is an invitation for people to interrupt you; the long ÔerÕ says ÔDonÕt interrupt me, even though I havenÕt thought what to say yet.Õ 
Again I change my behaviour and become authoritative. I ask them what IÕve done to create this change in my relation with them, and whatever they guess to be the reasonÑÔYouÕre holding eye contactÕ, ÔYouÕre sitting straighterÕÑI stop doing, yet the effect continues. Finally I explain that IÕm keeping my head still whenever I speak, and that this produces great change in the way I perceive myself and am perceived by others. I suggest you try it now with anyone youÕre with. Some people find it impossible to speak with a still head, and more curiously, some students maintain that itÕs still while theyÕre actually jerking it about. I let such students practise in front of a mirror, or I use videotape. Actors needing authorityÑtragic heroes and so onÑhave to learn this still head trick. You can talk and waggle your head about if you play the gravedigger, but not if you play Hamlet. Officers are trained not to move the head while issuing commands. 
My belief (at this moment) is that people have a preferred status; that they like to be low, or high, and that they try to manoeuvre themselves into the preferred positions. A person who plays high status is saying ÔDonÕt come near me, I bite.Õ Someone who plays low status is saying ÔDonÕt bite me, IÕm not worth the trouble.Õ In either case the status played is a defence, and itÕll usually work. ItÕs very likely that you will increasingly be conditioned into playing the status that youÕve found an effective defence. You become a status specialist, very good at playing one status, but not very happy or competent at playing the other. Asked to play the ÔwrongÕ status, youÕll feel ÔundefendedÕ. 
I reassure my students, and encourage them, and let them have conversations together, trying out different ways of changing their status. One student might try moving very smoothly (high status) while his partner 
moves jerkily (low status).1 
One might keep putting his hands near his face while he speaks, and the other might try keeping his hands away from his face. One might try holding his toes pointing inwards (low status), while one sits back and spreads himself (high status). 
These are just tricks in order to get the students to experience status changes. If I speak with a still head, then IÕll do many other high-status things quite automatically. IÕll speak in complete sentences, IÕll hold eye contact. IÕll move more smoothly, and occupy more ÔspaceÕ. If I talk with my toes pointing inwards IÕm more likely to give a hesitant little ÔerÕ before each sentence, and IÕll smile with my teeth covering my bottom lip, and IÕll sound a little breathless, and so on. We were amazed to find that apparently unrelated things could so strongly influence each other; it didnÕt seem reasonable that the position of the feet could influence sentence structure and eye contact, but it is so. 
Once students have understood the concepts, and have been coaxed into experiencing the two states, then I get them to play scenes in which: (I) both lower status; (2) both raise status; (3) one raises while the other lowers; (4) the status is reversed during the scene. 
I insist that they have to get their status just a little above or below their partnerÕs. This ensures that they really ÔseeÕ their partner, as they have exactly to relate their behaviour to his. The automatic status skills then Ôlock on toÕ the other actor, and the students are transformed into observant, and apparently very experienced improvisers. Of course, they will have been playing status whenever they improvised, but it would be usually a personal status, not the status of a character. They would be relating to the problem of succeeding in the eyes of the audience. These status exercises reproduce on the stage exactly the effects of real life, in which moment by moment each person adjusts his status up or down a fraction. 
When actors are reversing status during a scene itÕs good to make them grade the transitions as smoothly as possible. I tell them that if I took a photograph every five seconds, IÕd like to be able to arrange the prints in order just by the status shown. ItÕs easy to reverse status in one jump. Learning to grade it delicately from moment to moment increases the control of the actor. The audience will always be held when a status is being modified. 
It isnÕt necessary for an actor to achieve the status heÕs trying to play in order to interest an audience. To see someone trying to be high, and failing, is just as delightful as watching him succeed. 
Here are some notes made by students who had just been introduced to status work. 
ÔThe using of different types of ÒerÓ found me swinging unavoidably from feeling now inferior, now superior, then inferior again. I found myself crossing my arms, fidgeting, walking with my hands in my pocketsÑall movements unnatural to me. I find myself suddenly freezing my body in order to check up on my status.Õ 
ÔNothing has been done in class that I didnÕt believe or ÒknowÓ. But I couldnÕt have stated it.Õ 
ÔDuring that scene with Judith in which she at first touched her head all the time, and then gradually stopped doing it, I couldnÕt define the change in her movements, and yet for some reason my attitude changed towards her. When she touched her head I tried to be more helpful, reassuring, whereas once she stopped, I felt more distant and businesslikeÑalso a bit more challengedÑwhereas previously IÕd felt nothing but sympathy.Õ 
ÔIÕve often been told that an actor should be aware of his body but I didnÕt understand this until I tried talking with my head still.Õ 
ÔThe most interesting revelation to me was that every time I spoke to someone I could tell if I felt submissive or the opposite. I then tried to play status games in secret with people I knew. Some people I thought I knew very well I wouldnÕt dare try it with. Other relatively new friends were easy to play status games with.Õ 
ÔSense of domination when I hold eye contact. Almost a pride in being able to look at someone else and have them look away. Looking away and backÑfelt persecuted. As if everyone was trying to crush me underfoot.Õ 
ÔStatusÑclothes not important. I was walking to the shower with only a towel over my shoulder when I met a fully dressed student who took on a very low-status look and allowed me to pass on my way.Õ 
ÔEvery time I speak to someone I can now tell if IÕm submissive or not.Õ 
ÔIÕve always thought that the man I should like to marry should be smarter than me; someone I could look up to and respect. Well, my boyfriend is now smarter than me, and I usually respect his knowledge, but often I find his high status a nuisance. Perhaps I should seek someone I consider IÕm on the same level with?Õ 
ÔI felt the dominant figure in the conversation and proceeded to try and subjugate myself to her whims. I did this by the Òtouch the head and faceÓ method. What happened here is that, while prior to this move I had done most of the talking and directed the conversation, after this . . . I was hard put to get a word in edgeways.Õ 
ÔI find that when I slow my movements down I go up in status.Õ 
ÔI felt as if all the world had suddenly been revealed to me. I realise that when I talk to people, my attitude of inner feeling is of almost talking down to them.Õ 
ItÕs a good idea to introduce a bystander into a status scene with instructions to Ôtry not to get involvedÕ. If you are a ÔcustomerÕ in a ÔrestaurantÕ, and someone at the same table quarrels with the ÔwaiterÕ, then your very subtle status manoeuvrings are a delight to watch. 
I increase the confidence of the actors by getting them to play sequences of status exercises. For example, a breakfast scene in which a husband plays low and a wife lays high, might be followed by an office scene in which the husband plays high to a secretary who plays low, leading on to a supper scene in which both wife and husband play lowÑand so on. Once the status becomes automatic, as it is in life, itÕs possible to improvise complex scenes with no preparation at all. The status exercises are really crutches to support the actor so that instinctual systems can operate. The actor then feels that everything is easy, and he doesnÕt experience himself as ÔactingÕ any more than he does in life, even though the actual status heÕs playing may be one very unfamiliar to him. 
Without the status work my improvisation group, the Theatre Machine, could never have toured successfully in Europe; not without preparing the scenes first. If someone starts a scene by saying ÔAh, another sinner! WhatÕs it to be, the lake of fire or the river of excrement?Õ then you canÕt ÔthinkÕ fast enough to know how to react. You have to understand that the scene is in Hell, and that the other person is some sort of devil, and that youÕre dead all in a split second. If you know what status youÕre playing the answers come automatically. 
ÔWell?Õ 
ÔExcrementÕ, you say, playing high status, without doing anything you experience as ÔthinkingÕ at all, but you speak in a cold voice, and you look around as if Hell was less impressive than youÕd been led to believe. If youÕre playing low status you say ÔWhich ever you think best, SirÕ, or whatever. Again with no hesitation, and with eyes full of terror, or wonder. 
All this isnÕt so far away from Stanislavsky as some people might suppose, even though in Creating a Role Stanislavsky wrote: ÔPlay the external plot in terms of physical actions. For example: enter a room. But since you cannot enter unless you know where you came from, where you are going and why, seek out the external facts of the plot to give you a basis for physical actions.Õ (From Appendix A.) Some ÔmethodÕ actors take this to mean that they have to know all the Ôgiven circumstancesÕ before they can improvise. If I ask them to do something spontaneous they react as if theyÕve been asked to do something indecent. This is the result of bad teaching. In order to enter a room all you need is what status you are playing. The actor who understands this is free to improvise in front of an audience with no given circumstances at all! Interestingly enough Stanislavsky himself would almost certainly agree. In Chapter Eight of Creating a Role he makes the director, Tortsov, tell the narrator: 
Ô ÒGo up on the stage and play for us KhlestakovÕs entrance in the second act.Ó 
Ô ÒHow can I play it since I donÕt know what I have to do?Ó said I with surprise and objection in my tone. 
Ô ÒYou do not know everything but you do know some things. So play the little that you know. In others words, execute out of the life of the part those small physical objectives which you can do sincerely, truthfully, and in your own person.Ó 
Ô ÒI canÕt do anything because I donÕt know anything!Ó 
Ô ÒWhat do you mean?Ó objected Tortsov. ÒThe play says, ÔEnter Khlestakov.Õ DonÕt you know how to go into a room in an inn?Ó 
Ô ÒI do.Ó Õ 
What I think he ÔknowsÕ is that he must play a particular status. 
One way to teach transitions of status is to get students to leave the class, and then come in through the real door and act Ôentering the wrong roomÕ. ItÕs then quite normal to see students entering with head down, or walking backwards, or in some other way that will prevent them from seeing that it is the wrong room. They want time to really enter before they start ÔactingÕ. They will advance a couple of paces, act seeing the audience, and leave in a completely phoney way. 
I remind students that entering the wrong room is an experience we all have, and that we always know what to do, since we do ÔsomethingÕ. I explain that IÕm not asking the students to ÔactÕ, but just to do what they do in life. We have a radar which scans every new space for dangers, an early-warning system programmed-in millions of years ago as a protection against sabre-tooth tigers, or bigger amoebas or whatever. ItÕs therefore very unusual to refuse to look into the space you are entering. 
As soon as the Ôwrong roomÕ exercise becomes ÔrealÕ they understand that a change of status is involved. You prepare a status for one situation, and have to alter it when suddenly confronted by the unexpected one. I then set the students to predetermine the direction of the status change, and of course errors are often made. Someone trying to play low status may have to be told to smile, and if he smiles with both sets of teeth (an aggressive smile) he may have to be asked to show the top teeth only. People who want to rise in status may have to be told to turn their backs to us when they leave. Neither smiling nor turning your back is essential but it may help the student get the feeling. In difficult cases it helps to use videotape. 
A more complex version of this exercise is really a little play. I invented it at RADA when I was asked if I could push the students into more emotional experiences. ItÕs for one characterÑletÕs say heÕs a teacher, although he could be any profession. He arrives late carrying the register and a pair of glasses. He says something like ÔAll right, quiet there, now thenÕ, treating us as the class. As he is about to read the register he puts the glasses on, and sees not his class, but a meeting of the School Governors. He apologises, dropping in status frantically, and struggles to the door, which sticks. He wrestles with it and after about ten seconds it comes free. The actor feels a very great drop in status when the door jams. It takes him back to feelings he may not have experienced since childhood: feelings of impotence, and of the hostility of objects. 
Once outside, the actor either stops the exercise, or if he feels brave, re.enters, and plays the scene again and again. This exercise can turn people into crumbling wrecks in a very short time, and for actors who like to ÔpretendÕ without actually feeling anything, it can be a revelation. One Scandinavian actor who apparently had never really achieved anything because of his self-consciousness, suddenly ÔunderstoodÕ and became marvellous. It was for him a moment of satori. The terrifying thing is that thereÕs no limit. For example: 
ÔWhy didnÕt anyone tell me that the room had been changed? I just made a complete fool of myself in front of the Governors.Õ (Puts on glasses. Sees Governors.) 
ÔAugh! I . . . I . . . what can I say. Mr Headmaster . . . please . . . I . . . oh . . . do excuse me . . . The door. IÕm afraid . . . it sticks . . . the damp weather, you . . . ah . . . so . . . so sorry.Õ 
(Grovels out. Re-enters.) ÔOh God, itÕs nice to find someone in the staff-room. Is there any tea on? The most embarrassing thing just happened to me . . . I . . .Õ (Puts on glasses. Sees Governors.) 
ÔOh . . . I . . . what must you think of me . . . I . . . I seem to be having some sort of breakdown . . . havenÕt been well lately. So sorry to interrupt . . . The . . . the door . . . THE DOOR! Augh! . . . IÕm sorry . . . outrageous conduct . . . please understand . . . er . . . er . . .Õ 
(Exit. Re-enters.) ÔItÕs nice of you to see me at such short notice. I know that psychiatrists are very busy . . . I . . .Õ 
(Puts on glasses. See Governors.) Ô. . . I know it was wrong to commit suicide, God, but . . .Õ (Puts on glasses. See Governors.) 
I wouldnÕt push anyone into playing this game, and it must be understood by the class that people are allowed to get upset, and are not to be punished by being considered exhibitionistic or cissy. 
I repeat all status exercises in gibberish, just to make it quite clear that the things said are not as important as the status played. If I ask two actors to meet, with one playing high, and one playing low, and to reverse the status while talking an imaginary language, the audience laugh amazingly. We donÕt know whatÕs being said, and neither do the actors, but the status reversal is enough to enthral us. If youÕve seen great comedians working in a language you donÕt understand youÕll know what I mean. 
I get the actors to learn short fragments of text and play every possible status on them. For example, A is late, and B has been waiting for him. 
A: Hallo. 
B: Hallo. 
A: Been waiting long? 
B: Ages. The implication is that B lowers A, but any status can be played. If both play high then A might stroll on with Ôall the time in the worldÕ and say ÔHalloÕ as if he wasnÕt late at all. B might hold eye contact and say ÔHalloÕ with emphasis. A might look away airily and say Ôbeen waiting long?Õ with a sigh as if B were being ÔdifficultÕ. ÔAges,Õ says B, staring at him, or walking off as if expecting A to follow. If both are to play low, then A might arrive running; B might stand up, bend the head forwards and give a low-status smile. ÔHallo,Õ says A breathlessly, showing embarrassment that B has stood. ÔHallo,Õ replies B, also a little breathless. ÔBeen waiting long?Õ asks A with anxiety. ÔAges,Õ says B with a weak laugh as if making a feeble joke. 
HereÕs a dialogue taking place in ÔSirÕsÕ office. 
SIR: Come in. Ah, sit down Smith. I suppose you know why I sent for you? SMITH: No, Sir. (Sir pushes a newspaper across the desk.) 
SMITH: I was hoping you wouldnÕt see that. SIR: You know we canÕt employ anyone with a criminal 
record. SMITH: WonÕt you reconsider? SIR: Good-bye, Smith. SMITH: I never wanted your bloody job anyway. 
(Exit Smith.) 
If Smith plays high, then saying ÔWonÕt you reconsiderÕ gives him an enormous resistance to work against. When Sir says ÔGood-bye, SmithÕ low status it produces a gripping scene. For Smith to say ÔI didnÕt want your bloody job anywayÕ low status, it may be necessary for him to burst into tears. 
One interesting complication in such a scene is that Smith will have to play low status to the space, even when playing high status to Sir, or itÕll look like his office. Conversely, Sir must play high status to the space, even when playing low status to Smith. If he doesnÕt heÕll look like an intruder. ÔMove about,Õ I say. ÔAnswer the telephone. Walk over to the window.Õ 
Status is played to anything, objects as well as people. If you enter an empty waiting-room you can play high or low status to the furniture. A king may play low status to a subject, but not to his palace. 
An actor is waiting on stage for someone to enter and play a scene with him. ÔWhat status are you playing?Õ I ask. He says, ÔI havenÕt started yet.Õ ÔPlay low status to the bench,Õ I say. 
He looks around him as if he was in a park that he suspects may be private. Then he ÔseesÕ a pigeon, and mimes feeding it, rather unconvincingly. ÔPlay low status to the pigeon,Õ I say, and immediately his mime improves, and the scene is believable. More ÔpigeonsÕ arrive, and one lands on the bench and starts pecking at the bread heÕs holding. Another lands on his arm, and then shits on him. He wipes the mess off surreptitiously. And so on. He doesnÕt need another actor to play status scenes with. He can do it with anything in the environment. 
I give students a very strong feeling of ÔstatusÕ by making them use only the way they look and sound to ward off attacks. I call it Ônon-defenceÕ, but really itÕs one of the best of all defences. Imagine two siblings, one of whom (A) lives in the flat of the other (B). B enters and asks if any letters have arrived for him. A says that there is one on the sideboard. B picks it up and sees itÕs been opened. A is always opening BÕs letters which causes conflict between them. The scene will probably develop something like this: 
B: Why did you open my letter? 
A: Is it open? 
B: You always open my letters. 
A: I donÕt know who did it. 
B: No one else has been here! B will probably start to push A about, and IÕll have to stop the scene for fear that they might hurt each other. 
I start the scene again, but tell A that he is to admit everything, while playing low status. 
B: Did you open my letter? 
A: 
Yes. B stops the attack. He pauses. 

B: Yes? 
A: Yes. 
B: Well, what did you do it for? 

A:
 I wanted to see what was inside. B is checked again. He may step backwards. He may even retreat to the furthest wall and lean against it. I encourage him to be angry, and to close in on A. 


B: How dare you open my letters? 
A: YouÕre right to be angry. IÕm a shit. 
B: I told you never to open my letters. 
A: I always do it. 
B: You do? B finds it increasingly difficult to press home his attack. If he starts to shake A, then A must cry and keep nodding his head, and saying ÔYouÕre right, youÕre right.Õ B can of course override his instinctsÑ human beings do unfortunately have this abilityÑbut the more he attacks, the more strongly a mysterious wind seems to be trying to blow him away. If A makes an error, and rises in status, then B closes in, but if the low status is maintained then B has to consciously ÔforceÕ his anger. 
B: Well, donÕt open them again. 
A: I poke my nose into everything. 
IÕve seen the low-status player leap about with joy and roll over and over on the floor after playing such a scene. ItÕs exhilarating to be controlling the movements of the other person as if he were a puppet. When I explained that the more A accepted BÕs dominance the more powerfully B was deflected, B said, ÔThatÕs right. I thought, ÒMother put him up to it.Ó Õ 
Non-defence is exploited by the wolf who exposes his neck and underbelly to a dominant wolf as a way of ending a losing battle. The top Wolf wants to bite, but canÕt. Some Congolese soldiers dragged two white journalists out of a jeep, shot one and were about to shoot the other when he burst into tears. They laughed and kicked him back to the jeep and let him drive away, while they waved and cheered. It was more satisfying to see the white man cry than to shoot him. 
Once non-defence has been mastered together with a low-status attitude, I teach it as a high-status exercise. The same kind of dialogue occurs: 
B: Did you open my letter? 
A: Yes. (Quite calmly, and sipping his coffee as if no attack was being made on him.) 
B: Yes? (Momentarily nonplussed.) 
A: I always open your letters. (Dismissively.) (And so on.) 
At first hardly anyone can carry off such a scene. They pretend to be high status, but you can see that theyÕre actually crumbling. I explain that they are making concealed low-status movements. An arm climbs up to the back of the chair as if wanting to flee from the aggressor, or to hold on for support. A foot starts to tap as if it wanted to go. 
The best solution IÕve found is to weight the situation heavily in favour of A. For example, I might set the scene in AÕs house, with B a guest who arrived late the night before, and now meets his host for the first time at breakfast. I make BÕs position worse by setting him the problem asking A for his daughterÕs hand in marriage. Once AÕs position is reinforced in this way he should be able to maintain his high status while making no verbal defence at all. 
A: You must be John . . . 
B: Er . . . yes. 
A: Cynthia tells me you want to marry her . . . 
B: ThatÕs right. 
A: Oh, by the way, a letter came for you this morning. 
B: ItÕs been opened. 
A: I open everyoneÕs letters. 
B: But it was addressed to me. 
A: ItÕs from your mother. Some of it I thought most unsuitable. YouÕll see I crossed some paragraphs out . . . (And so on.) 

4 Insults 
If you can get the students to insult each other playfully, then the status work will become easier. Playing scenes with custard pies might be equally liberating, but IÕve never had the opportunity. Once you can accept being insulted (the insult is the verbal equivalent of the custard pie), then you experience a great elation. The most rigid, self-conscious, and defensive people suddenly unbend. 
ItÕs no good just asking the students to insult each other. ItÕs too personal. If youÕve just called someone Ôkipper feetÕ itÕs disconcerting to suddenly notice that heÕs flat-footed. If your ears stick out then itÕs upsetting to be called Ôcloth earsÕ. On the other hand it is important for an actor to accept being insulted. The stage becomes an even more ÔdangerousÕ area if you canÕt admit your disabilities. The young George Devine cried once because the audience laughed when the character he was playing was referred to as thin. I remember a flat-chested actress being destroyed on stage because an adolescent shouted out that she was a man. The actor or improviser must accept his disabilities, and allow himself to be insulted, or heÕll never really feel safe. 
My solution is to remove all responsibility for the choice of insult from the person doing the insulting. I divide the class into two halves, and get each group to write out a list of names that would insult people: fool, slut, pig, arsehole, jerk, meatface, dumbhead, flatfoot, pigeyes, skinny twat, bugeyes, buckteeth, cowflop, monkeyface, swine, rathead, shitnose, bullshitter, faggot. Only half the class know who suggested a particular insult, and each suggestion has already been stamped with half the classÕs approval. 
I put the lists aside and get the students to play ÔshopÕ. 
ÔCan I help you?Õ 
ÔYes, IÕd like a pair of shoes.Õ 
ÔWould these do?Õ 
ÔIÕd like another colour.Õ 
ÔIÕm afraid this is the only colour we have, Sir.Õ 
ÔAh. Well, perhaps a hat.Õ 
ÔIÕm afraid thatÕs my hat, Sir.Õ And so onÑvery boringly, with both actors ÔblockingÕ the transaction in order to make the scene more ÔinterestingÕ (which it doesnÕt). 
I explain that I donÕt want them to make the scene ÔinterestingÕ, that they are just to buy and sell something. They start again. 
ÔCan I help you?Õ 
ÔJust browsing.Õ 
ÔNo youÕre not,Õ I say. ÔBuy something.Õ 
ÔI want a hat.Õ 
ÔHow about this one, Sir?Õ 
ÔBuy it,Õ I say. 
ÔIÕll buy it.Õ 
ÔTwo pounds ninety, Sir.Õ 
ÔHere.Õ 
ÔIÕm afraid I donÕt have any change, Sir.Õ 
ÔYes you do,Õ I insist. 
I have to struggle with the actors before they will agree just to buy something and sell something. Then I get them to play the scene again, but adding the insults. I give them a list each, and get them to add an insult to the end of each sentence. This idea delights everyone, but itÕs very boring. 
ÔCan I help you, fool?Õ 
ÔYes, bugeyes!Õ 
ÔDo you want a hat, slut?Õ (And so on.) 
I explain that insulting is of no interest. What we really want to see is someone being insulted. The interest we have in custard pies is in seeing them hit people. I tell them to repeat every insult in disbelief and outrage. As soon as they do this the performers get deeply involved and are often impossible to stop. People who are bound physically relax into a greater physical freedom. Gestures flow instead of being suddenly jerked to a stop. 
ÔCan I help you, pig?Õ 
ÔPig! Why I . . . Flat-footed pig yourself!Õ 
ÔMe! Flat-footed pig! You call me a flat-footed pig, you . . . you arsehole!Õ 
ÔArsehole!Õ 
ÔBuy something!Õ I shout. 
ÔI want a hat, buckteeth!Õ 
ÔBuckteeth! Try this for size, jerk!Õ 
ÔJerk! Jerk! You call me jerk! IÕll take itÑCowflop!Õ 
ÔCowflop!Õ (And so on.) 
The insults must remain an ornamentation on the scene, they mustnÕt become the scene itself. Once this is understood they can be applied to any situation. If you keep changing the lists then the most terrible things will have been said to everybody. I then give one actor a list, and let the other actor make up his own, and I set up scenes with several people. For example master-servant teams can meet, and insult in this way. But there must be some purpose theyÕre trying to achieve as well as Ôbeing insultedÕ. In my experience this game is very ÔreleasingÕ. The status lowering is so drastic, and at the same time so pleasurable, that ordinary status scenes hold fewer terrors. 
In the average school the teachers are supposed to inhibit their pupils, and the kind of healing openness typical of therapy groups simply isnÕt possible, but the game is useful even with censored lists, or even in gibberish. Gibberish imposes an acceptance of the insult, or no one can understand whatÕs happening. The technique is to repeat the last sound of any gibberish sentence. 
ÔGort intok horntow lipnol.Õ 
ÔLipnol! Lipnol! Grant hork lop sonto inkutu!Õ 
ÔInkutu! Die gorno inkutu! Krankon!Õ 
ÔKrankon!Õ (And so on.) 
This insult game can be played between two groups who slowly approach each other, but the teacher must ensure that every insult is received. Enormous energy is released, with individuals running forwards to hurl their insults, and then being dragged back by their friends. The target of the insults should be the opposing group, not individual members. The game usually ends with actors standing face to face and screaming each other, and everyone having a very good time. If theyÕve got ÔhighÕ on the game, you can make them repeat it in mime. It makes a good ÔasideÕ exercise if actors who are insulted repeat the word to the audience. 

5 Status Specialists 
If you wish to teach status interactions, itÕs necessary to understand that however willing the student is consciously, there may be very strong subconscious resistances. Making the student safe, and getting him to have confidence in you, are essential. You then have to work together with the student, as if you were both trying to alter the behaviour of some third person. ItÕs also important that the student who succeeds at playing a status he feels to be alien should be instantly rewarded, praised and admired. ItÕs no use just giving the exercises and expecting them to work. You have to understand where the resistance is, and devise ways of getting it to crumble. Many teachers donÕt recognise that thereÕs a problem because they only exploit the ÔpreferredÕ status. In a bad drama school itÕs possible to play your ÔpreferredÕ status all the time, since they cast you to type, exploiting what you can do, instead of widening your range. In the professional theatre actors divide up roughly into high-status and low-status specialists. The actorsÕ directory Spotlight used to have the high-status specialists at the front (called ÔstraightÕ), followed by the low-status specialists (called Ôcharacter actorsÕ), followed by children, and then dogs. This isnÕt as bad as it sounds, but itÕs symptomatic of the tendency for actors to overspecialise. A proper training for actors would teach all types of transaction. 
Some problems: there are students who will report no change of sensation when they alter their eye-contact patterns. If you observe them closely youÕll see that the ones who always play low status in life wonÕt ever hold eye contact long enough to feel dominant. When high-status specialists break eye contact and glance back, theyÕll be holding the glance back for at least a second, which is too long. You may have to precisely control the length of time that they look before they experience the change of sensation. Then theyÕll say, ÔBut it feels wrong.Õ This feeling of wrongness is the one they have to learn as being correct. 
I remember a girl who always played high status in improvisations, and who had never experienced safety and warmth as a performer. When I asked her to put a short ÔerÕ in front of each sentence she used the long ÔeeeerrrrrÕ but denied she was doing it. When I asked her to move her head as she talked she moved it in an abstract way, as if watching a fly circling in front of her. I asked her to play low status with an expert low-status improviser, but she held on to herself tightly with her arms and crossed her legs as if refusing to let her partner ÔinvadeÕ her. I asked her to unfold and then to tilt her head and suddenly she was transformedÑwe wouldnÕt have recognised her. She became soft and yielding and really seemed to enjoy the feelings that flooded into her, and she acted with feeling and rapport for the first time. Now that sheÕs learned to play low status with a low-status partner she can learn to play it with a high-status partner. 
Another student refused to play high status in anything but a wooden manner. He said that he lived in a working-class area and that he didnÕt want to be stuck-up. I explained that I wasnÕt trying to remove his present skills, which were very necessary, but only to add a few new ones. He believed that it was necessary to play low status within his working-class community, not realising that you can play high or low in any situation. His problem is that he plays low status well and he wonÕt experiment with other skills. 
I asked him to play a scene in which he was to tell his father he had VD. I chose the scene in order to stir him up, and involve his real feelings. All young men have anxieties in that area. He acted out a scene with no conviction at all, and tried to think up Ôclever thingsÕ to say. 
ÔIÕll give you the dialogue,Õ I said. ÔEnter. Go to the window. Look out, then turn and say youÕve got VD.Õ 
He did this. He looked out of the window and immediately made trivial movements, and dropped down in status. 
I stopped him. I explained that if he turned from the window, looked at his father and didnÕt move his head, then heÕd experience exactly the sensations he was trying to avoid. I said that he mustnÕt try to suppress the head movements but that he must be aware when he does them, and then somehow feel so dominant that he no longer needs to make them. When he repeats the scene itÕs his father who breaks eye contact and starts to crumble. From this beginning the student can learn to play characters of any social class, and make them high or low. 

6 Space 
I canÕt avoid talking about ÔspaceÕ any longer, since status is basically territorial. Space is very difficult to talk about, but easy to demonstrate. 
When I was commissioned to write my first play IÕd hardly been inside a theatre, so I watched rehearsals to get the feel of it. I was struck by the way space flowed around the actors like a fluid. As the actors moved I could feel imaginary iron filings marking out the force fields. This feeling of space was strongest when the stage was uncluttered, and during coffee breaks, or when they were discussing some difficulty. When they werenÕt acting, the bodies of the actors continually adjusted. As one changed position so all the others altered their postures. Something seemed to flow between them. When they were ÔactingÕ each actor would pretend to relate to the others, but his movements would stem from himself. They seemed ÔencapsulatedÕ. In my view itÕs only when the actorÕs movements are related to the space heÕs in, and to the other actors, that the audience feel Ôat oneÕ with the play. The very best actors pump space out and suck it in, or at least thatÕs what it feels like. When the movements are not spontaneous but ÔintellectualÕ the production may be admired, but you donÕt see the whole audience responding in empathy with the movements of the actors. 
HereÕs Stanislavsky describing a performance by Salvini, an actor who obviously used space in the way I mean: 
ÔSalvini approached the platform of the Doges, thought a little while, concentrated himself and, unnoticed by any of us, took the entire audience of the great theatre into his hands. It seemed that he did this with a single gestureÑthat he stretched his hand without looking into the public, grasped all of us in his palm, and held us there as if we were ants or flies. He closed his fist, and we felt the breath of death; he opened it, and we felt the warmth of bliss. We were in his power, and we will remain in it all our lives. . .Õ 
The movement teacher Yat Malmgren told me that as a child heÕd discovered that he didnÕt end at the surface of his body, but was actually an oval ÔSwiss cheeseÕ shape. To me, this is Ôclosed-eyeÕ space, and you experience it when you shut your eyes and let your body feel outwards into the surrounding darkness. Yat also talked about people who were cut off from sensing areas of themselves. ÔHe has no arms,Õ he would say, or ÔShe has no legsÕ, and you could see what he meant. When I investigated myself I found many areas that I wasnÕt experiencing, and my feelings are still defective. What I did find was another shape besides the ÔSwiss cheeseÕ shape: a parabola sweeping ahead of me like a cometÕs tail. When I panic, this parabola crushes in. In stage fright space contracts into a narrow tunnel down which you can just about walk without bumping into things. In cases of extreme stage fright the space is like a plastic skin pressing on to you and making your body rigid and bound. The opposite of this is seen when a great actor makes a gesture, and itÕs as if his arm has swept right over the heads of the people sitting at the back of the audience. 
Many acting teachers have spoken of ÔradiationsÕ, and they often sound like mystics. HereÕs Jean-Louis Barrault: 
ÔJust as the earth is surrounded by an atmosphere, the living human being is surrounded by a magnetic aura which makes contact with the external objects without any concrete contact with the human body. This aura, or atmosphere, varies in depth according to the vitality of human beings. . . 
ÔThe mime must first of all be aware of this boundless contact with things. There is no insulating layer of air between the man and the outside world. Any man who moves about causes ripples in the ambient world in the same way a fish does when it moves in the water.Õ (The Theatre of Jean-Louis Barrault, Barrie and Rockcliff, 1961.) 
This isnÕt very scientific, but like all magical language it does communicate a way an actor can ÔfeelÕ. If I stand two students face to face and about a foot apart theyÕre likely to feel a strong desire to change their body position. If they donÕt move theyÕll begin to feel love or hate as their ÔspaceÕ streams into each other. To prevent these feelings theyÕll modify their positions until their space flows out relatively unhindered, or theyÕll move back so that the force isnÕt so powerful. High-status players (like high-status seagulls) will allow their space to flow into other people. Low-status players will avoid letting their space flow into other people. Kneeling, bowing and prostrating oneself are all ritualised low-status ways of shutting off your space. If we wish to humiliate and degrade a low-status person we attack him while refusing to let him switch off. A sergeant-major will stand a recruit to attention and then scream at his face about an inch away. Crucifixion exploits this effect, which is why itÕs such a powerful symbol as compared to, say, boiling someone in oil. 
Imagine a man sitting neutrally and symmetrically on a bench. If he crosses his left leg over his right then youÕll see his space flowing over to the right as if his leg was an aerofoil. If he rests his right arm along the back of the bench youÕll see his space flowing out more strongly. If he turns his head to the right, practically all his space will be flowing in the same direction. Someone who is sitting neutrally in the ÔbeamÕ will seem lower-status. Every movement of the body modifies its space. If a man who is sitting neutrally crosses his left wrist over his right the space flows to his right, and vice versa. ItÕs very obvious that the top hand gives the direction, but the class are amazed. The difference seems so trivial, yet they can see itÕs a quite strong effect. 
The body has reflexes that protect it from attack. We have a Ôfear-crouchÕ position in which the shoulders lift to protect the jugular and the body curls forward to protect the underbelly. ItÕs more effective against carnivores than against policemen jabbing at your kidneys, but it evolved a long time ago. The opposite to this fear crouch is the Ôcherub postureÕ, which opens all the planes of the body: the head turns and tilts to offer the neck, the shoulders turn the other way to expose the chest, the spine arches slightly backwards and twists so that the pelvis is in opposition to the shoulders exposing the under-bellyÑand so on. This is the position I usually see cherubs carved in, and the opening of the body planes is a sign of vulnerability and tenderness, and has a powerful effect on the onlooker. High-status people often adopt versions of the cherub posture. If they feel under attack theyÕll abandon it and straighten, but they wonÕt adopt the fear crouch. Challenge a low-status player and heÕll show some tendency to slide into postures related to the fear crouch. 
When the highest-status person feels most secure he will be the most relaxed person, as for example in the opening scenes of KozintsevÕs film of King Lear. A solemn ceremony is arranged, the daughters take their places, an atmosphere of expectancy is built up, and then Lear (Juri Jarvet) enters as if he owns the place, warms his hands at the fire, and Ômakes himself at homeÕ. The effect is to enormously elevate Lear in status. Lears who strain to look powerful and threatening in this opening scene miss the point, which is that Lear is so confident, and trustful, that he willingly divides his kingdom and sets in motion his own destruction. 
Status can also be affected by the shape of the space you are in. The corners of couches are usually high-status, and high-status ÔwinnersÕ are allowed to take them. If you leave a car in the middle of a great wilderness there is a moment when you Ômove out of the space of the carÕ. In the wilderness the effect is very strong, for people always like to be beside objects. Thrones are usually set against walls and often have a canopy set high up below the ceilingÑpossibly a relic of the need to swing up into trees in emergencies. 
Imagine an empty beach. The first family to arrive can sit anywhere, but theyÕll either take up position against some rocks, or sit a third of the way in Ñsupposing itÕs all equally sandy. In my part of England, where there are many small beaches, the next family to appear might well move on to the next beach, regarding the first one as ÔclaimedÕ. If they do move in theyÕll stake out Ôtheir part of the beachÕ, away from the first group. If they sat close to the first group then theyÕd have to make friends, which could be difficult. If they sat close without making friends, then the first group would react with alarm. ÔCloseÕ is a concept related to the amount of space available. Once the beach fills up with people you can sit very close to the original family. The space people demand around them contracts as more people are added. Finally as the beach reaches saturation people stare at the sky, or roll in to face their friends, or cover their faces with newspaper or whatever. 
People will travel a long way to visit a ÔviewÕ. The essential element of a good view is distance, and preferably with nothing human in the immediate foreground. When we stand on a hill and look across fifty miles of emptiness at the mountains, we are experiencing the pleasure of having our space flow out unhindered. As people come in sight of a view, itÕs normal for their posture to improve and for them to breathe better. You can see people remarking on the freshness of the air, and taking deep breaths, although itÕs the same air as it was just below the brow of the hill. Trips to the sea, and our admiration of mountains are probably symptoms of overcrowding. 
Approach distances are related to space. If I approach someone on open moorland I have to raise an arm and shout Ôexcuse meÕ as soon as IÕm within shouting distance. In a crowded street I can actually brush against people without having to interact. 
Imagine that two strangers are approaching each other along an empty street. ItÕs straight, hundreds of yards long and with wide pavements. Both strangers are walking at an even pace, and at some point one of them will have to move aside in order to pass. You can see this decision being made a hundred yards or more before it actually ÔneedsÕ to be. In my view the two people scan each other for signs of status, and then the lower one moves aside. If they think theyÕre equal, both move aside, but the position nearest the wall is actually the strongest. If each person believes himself to be dominant a very curious thing happens. They approach until they stop face to face, and do a sideways dance, while muttering confused apologies. If a little half-blind lady wanders into your path this ÔmirrorÕ dance doesnÕt happen. You move out of her way. ItÕs only when you think the other person is challenging that the dance occurs, and such incidents are likely to stick in the mind. I remember doing it in a shop doorway with a man who took me by my upper arms and moved me gently out of his path. It still rankles. Old people who donÕt want to give way, and who cling to the status they used to have, will walk along the street hugging the wall, and Ônot noticingÕ anyone who approaches them. If, as an experiment, you also hug the wall very funny scenes occur when you stop face to faceÑ but the sideways dance doesnÕt happen because youÕre conscious of what youÕre doing. Old people in, say, Hamburg, often collide with young Britishers in the street, because they expect the young to step aside for them. Similarly, a high-status stripper will walk stark naked into a stagehand who stands in her way. In the Russian Hamlet film thereÕs a moment where Hamlet finds his way momentarily obstructed by a servant and he smashes him down. When you watch a bustling crowd from above itÕs amazing that they donÕt all bump into each other. I think itÕs because weÕre all giving status signals, and exchanging subliminal status challenges all the time. The more submissive person steps aside. 
This means that when two improvisers pass on a bare stage it may be possible to say where they are, even though they may not have decided on a location. The class will agree that the actors look as if theyÕre in a hospital corridor, or in a crowded street, or passing on a narrow pavement. We judge this from the distance at which they make the first eye contact, and from the moment that they Ôswitch offÕ from each other before passing. The class may not know why they imagine the actors in a particular environment, but there is often a general agreement. When actors and directors misjudge social distances, or distort them for Ôdramatic effectÕ the audience will, at some level, know that the work is not truthful. 
One way to teach a student an appreciation of social distance is to get him to hand out leaflets in the street. You canÕt just thrust your hand out at people, you have to establish that youÕre giving out leaflets, and then present one at the right moment. When you get it wrong people either ignore you, or show alarm. Another way is to get the students, working in pairs, to identify strangers in the street as if they knew them. I get one student to do the recognising ÔHi! How are you? HowÕs the family? Same old job thenÕ, and so on, while the other student acts bored, and says ÔCome on, weÕll be lateÕ, and generally expresses impatience. Most people find this approach very convincing, and sometimes extremely interesting scenes take place, but if the students are nervous, they will probably mistime the initial approach. Then it looks as if they are invisible. You can see them greeting people who sweep past as if they didnÕt exist. The great advantage in working in the street is that you canÕt dismiss real peopleÕs reactions as ÔuntruthfulÕ. 
Another way of opening peopleÕs eyes to the way the body positions assert dominance or submission by controlling space is to ask two people who have established a spatial relationship between themselves to freeze, and let the other students study them. Many students still wonÕt understand, but if you take the two ÔstatuesÕ, lift them, together with their chairs, and place them on the opposite sides of each other, the change is dramatic. Their ÔspaceÕ which seemed so ÔnaturalÕ looks weird, and everyone can see how carefully they had adjusted their movements to fit in with each other. 
I ask students (for homework!) to watch groups of people in coffee bars, and to notice how everyoneÕs attitude changes when someone leaves or joins a group. If you watch two people talking, and then wait for one to leave, you can see how the person remaining has to alter his posture. He had arranged his movements to relate to his partnerÕs, and now that heÕs alone he has to change his position in order to express a relationship to the people around him. 

7 Master-Servant 
One status relationship that gives immense pleasure to audiences is the master-servant scene. A dramatist who adapts a story for the stage will often add a servant, especially if itÕs a comedy; Sophocles gave Silenus as a slave to the Cyclops, Molire gave Don Juan a servant, and so on. The master-servant scene seems to be funny and entertaining in all culturesÑeven people who have never seen a manservant have no difficulty in appreciating the nuances. 
The relationship is not necessarily one in which the servant plays low and the master plays high. Literature is full of scenes in which the servant refuses to obey the master, or even beats him and chases him out of the house. The whole point of the master-servant scene is that both partners should keep see-sawing. Dramatists go to ludicrous lengths to devise situations in which the servant actually has to pretend to be the master, and the master to pretend to be the servant! 
If I ask two students to play a master-servant scene they will almost always look like a parent helping a child, or one friend helping another friend, or at best, as if some incompetent person is standing in for the real servant whoÕs off sick. Once theyÕve been trained the servant can throttle the master while remaining visibly the servant. This is very pleasing to the audience, even though they may have no idea of the forces operating. 
I teach that a master-servant scene is one in which both parties act as if all the space belonged to the master. (JohnstoneÕs law!) An extreme example would be the eighteenth-century scientist Henry Cavendish, who is reported to have fired any servant he caught sight of! (Imagine the hysterical situations: servants scuttling like rabbits, hiding in grandfather clocks and ticking, getting stuck in huge vases.) People who are not literally masters and servants may act out the roles, henpecked husbands and dominant wives for example. The contrasts between the status played between the characters and the status played to the space fascinates the audience. 
When the masters are not present, then the servants can take full possession of the space, sprawl on the furniture, drink the brandy, and so on. You may have noticed how ÔshiftyÕ chauffeurs look when their masters are away. They can smoke, chat together and treat the cars as their ÔownÕ, but being in the street they feel ÔexposedÕ. They have to keep a Ôweather eye outÕ. When the master is present, the servant must take care at all times not to dominate the space. One might imagine that since the servants have work to do, everything possible should be done to see that theyÕre kept ÔfreshÕ and at ease, but a servant is not a worker in this sense. You can work for someone without being Ôtheir servantÕ. A servantÕs primary function is to elevate the status of the master. Footmen canÕt lean against the wall, because itÕs the masterÕs wall. Servants must make no unnecessary noise or movement, because itÕs the masterÕs air theyÕre intruding on. 
The preferred position for a servant is usually at the edge of the masterÕs Ôparabola of spaceÕ. This is so that at any moment the master can confront him and dominate him. The exact distance the servant stands from the master depends on his duties, his position in the hierarchy, and the size of the room. 
When the servantÕs duties take him into close proximity with the master he must show that he invades the masterÕs space ÔunwillinglyÕ. If you have to confront the master in order to adjust his tie you stand back as far as possible, and you may incline your head. If youÕre helping with his trousers you probably do it from the side. Crossing in front of the master the servant may ÔshrinkÕ a little, and heÕll try to keep a distance. Working behind the master, brushing his coat, he can be as close as he likes, and visibly higher, but he mustnÕt stay out of sight of the master unless his duties require it (or unless he is very low status). 
The servant has to be quiet, to move neatly, and not to let his arms or legs intrude into the space around him. ServantsÕ costumes are usually rather tight so that their bodies take up a minimum of space. Other things being equal, the servant should be near a door so that he can be instantly dismissed without having to walk round the master. You can see servants edging surreptitiously into this position. 
ItÕs always interesting for the audience when the master tries to coax the servant out of his role. 
ÔAh, Perkins, sit down, will you.Õ 
ÔIn . . . in your chair, Sir?Õ 
ÔCertainly certainly, what will you have?Õ 
ÔEr . . . er . . .Õ 
ÔWhisky? Soda?Õ 
ÔAnything you wish, Sir.Õ 
ÔOh come on, man, you must have some preference. DonÕt sit on the edge of the chair, Perkins, relax, make yourself comfortable. IÕd like your advice, actually.Õ And so on. ItÕs interesting because the audience knows that if the servant does step out of his role, thereÕll be trouble. 
ÔHow dare you take a cigar, Perkins!Õ 
ÔBut Sir, you told me to make myself at home, Sir!Õ If the master and the servant agree to step out of their roles everyone else will be furiousÑas when Queen Victoria made friends with John Brown. 
I get my students to mime dressing and undressing each other as masters and servants. ItÕs very easy to see when the space is wrong, and they suddenly Ôcatch onÕ. I also play scenes with nice masters and horrible servants, and nasty masters with flustered servants. You can improvise quite long plays by putting together a structure of such scenes (this is how the Commedia dellÕ Arte scenarios worked). For example: (1) nice master, nasty servant; (2) nasty master, nice servant; 
(3)
 both teams interrelate and quarrel; (4) Team One prepares for duel; 

(5) 
Team Two prepares for duel; (6) the duel. 


On a good night the Theatre Machine could improvise a half-hour comedy based on this structure. Sometimes the servants have to fight the actual duel, sometimes the duel is fought on piggyback with the servants as horses, and so on. 
ItÕs very easy to invent master-servant games, but there are some that are particularly important for public improvisers. One is Ôkeeping the servant on the hopÕ. In this game the master objects to everything the servant is, or say, or does. The servant accepts the masterÕs statement, and then deflects it. 
ÔSmith! Why are you wearing that ridiculous uniform?Õ 
ÔItÕs your birthday, Sir.Õ This is a correct answer. ÔIÕm not wearing a uniform, SirÕ rejects the masterÕs statement, and is therefore incorrect. ÔYou told me to, SirÕ is also wrong because itÕs implying that the challenge shouldnÕt have been made. 
You can always recognise a correct reply, because the master ÔbogglesÕ for a moment, as his mind readjusts. 
ÔYour coffee, Sir.Õ 
ÔWhereÕs the sugar?Õ 
ÔItÕs in, Sir.Õ A correct answer, since the servant has accepted that the master takes sugar, and that there isnÕt any visible. To say ÔWhat about your diet, Sir?Õ or ÔYou donÕt take sugar, SirÕ would be less correct, and feebler. 
Another game involves the servant getting himself into trouble. 
ÔWhy are you wearing that uniform, Smith?Õ 
ÔI burned the other one, Sir.Õ 
Or: 
ÔWhereÕs the sugar?Õ 
ÔIÕve eaten the last lump, Sir.Õ 
This game also generates its own content. 
ÔGood morning, Jenkins.Õ 
ÔIÕm afraid itÕs not morning, Sir. I forgot to wake you.Õ 
ÔAugh! Four oÕclock in the afternoon. DonÕt you know what day it is?Õ 
ÔYour coronation, Sir.Õ 
There is a lazzi that I use in teaching this game. ItÕs a particular pattern of master-servant dialogue in which the servant is so guilty that he ÔoverconfessesÕ. I got it from Molire. 
ÔAh Perkins! I have a bone to pick with you!Õ 
ÔNot the rhubarb patch, Sir.Õ 
ÔWhat about the rhubarb patch?Õ 
ÔI let the goat in by mistake, Sir.Õ 
ÔYou let my goat eat my rhubarb! You know I have a passion for rhubarb! What will we do with all the custard we ordered?Õ 
ÔIÕm planting some more, Sir.Õ 
ÔSo I should hope. No! ItÕs much worse than mere rhubarb!Õ 
ÔOh, Sir! The dog!Õ 
ÔMy dog!Õ 
ÔYes, Sir. I couldnÕt stand it following me around and sniffing me and messing everywhere, and, and it wetting me when you made me stand to attention at parties, and them all laughing. ThatÕs why I did it, Sir!Õ 
ÔDid what?Õ 
ÔWhy, nothing, Sir.Õ 
ÔDid what? What did you do to poor Towser?Õ 
ÔI . . . I . . .Õ 
ÔGo on!Õ 
ÔPoisoned it, Sir.Õ 
ÔYou poisoned my dog!Õ 
ÔDonÕt hit me, Sir.Õ 
ÔHit you! Hanging would be too good for you. Why itÕs worse than the thing I wanted you for in the first place. YouÕd better make a clean breast of it.Õ 
ÔBut what have I done?Õ 
ÔYouÕve been found out, Perkins.Õ 
ÔOh no, Sir.Õ 
ÔOh yes!Õ 
ÔOh, Sir.Õ 
ÔScoundrel!Õ 
ÔShe shouldnÕt have told you, Sir.Õ 
ÔWhat?Õ 
ÔShe got me in the bathroom, Sir. She swore sheÕd scream and tell you that IÕd attacked her, Sir. She tore her clothes off, Sir.Õ 
ÔWhat! What!Õ The literary value may not be high, but audiences laugh a lot. 
Getting an actor to play both parts in a master-servant scene can accelerate the skills. When the actor is wearing a hat heÕs the master, then he removes it and leaps into the position in which heÕs been imagining the servant, and plays the servant role. The moment he canÕt think what to say he changes roles. He can throttle himself, and beat himself up, or praise himself, and he ÔblocksÕ the action far less. ItÕs actually easier to play master-servant scenes as solos. The mind has an ability to split itself readily into several peopleÑFrederick Perls got people to play Ôtop-dogÕ and ÔunderdogÕ in a similar way. 
An excellent way to play master-servant scenes is to let one actor do both voices, the other mouthing the words that are supposed to be his. This sounds very difficult, but itÕs actually easier to sustain long scenes in this way. At first the actor whoÕs mouthing the words will play a passive role. ItÕs necessary to prod him into developing the action. If he picks up a chair and threatens the master with it, then the master will have to say something appropriate, like ÔWhereÕs the money you owe me, Sir?Õ Perhaps the master will beat the servant up and do all the screams and pleas for mercy himself. 
If you experiment with master-servant scenes you eventually realise that the servant could have a servant, and the master could have a master, and that actors could be instantly assembled into pecking orders by just numbering them. You can then improvise very complicated group scenes on the spur of the moment. 
I introduce pecking orders as clown games, oversimplifying the procedures, and creating complex absurdities which ÔcartoonÕ real life. Orders and blame are passed one way along the hierarchy, excuses and problems are passed the other way. So far as possible each person is to interact with the one next to him in rank. Audiences never seem to tire of dialogue like this: 
1: Chair! 
2: Chair! 
3: Get a chair! 
4: Yes, Sir. 
1: WhatÕs happening? 
2: IÕll just check, Sir. WhereÕs the chair? 
3: Number FourÕs getting it, Sir. 
4: Beg pardon, but I canÕt find one, Sir. 
3: He canÕt find one! 
2: ÔSir!Õ How dare you address me without calling me ÔSirÕ? 
3: Yes, Sir! Number Four reports that there is no chair, Sir! 
1: WhatÕs going on here, Number Two? 
2: ThereÕs no chair, Sir. 
1: No chair! This is monstrous! Have someone crouch so that I can sit on them! 
2: Number Three, have Number Four crouch so that Number One can sit on him. 
4: Permission to speak, Sir! (And so on.) 
The patterns become even clearer if you give each actor a long balloon with which to hit people. If Number One hits Number Two, Number Two apologies to him, and hits Number Three, and so on. Number Four, who canÕt hit anyone, ducks, or cries, or bites his lip, or dies, or whatever. Each person can also try to make faces at anyone ÔaboveÕ him, without getting caught (if possible). If Number One sees Number Three make a face at Number Two, he informs Number Two, and so on. This may look very tedious on the page, but these simple rules produce amazing permutations. 
One of the craziest Ôclown gamesÕ is a version of Ôtaking the hatÕ. IÕve seen spectators collapsing with laughter. I start the game by taking four students and numbering them one to four. Each wears a soft trilby hat. First, Number One takes Number TwoÕs hat and throws it at his feet. Number Two reacts with horror and embarrassment and shrieks for Number Three to pick the hat up and replace it. Number Two then takes Number ThreeÕs hat Ñand so on, except that Number Four will have to put his own hat on. 
I then tell Number One that although he prefers to take Number TwoÕs hat, he can in fact take anybodyÕs. Number Two similarly prefers to take Number ThreeÕs, but he can also take Number FourÕs. 
Once this pattern is almost learnt, I let people weave about and try not to get their hats taken. And I insist that the hats must be thrown at the feet. People have a strong impulse to throw or kick the hats right away, which breaks up the group and spoils the crazy patterns. If you can keep the actors ÔhighÕ on the game they will now be using their bodies like excellent physical comedians, they will have a marvellous ÔrapportÕ with each other, and absolutely no trace of self-consciousness. I make them play a scene while continuing this insane activity. I sent them outside and get them to enter as if burgling a house in which people are asleep upstairs. Or I get them to pack for the holidays, or interact with another pecking order who are also Ôtaking hatsÕ. Number one will probably have to throw insane fits of rage to get anything done, but itÕs more important that the scene is played than the hat game ÔdemonstratedÕ. You canÕt even teach this game unless you yourself are ÔhighÕ and expressing great drive and energy. 
Actors should become expert at each stage of a pecking order. There will be actors who can at first only play one role really well. Videotape is useful in explaining to them where their behaviour is inappropriate. 
Number One in a pecking order has to make sure that everything is functioning properly. Anything that irritates him must be suppressed. At all times everything must be organised for his personal contentment. He can also add his own rules, insisting that absolute silence should be maintained at all times, or that the word ÔisÕ should be abolished from the language, or whatever. Desmond Morris, in The Human Zoo (Cape, 1969; Corgi, 1971) gives Ôten golden rulesÕ for people who are Number Ones. He says, ÔThey apply to all leaders, from baboons to modern presidents and prime ministers.Õ They are: 
1. 
You must clearly display the trappings, postures and gestures of dominance. 

2. 
In moments of active rivalry you must threaten you subordinates aggressively. 

3. 
In moments of physical challenge you (or your delegates) must be able forcibly to overpower your subordinates. 

4. 
If a challenge involves brain rather than brawn you must be able to outwit your subordinates. 

5. 
You must suppress squabbles that break out between your subordinates. 

6. 
You must reward your immediate subordinates by permitting them to enjoy the benefits of their high ranks. 

7. 
You must protect the weaker members of the group from undue persecution. 

8. 
You must make decisions concerning the social activities of your group. 


9. You must reassure your extreme subordinates from time to time. 
10. You must take the initiative in repelling threats or attacks arising from outside your group. 
Number Four has to keep Number Three happy while avoiding the attention of One or Two. If addressed by One or Two he must avoid any appearance of wanting to usurp ThreeÕs position. If the general speaks to a private we should expect the private to keep glancing at the sergeant. If the general lowers the sergeant the private may be secretly delighted but heÕll have to hide it, and at the time he might be expected to find it embarrassing. 
Number Four has to be an expert at making excuses, and in evading responsibility. He must also be inventing problems to pass up the pecking order. 
Basically, One imposes aims and tries to get them fulfilled, while Four discovers that the house is on fire, or the enemy approaching, or that thereÕs only three minutesÕ oxygen left, and so on. Two and Three are mostly concerned with maintaining their respective positions, and with the communication of information up and down the line. 
More naturalistic pecking-order work can be introduced as Ôstatus towersÕ. Someone begins with some low-status activity, and each person who enters the scene plays a step higher. Or you can start at the top and add each person one step down. 
It is the lack of pecking-order that makes most crowd scenes look unconvincing. The ÔextrasÕ mill about trying to look ÔrealÕ, and the spaces between them are quite phoney. In films where Mafia bandits wait on a hillside while their leader confers with someone, you can see that the director has spaced them out ÔartisticallyÕ, or has just said Ôspread yourself outÕ. By just numbering people in hierarchies so that they knew what status they were, such errors could be avoided. 

8 Maximum Status Gaps 
In life, status gaps are often exaggerated to such an extent that they become comical. Heinrich Harrer met a Tibetan whose servant stood holding a spitoon in case the master wanted to spit. Queen Victoria would take her position and sit, and there had to be a chair. George the Sixth used to wear electrically heated underclothes when deerstalking, which meant a gillie had to follow him around holding the battery. 
I train actors to use minimum status gaps, because then they have to assess the status of their partners accurately, but I also teach them to play maximum status-gap scenes. For example, I ask the actors to play a scene in which a master is as high in status as possible, and the servant as low as possible. At first theyÕll play ineptly. The master looks uncomfortable, and the servant intrudes on the masterÕs space. 
I start the scene again and say that the moment the master feels the slightest irritation heÕs to snap his fingersÑthe servant will then commit suicide. IÕll have to prod the master into action because heÕll be reluctant to exercise his power. The moment the master looks irritated I say ÔKill him!Õ and send in more servants until the stage is littered with bodies. Everyone laughs a lot, but often the servants have no idea why theyÕre being killed. I ask the master to explain the reasons, but I stress that he doesnÕt need to be fair. The servants usually think the master is being harsh, but the audience are amazed that servants survive so long, since everything they do is inept. Servants are killed because they wave their arms about, because they clump about, because theyÕre disrespectful, or because they misunderstand the masterÕs requirements. 
Now I give the servants three lives, so they die at the third snap of the fingers. Amazingly youÕll see them doing exactly the same thing after a finger snap as before it. ÔDo something different,Õ I shout, ÔheÕs about to kill you again.Õ The servants seem amazingly unadaptableÑthis is because theyÕre demonstrating their role as servants rather than attending to the needs of the master. At first they survive for just a few seconds, but soon theyÕre surviving for minutes, and the masters begin to feel amazingly pampered as theyÕre thrust up in status by their servants. 
Once a maximum-gap master-servant scene is established, I send in a third person who has to placate the master, and cope with servants as well. 
In one form of this game you reverse the expected status. If an executioner is trying to play as low as possible, then heÕll be too nervous to roll the last cigarette, heÕll apologise for the untidiness, heÕll ask for an autograph, or heÕll accidentally shoot himself in the foot. The suicide on the ledge who plays high status may argue the rescuer into jumping off. ItÕs very easy to create scenes this way. 
ÔExcuse me, Miss . . .Õ 
ÔNext cashier please. IÕm just going off duty.Õ 
ÔEr . . . no, no . . . IÕm not a customer.Õ 
ÔIf youÕll just join the queue over there, Sir . . .Õ 
ÔIÕve got a note. Here.Õ 
ÔFour shirts, two pants, six socks?Õ 
ÔNo, no . . . er . . . here, this one.Õ 
ÔHand over the money? This is a stick-up!Õ 
ÔNot so loud.Õ 
ÔWell, how much did you want?Õ 
ÔAll of it!Õ 
ÔDonÕt be absurd!Õ 
ÔYeah, well, just a few quid then, to tide us over.Õ 
ÔI shall have to refer this to Mr Carbuncle.Õ 
Ô50p, then!Õ 
Maximum-status-gap exercises produce ÔabsurdÕ improvisations. (I donÕt like the term Ôtheatre of the absurdÕ, because the best ÔabsurdÕ plays present ÔequivalentsÕ for reality, and arenÕt nonsensical, and many conventional writers have written ÔexistentialÕ plays. ÔAbsurdÕ plays are based on maximum-status-gap transactions.) 

9 Text 
Although this short essay is no more than an introduction, by now it will be clear to you that status transactions arenÕt only of interest to the improviser. Once you understand that every sound and posture implies a status, then you perceive the world quite differently, and the change is probably permanent. In my view, really accomplished actors, directors, and playwrights are people with an intuitive understanding of the status transactions that govern human relationships. This ability to perceive the underlying motives of casual behaviour can also be taught. 
In conclusion, but as a coda, rather than a summing-up, IÕd suggest that a good play is one which ingeniously displays and reverses the status between the characters. Many writers of great talent have failed to write successful plays (Blake, Keats, Tennyson, among others) because of a failure to understand that drama is not primarily a literary art. Shakespeare is a great writer even in translation; a great production is great even if you donÕt speak the language. A great play is a virtuoso display of status transactions ÑWaiting for Godot, for example. The ÔtrampsÕ play friendship status, but thereÕs a continual friction because Vladimir believes himself higher than Estragon, a thesis which Estragon will not accept. Pozzo and Lucky play maximum-gap master-servant scenes. The ÔtrampsÕ play low status to Lucky, and Pozzo often plays low status to the trampsÑwhich produces thrilling effects. HereÕs a section where the ÔtrampsÕ are asking why Lucky holds the bags instead of resting them on the ground. 
POZZO: . . . LetÕs try and get this clear. Has he got the right to? Certainly he has. It follows that he doesnÕt want to. ThereÕs reasoning for you. And why doesnÕt he want to? (Pause.) Gentlemen, the reason is this. 
VLADIMIR: (To Estragon.) Make a note of this. POZZO : He wants to impress me so that IÕll keep him. ESTRAGON : What? POZZO : . . . In reality, he carries like a pig. ItÕs not his job. VLADIMIR: You want to get rid of him? 
POZZO: He imagines that when I see him indefatigable IÕll regret my decision. Such is his miserable scheme. As though I were short of slaves! (All three look at Lucky.) Atlas, son of Jupiter! 
If you observe the status, then the play is fascinating. If you ignore it the play is tedious. Pozzo is not really a very high-status master, since he fights for status all the time. He owns the land, but he doesnÕt own the space. 
POZZO: . . . I must be getting on. Thank you for your society. (He reflects.) Unless I smoke another pipe before I go. What do you say? (They say nothing.) Oh, IÕm only a small smoker, a very small smoker, IÕm not in the habit of smoking two pipes one on top of the other, it makes (Hand to heart, sighing) my heart go pit-a-pat. (Silence.) But perhaps you donÕt smoke? Yes? No? ItÕs of no importance. (Silence.) But how am I to sit down now, without affectation, now that I have risen? Without appearing toÑhow shall I sayÑwithout appearing to falter. (To Vladimir.) I beg your pardon? (Silence.) Perhaps you didnÕt speak? (Silence.) ItÕs of no importance. Let me see . . . (He reflects.) 
ESTRAGON: Ah! ThatÕs better. (He puts the bones in his pocket.) VLADIMIR: LetÕs go. ESTRAGON: So soon? 
POZZO: One moment! (He jerks the rope.) Stool! (He points with his whip. Lucky moves the stool.) More! There! (He sits down. Lucky goes back to his place.) Done it! (He fills 
his pipe.) 
It must be clear, I think, that even the stage directions relate to status. Every ÔsilenceÕ is lowering to Pozzo. I remember a reviewer (Kenneth Tynan) making fun of BeckettÕs pauses, but this just shows a lack of understanding. Obviously BeckettÕs plays need careful pacing, but the pauses are part of the pattern of dominance and submission. Godot earns its reputation as a boring play only when directors try to make it ÔsignificantÕ, and ignore the status transactions. 
I donÕt myself see that an educated man in this culture necessarily has to understand the second law of thermodynamics, but he certainly should understand that we are pecking-order animals and that this affects the tiniest details of our behaviour. 
Note 
1. The high-status effect of slow motion means that TV heroes who have the power of superhuman speed are shown slowed down! Logic would suggest that you should speed the film up, but then theyÕd be jerking about like the Keystone Cops, or the bionic chicken. 


Spontaneity 

1 
ÔI was given the part of poor Armgard, so I stood in front of the class and as I began with ÒHere he cannot escape me, he must hear meÓ, I suddenly noticed a warm friendly feeling in the region of the stomach, like a soft hotwater bottle in a cold bed, and when I got to ÒMercy, Lord Governor! Oh, pardon, pardonÓ, I was already on my knees, tears streaming from my eyes and nose, and sobbing to such an extent that I could only finish the passage ÒMy wretched orphans cry for breadÓ with supreme difficulty. The fishhead was in favour of a more restrained performance and her cutting voice drove me to the back of the class room with words of ÒUn-German hysterical conductÓ. It was a nightmare. I almost died of shame and prayed for an earthquake or an air raid to deliver me from the derision and shock . . . apart from the nagging voice all went still, the others stared at me as though they had unwittingly harboured a serpent in their midst. The rest of my days with Weise were torture. I was afraid of the others and myself for I could never be certain that I wouldnÕt again throw myself down in tears because of the orphans. . .Õ (Hildegarde Knef, The Gift Horse, Andr Deutsch, 1971.) ItÕs possible to turn unimaginative people into imaginative people at a momentÕs notice. I remember an experiment referred to in the British Journal of PsychologyÑprobably in the summer of 1969 or 1970Ñ in which some businessmen who had showed up as very dull on work-association tests were asked to imagine themselves as happy-go-lucky hippy types, in which persona they were retested, and showed up as far more imaginative. In creativity tests you may be asked to suggest different ways of using a brick; if you say things like ÔBuild a houseÕ, or ÔBuild a wallÕ, then youÕre classified as unimaginativeÑif you say ÔGrind it up and use it for diarrhoea mixtureÕ, or ÔRub off warts with itÕ, then youÕre imaginative. IÕm oversimplifying, but you get the general idea. 
Some tests involve picture completion. You get given a lot of little squares with signs in them, and you have to add something to the sign. ÔUncreativeÕ people just add another squiggle, or join up a ÔCÕ shape to make a circle.
