ÔCreativeÕ people have a great time, parallel lines become the trunk of a tree, a ÔVÕ on its side becomes the beam of a lighthouse, and so on. It may be a mistake to think of such tests as showing people to be creative, or uncreative. It may be that the tests are recording different activities. The person who adds a timid squiggle may be trying to reveal as little as possible about himself. If we can persuade him to have fun, and not worry about being judged, then maybe he can approach the test with the same attitude as a ÔcreativeÕ person, just like the tired businessmen when they were pretending to be hippies. 
Most schools encourage children to be unimaginative. The research so far shows that imaginative children are disliked by their teachers. Torrance gives an eye-witness account of an Ôexceptionally creative boyÕ who questioned one of the rules in the textbook: ÔThe teacher became irate, even in the presence of the principal. She fumed, ÒSo! You think you know more than this book!ÓÕ She was also upset when the boy finished the problems she set almost as quickly as it took to read them. ÔShe couldnÕt understand how he was getting the correct answer and demanded that he write down all of the steps he had gone through in solving each problem.Õ 
When this boy transferred to another school, his new principal telephoned to ask if he was the sort of boy Ôwho has to be squelched rather roughlyÕ. When it was explained that he was Ôa very wholesome, promising lad who needed understanding and encouragementÕ the new principal exclaimed Ôrather brusquely, ÒWell, heÕs already said too much right here in my office!ÓÕ (E. P. Torrance, Guiding Creative Talent, Prentice-Hall, 1962.) One of my students spent two years in a classroom where the teacher had put a large sign over the blackboard. It said ÔGet into the ÒYes, SirÓ attitude.Õ No doubt we can all add further anecdotes. Torrance has a theory that Ômany children with impoverished imaginations have been subjected to rather vigorous and stern efforts to eliminate fantasy too early. They are afraid to think.Õ Torrance seems to understand the forces at work, but he still refers to attempts to eliminate fantasy too early. Why should we eliminate fantasy at all? Once we eliminate fantasy, then we have no artists. 
Intelligence is proportional to population, but talent appears not to be related to population numbers. IÕm living in a city at the edge of the Rocky Mountains; the population is much greater than it was in Shakespearian London, and almost everyone here is literate, and has had many thousands of dollars spent on his education. Where are the poets, and playwrights, and painters, and composers? Remember that there are hundreds of thousands of ÔliterateÕ people here, while in ShakespeareÕs London very few people could read. The great art of this part of the world was the art of the native people. The whites flounder about trying to be ÔoriginalÕ and failing miserably. 
You can get a glimmer of the damage done when you watch people trying out pens in stationersÕ shops. They make feeble little scribbles for fear of giving something away. If an Aborigine asked us for a sample of Nordic art weÕd have to direct him to an art gallery. No Aborigine ever told an anthropologist, ÔSorry, Baas, I canÕt draw.Õ Two of my students said they couldnÕt draw, and I asked, ÔWhy?Õ One said her teacher had been sarcastic because sheÕd painted a blue snowman (every childÕs painting was pinned up on the walls except hers.) The other girl had drawn trees up the sides of her paintings (like Paul Klee), and the teacher drew a ÔcorrectÕ tree on top of hers. She remembered thinking ÔIÕll never draw for you again!Õ (One reason given for filling in the windows of the local schools here is that itÕll help make the children more attentive!) Most children can operate in a creative way until theyÕre eleven or twelve, when suddenly they lose their spontaneity and produce imitations of Ôadult artÕ. When other races come into contact with our culture something similar happens. The great Nigerian sculptor Bamboya was set up as principal of an art school by some philanthropic Americans in the 1920s. Not only did he fail to hand on his talents, but his own inspiration failed him. He and his students could still carve coffee tables for the whites, but they werenÕt inspired any more. 
So-called Ôprimitive paintersÕ in our own culture sometimes go to art school to improve themselvesÑand lose their talent. A critic told me of a film school where each new student made a short film unaided. These, he said, were always interesting, although technically crude. At the end of the course they made a longer, technically more proficient film, which hardly anyone wanted to see. He seemed outraged when I suggested they should close the school (he lectured there); yet until recently our directors didnÕt get any training. Someone asked Kubrick if it was usual for a director to spend so much care on lighting each shot and he said, ÔI donÕt know. IÕve never seen anyone else light a film.Õ 
You have to be a very stubborn person to remain an artist in this culture. ItÕs easy to play the role of ÔartistÕ, but actually to create something means going against oneÕs education. I read an interview once in which Grandma Moses was complaining that people kept urging her to improve her snow scenes by putting blue in them, but she insisted that the snow she saw was white, so she wouldnÕt do it. This little old lady could paint because she defied the ÔexpertsÕ. Even after his works had been exhibited in court as proof that he wasnÕt in his right mind, Henri Rousseau still had the stubbornness to go on painting! 
We see the artist as a wild and aberrant figure. Maybe our artists are the people who have been constitutionally unable to conform to the demands of the teachers. Pavlov found that there were some dogs that he couldnÕt ÔbrainwashÕ until heÕd castrated them, and starved them for three weeks. If teachers could do that to us, then maybe theyÕd achieve PlatoÕs dream of a republic in which there are no artists left at all. 
Many teachers think of children as immature adults. It might lead to better and more ÔrespectfulÕ teaching, if we thought of adults as atrophied children. Many Ôwell adjustedÕ adults are bitter, uncreative frightened, unimaginative, and rather hostile people. Instead of assuming they were born that way, or that thatÕs what being an adult entails, we might consider them as people damaged by their education and upbringing. 
2 
Many teachers express surprise at the switch-off that occurs at puberty, but I donÕt, because first of all the child has to hide the sexual turmoil heÕs in, and secondly the grown-upsÕ attitude to him completely changes. 
Suppose an eight-year-old writes a story about being chased down a mouse-hole by a monstrous spider. ItÕll be perceived as ÔchildishÕ and no one will worry. If he writes the same story when heÕs fourteen it may be taken as a sign of mental abnormality. Creating a story, or painting a picture, or making up a poem lay an adolescent wide open to criticism. He therefore has to fake everything so that he appears ÔsensitiveÕ or ÔwittyÕ or ÔtoughÕ or ÔintelligentÕ according to the image heÕs trying to establish in the eyes of other people. If he believed he was a transmitter, rather than a creator, then weÕd be able to see what his talents really were. 
We have an idea that art is self-expressionÑwhich historically is weird. An artist used to be seen as a medium through which something else operated. He was a servant of the God. Maybe a mask-maker would have fasted and prayed for a week before he had a vision of the Mask he was to carve, because no one wanted to see his Mask, they wanted to see the GodÕs. When Eskimos believed that each piece of bone only had one shape inside it, then the artist didnÕt have to Ôthink upÕ an idea. He had to wait until he knew what was in thereÑand this is crucial. When heÕd finished carving his friends couldnÕt say ÔIÕm a bit worried about that Nanook at the third iglooÕ, but only, ÔHe made a mess getting that out!Õ or ÔThere are some very odd bits of bone about these days.Õ These days of course the Eskimos get booklets giving illustrations of what will sell, but before we infected them, they were in contact with a source of inspiration that we are not. ItÕs no wonder that our artists are aberrant characters. ItÕs not surprising that great African sculptors end up carving coffee tables, or that the talent of our children dies the moment we expect them to become adult. Once we believe that art is self-expression, then the individual can be criticised not only for his skill or lack of skill, but simply for being what he is. 
Schiller wrote of a Ôwatcher at the gates of the mindÕ, who examines ideas too closely. He said that in the case of the creative mind Ôthe intellect has withdrawn its watcher from the gates, and the ideas rush in pell-mell, and only then does it review and inspect the multitude.Õ He said that uncreative people Ôare ashamed of the momentary passing madness which is found in all real creators . . . regarded in isolation, an idea may be quite insignificant, and venturesome in the extreme, but it may acquire importance from an idea that follows it; perhaps in collation with other ideas which seem equally absurd, it may be capable of furnishing a very serviceable link.Õ 
My teachers had the opposite theory. They wanted me to reject and discriminate, believing that the best artist was the one who made the most elegant choices. They analysed poems to show how difficult ÔrealÕ writing was, and they taught that I should always know where the writing was taking me, and that I should search for better and better ideas. They spoke as if an image like Ôthe multitudinous seas incarnadineÕ could have been worked out like the clue to a crossword puzzle. Their idea of the ÔcorrectÕ choice was the one anyone would have made if he had thought long enough. 
I now feel that imagining should be as effortless as perceiving. In order to recognise someone my brain has to perform amazing feats of analysis: ÔShape . . . dark . . . swelling . . . getting closer . . . human . . . nose type X15, eyes type E24B . . . characteristic way of walking . . . look under relative . . .Õ and so on, in order to turn electromagnetic radiation into the image of my father, yet I donÕt experience myself as ÔdoingÕ anything at all! My brain creates a whole universe without my having the least sense of effort. Of course, if I say ÔHi DadÕ, and the approaching figure ignores me, then IÕd do something that I perceive as ÔthinkingÕ. ÔThatÕs not the coat he usually wears,Õ I think. ÔThis man is shorter.Õ ItÕs only when I believe my perceptions to be in error that I have to ÔdoÕ anything. ItÕs the same with imagination. Imagination is as effortless as perception, unless we think it might be ÔwrongÕ, which is what our education encourages us to believe. Then we experience ourselves as ÔimaginingÕ, as Ôthinking up an ideaÕ, but what weÕre really doing is faking up the sort of imagination we think we ought to have. 
When I read a novel I have no sense of effort. Yet if I pay close attention to my mental processes I find an amazing amount of activity. ÔShe walked into the room . . .Õ I read, and I have a picture in my mind, very detailed, of a large Victorian room empty of furniture, with the bare boards painted white around what used to be the edge of the carpet. I also see some windows with the shutters open and sunlight streaming through them. ÔShe noticed some charred papers in the grate . . .Õ I read, and my mind inserts a fireplace which IÕve seen in a friendÕs house, very ornate. ÔA board creaked behind her . . .Õ I read, and for a split second I see a FrankensteinÕs monster holding a wet teddy bear. ÔShe turned to see a little wizened old man . . .Õ, instantly, the monster shrivels to Picasso with a beret, and the room darkens and fills with furniture. My imagination is working as hard as the writerÕs, but I have no sense of doing anything, or Ôbeing creativeÕ. 
A friend has just read the last paragraph and found it impossible to imagine that sheÕs being creative when she reads. I tell her IÕll invent a story especially for her. ÔImagine a man walking along the street,Õ I say. ÔSuddenly he hears a sound and turns to see something moving in a doorway . . .Õ I stop and ask her what the man is wearing. 
ÔA suit.Õ 
ÔWhat sort of suit?Õ 
ÔStriped.Õ 
ÔAny other people in the street?Õ ÔA white dog.Õ ÔWhat was the street like?Õ ÔIt was a London street. Working-class. Some of the buildings have been 
demolished.Õ ÔAny windows boarded up?Õ ÔYes. Rusty corrugated iron.Õ ÔSo theyÕve been boarded up a long time?Õ SheÕs obviously created much more than I have. She doesnÕt pause to 
think up the answers to my questions, she ÔknowsÕ them. They flashed automatically into her consciousness. 
People may seem uncreative, but theyÕll be extremely ingenious at rationalising the things they do. You can see this in people who obey post.hypnotic suggestions, while managing to explain the behaviour ordered by the hypnotist as being of their own volition. 
People maintain prejudices quite effortlessly. For example, in this conversation (R. B. Zajonc, Public Opinion Quarterly, Princeton, 1960, Vol. 24, 2, pp. 280-96): 
MR X: The trouble with Jews is that they only take care of their own group. MR Y: But the record of the community chest shows that they give more generously than non-Jews. 
MR X: That shows that they are always trying to buy favour and intrude in Christian affairs. They think of nothing but money; thatÕs why there are so many Jewish bankers. 
MR Y: But a recent study shows that the per cent of Jews in banking is proportionally much smaller than the per cent of non-Jews. 
MR X: ThatÕs it. They donÕt go for respectable businesses. They would rather run nightclubs. In a way this bigot is being very creative. 
I knew a man who was discovered stark naked in a wardrobe by an irate husband. The wife screamed, ÔIÕve never seen this man before in my life.Õ ÔI must be in the wrong flat,Õ said my friend. These reactions arenÕt very satisfactory, but they didnÕt have to be Ôthought upÕ, they sprang to mind quite automatically. 
I sometimes shock students who have been trained by strict ÔmethodÕ teachers. 
ÔBe sad,Õ I say. 
ÔWhat do you mean, be sad?Õ 
ÔJust be sad. See what happens.Õ 
ÔBut whatÕs my motivation?Õ 
ÔJust be sad. Start to weep and youÕll know whatÕs upset you.Õ 
The student decides to humour me. 
ÔThat isnÕt very sad. YouÕre just pretending.Õ 
ÔYou asked me to pretend.Õ 
ÔRaise your arm. Now, why are you raising it?Õ 
ÔYou asked me to.Õ 
ÔYes, but why might you have raised it?Õ 
ÔTo hold on to a strap in the Tube.Õ 
ÔThen thatÕs why you raised your arm.Õ 
ÔBut I could have given any reason.Õ 
ÔOf course; you could have been waving to someone, or milking a giraffe, or airing your armpit . . .Õ 
ÔBut I donÕt have time to choose the best reason.Õ 
ÔDonÕt choose anything. Trust your mind. Take the first idea it gives you. Now try being sad again. Hold the face in a sad position, fight back the tears. Be unhappier. More. More. Now tell me why youÕre in this state?Õ 
ÔMy child has died.Õ 
ÔDid you think that up?Õ 
ÔI just knew.Õ 
ÔThere you are, then.Õ 
ÔMy teacher said you shouldnÕt act adjectives.Õ 
ÔYou shouldnÕt act adjectives without justifying them.Õ 
If an improviser is stuck for an idea, he shouldnÕt search for one, he should trigger his partnerÕs ability to give ÔunthoughtÕ answers. 
If someone starts a scene by saying ÔWhat are you doing here?Õ then his partner can instantly say, without thinking, ÔI just came down to get the milk, Sir.Õ 
ÔDidnÕt I tell you what IÕd do if I caught you again?Õ 
ÔOh Sir, donÕt put me in the refrigerator, Sir.Õ 
If you donÕt know what to do in a scene, just say something like, ÔOh my God! WhatÕs that?Õ 
This immediately jerks images into your partnerÕs mind: ÔMother!Õ he says, or ÔThat dogÕs messed the floor againÕ, or ÔA secret staircase!Õ or whatever. 
3 
At school any spontaneous act was likely to get me into trouble. I learned never to act on impulse, and that whatever came into my mind first should be rejected in favour of better ideas. I learned that my imagination wasnÕt ÔgoodÕ enough. I learned that the first idea was unsatisfactory because it was (1) psychotic; (2) obscene; (3) unoriginal. 
The truth is that the best ideas are often psychotic, obscene and unoriginal. My best known playÑa one-actor called Moby DickÑis about a servant who keeps his masterÕs one remaining sperm in a goldfish bowl. It escapes, grows to monstrous size, and has to be hunted down on the high seas. This is certainly a rather obscene idea to many people, and if I hadnÕt thrown away everything that my teachers taught me, I could never have written it. These teachers, who were so sure of the rules, didnÕt produce anything themselves at all. I was one of a number of playwrights who emerged in the late 1950s, and it was remarkable that only one of us had been to a universityÑthat was John ArdenÑand heÕd studied architecture. 
LetÕs take a look at these three categories. 
Psychotic Thought 
My feeling is that sanity is actually a pretence, a way we learn to behave. We keep this pretence up because we donÕt want to be rejected by other peopleÑand being classified insane is to be shut out of the group in a very complete way. 
Most people I meet are secretly convinced that theyÕre a little crazier than the average person. People understand the energy necessary to maintain their own shields, but not the energy expended by other people. They understand that their own sanity is a performance, but when confronted by other people they confuse the person with the role. 
Sanity has nothing directly to do with the way you think. ItÕs a matter of presenting yourself as safe. Little old men wander around London hallucinating visibly, but no one gets upset. The same behaviour in a younger, more vigorous person would get him shut away. A Canadian study on attitudes to mental illness concluded that it was when someoneÕs behaviour was perceived as ÔunpredictableÕ that the community rejected them. A fat lady was admiring a painting at a private view at the Tate when the artist strode over and bit her. They threw him out, but no one questioned his sanityÑit was how he always behaved. 
I once read about a man who believed himself to have a fish in his jaw. (The case was reported in New Society.) This fish moved about, and caused him a lot of discomfort. When he tried to tell people about the fish, they thought him ÔcrazyÕ, which led to violent arguments. After heÕd been hospitalised several timesÑwith no effect on the fishÑit was suggested that perhaps he shouldnÕt tell anyone. After all it was the quarrels that were getting him put away, rather than the delusion. Once heÕd agreed to keep his problem secret, he was able to lead a normal life. His sanity is like our sanity. We may not have a fish in our jaw, but we all have its equivalent. 
When I explain that sanity is a matter of interaction, rather than of oneÕs mental processes, students are often hysterical with laughter. They agree that for years they have been suppressing all sorts of thinking because they classified it as insane. 
Students need a ÔguruÕ who Ôgives permissionÕ to allow forbidden thoughts into their consciousness. A ÔguruÕ doesnÕt necessarily teach at all. Some remain speechless for years, others communicate very cryptically. All reassure by example. They are people who have been into the forbidden areas and who have survived unscathed. I react playfully with my students, while showing them that there are just as many dead nuns or chocolate scorpions inside my head as there are in anybodyÕs, yet I interact very smoothly and sanely. ItÕs no good telling the student that he isnÕt to be held responsible for the content of his imagination, he needs a teacher who is living proof that the monsters are not real, and that the imagination will not destroy you. Otherwise the student will have to go on pretending to be dull. 
At one time I went from a class of mental patients in the morning to a class of drama students in the afternoon. The work of the drama students was far more bizarre, because they werenÕt so scared of what their minds might do. The mental patients mistook even the normal working of the imagination as proof of their insanity. 
I remember the psychologist David Stafford-Clark criticising Ken Campbell at a public meeting. Ken had said that he encouraged his actors to act like lunatics, because then people would find them amusing. Stafford-Clark was upset at the idea that mad people should be thought ÔfunnyÕ, but thatÕs hardly KenÕs fault. Laughter is a whip that keeps us in line. ItÕs horrible to be laughed at against your will. Either you suppress unwelcome laughter or you start controlling it. We suppress our spontaneous impulses, we censor our imaginations, we learn to present ourselves as ÔordinaryÕ, and we destroy our talentÑ then no one laughs at us. If Shakespeare had been worried about establishing his sanity, he could never have written Hamlet, let alone Titus Andronicus; Harpo couldnÕt have inflated a rubber glove and 
milked it into the coffee cups;1 
Groucho would never have threatened to horse-whip someoneÑif he had a horse; W. C. Fields would never have leapt out of the aeroplane after his whisky bottle; Stan Laurel would never have snapped his fingers and ignited his thumb. 
We all know instinctively what ÔmadÕ thought is: mad thoughts are those which other people find unacceptable, and train us not to talk about, but which we go to the theatre to see expressed. 

Obscenity 
I find many things obscene, in the sense of repulsive or shocking. I find the use of film from real massacres in the titles of TV shows pretty nasty. I find the way people take pills and smoke cigarettes, and generally screw themselves up, rather awful. The way parents and teachers often treat children nauseates me. Most people think of obscene things as sexual like pubic hair, obscene language, but IÕm more shocked by modern cities, by the carcinogens in the air and in the food, by the ever-increasing volume of radioactive materials in the environment. In the first seven months of 1975 the cancer rate in America seems to have jumped by 5.2 per cent, but few noticedÑthe information didnÕt have Ônews valueÕ. 
Most peopleÕs idea of what is or isnÕt obscene varies. In some cultures certain times are set aside when the normal values are reversedÑthe ÔLord of MisruleÕ, Zuni clowning, many carnivalsÑ and something similar happens even in this culture, or so IÕm told, at office parties for example. PeopleÕs tolerance of obscenity varies according to the group theyÕre with, or the particular circumstances (Ôpas devant les enfantsÕ). People can laugh at jokes told at a party that they wouldnÕt find funny on a more formal occasion. It seems unfortunate to me that the classroom is often considered a ÔformalÕ area in this sense. 
The first school I taught at had one woman teacher. When she went out shopping at lunchtime, the men pulled their chairs round and told dirty stories non-stop. Down in the playground, as usual, the children were swopping similar stories, or writing ÔshitÕ or ÔfuckÕ on the walls, always correctly spelt; yet the staff considered the children Ôdirty little devilsÕ, and punished them for saying things which were far milder than things the teachers themselves would say, and enjoy laughing at. When these children grow up, and perhaps crack up, then theyÕll find themselves in therapy groups where theyÕll be encouraged to say all the things that the teacher 
would have forbidden during school.2 

Foulkes and Anthony (in Group Psychotherapy, Penguin, 1972) say that a therapeutic situation is one Ôin which the patient can freely voice his innermost thoughts towards himself, towards any other person, and towards the analyst. He can be confident that he is not being judged, and that he is fully accepted, whatever he may be, or whatever he may disclose.Õ Later they add: ÔWe encourage the relaxation of censorship. We do this by letting the patient members understand that they are not only permitted, but expected to say anything that comes to mind. We tell them not to allow any of their usual inhibitory considerations to stand in the way of voicing the ideas that come to them spontaneously.Õ 
I was at school more than twenty years ago, but in education the more things change the more they are the same. (Recent research suggests that the old ÔmonitorÕ system may be one of the most efficient teaching methods!) Here are some answers that headmasters gave to a questionnaire about sex education in their schools. (Reported in the New Statesman, 28 February 1969.) ÔIÕm against all Òfrank discussionÓ of these matters.Õ 
ÔThose who are determined to behave like animals can doubtless find out the facts for themselves.Õ 
ÔI am sick, sick of the talk about sex. IÕll have none of it in my school.Õ 
ÔEverything that needs to be done in my school is done individually, and in private by a missionary priest.Õ 
Notice the use of Ômy schoolÕ rather than Ôour schoolÕ. Recently a young girl burned to death because she was ashamed to run naked from a burning house. To some extent her teachers are to blame. HereÕs Sheila Kitzinger on some effects of middle-class prudery. 
ÔIn Jamaica I discovered that the West Indian peasant woman rarely feels discomfort in the perineum, or minds the pressure of the babyÕs head as it descends. But from the case studies of English middle-class women it appears that many of them worry about dirtying the bed and are often shocked by sensations against the rectum and the vagina in labourÑ sensations which they may find excruciating. They feel distressed, in fact, at just those sensations which the peasant woman meets with equanimity. 
ÔSome women find relaxation of the abdominal wall difficult, and especially so when they experience any pain. They have been taught to Òhold their tummies inÓ, and sometimes it goes against the grain to release these muscles.Õ (Sheila Kitzinger, The Experience of Childbirth, Gollancz, 1962.) She adds that women with prolonged labours tended to be ÔinhibitedÕ, embarrassed by the processes taking place in their bodies, ladylike in the extreme, and endured what they were undergoing stoically as long as they were able, without expressing their anxieties. It was not these womenÕs bodies that were causing them difficulties; they were being held up by the sort of people they were. They were not able to give birth.Õ 
When I have been teaching in universities, I havenÕt experienced any problem with censorshipÑat least not on ÔsexualÕ groundsÑand IÕm not saying that fear of obscenity is the most important factor in making people reject the first ideas that come to them, but it does help though, if improvisation teachers are not puritanical, and can allow the students to behave as they want to behave. The best situation is one in which the class is seen as a party, rather than a formal teacher-pupil set-up. If it isnÕt possible to let students speak and act with the same freedom they have outside the school, then it might be better not to teach them drama at all. The most repressed, and damaged, and ÔunteachableÕ students that I have to deal with are those who were the star performers at bad high schools. Instead of learning how to be warm and spontaneous and giving, theyÕve become armoured and superficial, calculating and self-obsessed. I could show you many many examples where education has clearly been a destructive process. 
My feeling isnÕt that the group should be ÔobsceneÕ, but that they should be aware of the ideas that are occurring to them. I donÕt want them to go rigid and blank out, but to laugh, and say ÔIÕm not saying thatÕ or whatever. 
Originality 
Many students block their imaginations because theyÕre afraid of being unoriginal. They believe they know exactly what originality is, just as critics are always sure they can recognise things that are avant-garde. 
We have a concept of originality based on things that already exist. IÕm told that avant-garde theatre groups in Japan are just like those in the West Ñwell of course, or how would we know what they were? Anyone can run an avant-garde theatre group; you just get the actors to lie naked in heaps or outstare the audience, or move in extreme slow motion, or whatever the fashion is. But the real avant-garde arenÕt imitating what other people are doing, or what they did forty years ago; theyÕre solving the problems that need solving, like how to get a popular theatre with some worth-while content, and they may not look avant-garde at all! 
The improviser has to realise that the more obvious he is, the more original he appears. I constantly point out how much the audience like someone who is direct, and how they always laugh with pleasure at a really ÔobviousÕ idea. Ordinary people asked to improvise will search for some original idea because they want to be thought clever. TheyÕll say and do all sorts of inappropriate things. If someone says ÔWhatÕs for supper?Õ a bad improviser will desperately try to think up something original. Whatever he says heÕll be too slow. HeÕll finally drag up some idea like Ôfried mermaidÕ. If heÕd just said ÔfishÕ the audience would have been delighted. No two people are exactly alike, and the more obvious an improviser is, the more himself he appears. If he wants to impress us with his originality, then heÕll search out ideas that are actually commoner and less interesting. I gave up asking London audiences to suggest where scenes should take place. Some idiot would always shout out either ÔLeicester Square public lavatoriesÕ or Ôoutside Buckingham PalaceÕ (never Ôinside Buckingham PalaceÕ). People trying to be original always arrive at the same boring old answers. Ask people to give you an original idea and see the chaos it throws them into. If they said the first thing that came into their head, thereÕd be no problem. 
An artist who is inspired is being obvious. HeÕs not making any decisions, heÕs not weighing one idea against another. HeÕs accepting his first thoughts. How else could Dostoyevsky have dictated one novel in the morning and one in the afternoon for three weeks in order to fulfil his contracts? If you consider the volume of work produced by Bach then you get some idea of his fluency (and weÕve lost half of it), yet a lot of his time was spent rehearsing, and teaching Latin to the choirboys. According to Louis Schlosser, Beethoven said: ÔYou ask me where I get my ideas? That I canÕt say with any certainty. They come unbidden, directly, I could grasp them with my hands.Õ Mozart said of his ideas: ÔWhence and how they come, I know not; nor can I force them. Those that please me I retain in the memory, and I am accustomed, as I have been told, to hum them.Õ Later in the same letter he says: ÔWhy my productions take from my hand that particular form and style that makes them Mozartish, and different from the works of other composers, is probably owing to the same cause which renders my nose so large or so aquiline, or in short, makes it MozartÕs, and different from those of other people. For I really do not study or aim at any originality.Õ 
Suppose Mozart had tried to be original? It would have been like a man at the North Pole trying to walk north, and this is true of all the rest of us. Striving after originality takes you far away from your true self, and makes your work mediocre. 
4 
LetÕs see how these theories work out in practice. Suppose I say to a student, ÔImagine a box. WhatÕs in it?Õ Answers will flash into his mind uninvited. Perhaps: ÔUncle Ted, dead.Õ 
If he said this then people would laugh, and heÕd seem good-natured and witty, but he doesnÕt want to be thought ÔinsaneÕ, or callous. ÔHundreds of toilet rollsÕ, says his imagination, but he doesnÕt want to appear preoccupied with excretion. ÔA big fat, coiled snakeÕ? NoÑtoo Freudian. Finally after a pause of perhaps two whole seconds he says ÔOld clothesÕ or ÔItÕs emptyÕ, and feels unimaginative and defeated. 
I say to a student, ÔName some objects.Õ 
He tenses up. ÔEr . . . pebble . . . er . . . beach . . . cliff . . . er . . . er . . .Õ 
ÔHave you any idea why youÕve blocked?Õ I ask. 
ÔI keep thinking of ÒpebbleÓ.Õ 
ÔThen say it. Say whatever occurs to you. It doesnÕt have to be original.Õ Actually it would be very original to keep saying the same word: ÔPebble. Another pebble. A big pebble. A pebble with a hole in it. A pebble with a white mark. The pebble with a hole in it again.Õ 
ÔSay a wordÕ, I say to someone else. 
ÔEr . . . er . . . cabbage,Õ he says looking alarmed. 
ÔThatÕs not the word you first thought of.Õ 
ÔWhat?Õ 
ÔI saw your lips move. They formed an ÒOÓ shape.Õ 
ÔOrange.Õ 
ÔWhatÕs wrong with the word orange?Õ 
ÔCabbage seemed more ordinary.Õ 
This student wants to appear unimaginative. What sort of crippling experiences must he have gone through before he came to me? 
ÔWhatÕs the opposite of ÒstarfishÓ?Õ 
He gapes. 
ÔAnswer, say it,Õ I shout, because I can see that he did think of something. 
ÔSunflower,Õ he says, amazed because he didnÕt know that was the idea that was about to come out of him. 
A student mimes taking something off a shelf. 
ÔWhat is it?Õ I ask. 
ÔA book.Õ 
ÔI saw your hand reject an earlier shape. What did you want to take?Õ ÔA tin of sardines.Õ 
ÔWhy didnÕt you?Õ 
ÔI donÕt know.Õ 
ÔWas it open?Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔAll messy?Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔMaybe you were opting for a pleasanter object. Mime taking something else off a shelf.Õ 
His mind goes blank. 
ÔI canÕt seem to think of anything.Õ 
ÔDo you know why?Õ 
ÔI keep thinking of the sardines.Õ 
ÔWhy donÕt you take down another tin of sardines?Õ 
ÔI wanted to be original.Õ 
I ask a girl to say a word. She hesitates and says ÔPig.Õ 
ÔWhat was the first word you thought of?Õ 
ÔPea.Õ 
ÔTell me a colour.Õ 
Again she hesitates. 
ÔRed.Õ 
ÔWhat colour did you think of first?Õ 
ÔPink.Õ 
ÔInvent a name for a stone.Õ 
ÔGround.Õ 
ÔWhat was the name you first thought of?Õ 
ÔPebble.Õ 
Normally the mind doesnÕt know that itÕs rejecting the first answers because they donÕt go into the long-term memory. If I didnÕt ask her immediately, sheÕd deny that she was substituting better words. 
ÔWhy donÕt you tell me the first answers that occur to you?Õ 
ÔThey werenÕt significant.Õ 
I suggest to her that she didnÕt say ÔPeaÕ because it suggested urination, that maybe she rejects pink because it reminds her of flesh. She agrees, and then says she rejected ÔPebbleÕ because she didnÕt want to say three words beginning with ÔPÕ. This girl isnÕt really slow, she doesnÕt need to hesitate. Teaching her to accept the first idea will make her seem far more inventive. 
The first time I meet a group I might ask them to mime taking a hat off, or to mime taking something off a shelf, or out of their pocket. I wonÕt watch them while they do it; IÕll probably look out of the window. Afterwards I explain that IÕm not interested in what they did, but in how their minds worked. I say that either they can put their hand out, and see what it closes on; or else they can think first, decide what theyÕll pick up, and then do the mime. If theyÕre worried about failing, then theyÕll have to think first; if theyÕre being playful, then they can allow their hand to make its own decision. 
Suppose I decide to pick up something. I can put my hand down and pick up something dangly. ItÕs and old, used rubber contraceptive, which isnÕt something I would have chosen to pick up, but it is what my hand ÔdecidedÕ to close on. My hand is very likely to pick up something I donÕt want, like a steaming horse-turd, but the audience will be delighted. They donÕt want me to think up something respectable to mime, like a bucket or a suitcase. I ask the class to try doing the mime both with and without ÔthinkingÕ so that they can sense the difference. If I make people produce object after object, then very likely theyÕll stop bothering to think first, and just swing along being mildly interested in what their hands select. HereÕs a sequence that 
was filmed, so I remember it pretty well. I said: ÔPut your hand into an 
imaginary box. What do you take out?Õ 
ÔA cricket ball.Õ 
ÔTake something else out.Õ 
ÔAnother cricket ball.Õ 
ÔUnscrew it. WhatÕs inside?Õ 
ÔA medallion.Õ 
ÔWhatÕs written on it?Õ 
Ô ÒChristmas 1948.ÓÕ 
ÔPut both hands in. What have you got?Õ 
ÔA box.Õ 
ÔWhatÕs written on it?Õ 
Ô ÒExport only.ÓÕ 
ÔOpen it and take something out.Õ 
ÔA pair of rubber corsets.Õ 
ÔPut your hands in the far corners of the box. What have you got?Õ 
ÔTwo lobsters.Õ 
ÔLeave them. Take out a handful of something.Õ 
ÔDust.Õ 
ÔFeel about in it.Õ 
ÔA pearl.Õ 
ÔTaste it. WhatÕs it taste of?Õ 
ÔPear drops.Õ 
ÔTake something off a shelf.Õ 
ÔA shoe.Õ 
ÔWhat size?Õ 
ÔEleven.Õ 
ÔReach for something behind you.Õ 
He laughs. 
ÔWhat is it?Õ 
ÔA breast . . .Õ 
Notice that IÕm helping him to fantasise by continually changing the ÔsetÕ 
(i.e. the category) of the questions. 
5 
There are people who prefer to say ÔYesÕ, and there are people who prefer to say ÔNoÕ. Those who say ÔYesÕ are rewarded by the adventures they have, and those who say ÔNoÕ are rewarded by the safety they attain. There are far more ÔNoÕ sayers around then ÔYesÕ sayers, but you can train one type to behave like the other.3 

ÔYour name Smith?Õ 
ÔNo.Õ 
ÔOh . . . are you Brown, then?Õ 
ÔSorry.Õ 
ÔWell, have you seen either of them?Õ 
ÔIÕm afraid not.Õ 
Whatever the questioner had in mind has now been demolished and he feels fed up. The actors are in total conflict. 
Had the answer been ÔYesÕ, then the feeling would have been completely different. 
ÔYour name Smith?Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔYouÕre the one whoÕs been mucking about with my wife then?Õ 
ÔVery probably.Õ 
ÔTake that, you swine. 
ÔAugh!Õ 
Fred Karno understood this. When he interviewed aspiring actors heÕd poke his pen into an empty inkwell and pretend to flick ink at them. If they mimed being hit in the eye, or whatever, heÕd engage them. If they looked baffled, and ÔblockedÕ him, then he wouldnÕt. 
There is a link with status transactions here, since low-status players tend to accept, and high-status players to block. High-status players block any action unless they feel they can control it. The high-status player is obviously afraid of being humiliated in front of an audience, but to block your partnerÕs idea is to be like the drowning man who drags down his rescuer. ThereÕs no reason why you canÕt play high status, and yet yield to other peopleÕs invention. 
ÔIs your name Smith?Õ 
ÔAnd what if it is?Õ 
ÔYouÕve been making indecent suggestions to my wife.Õ 
ÔI donÕt consider them indecent!Õ 
Many teachers get improvisers to work in conflict because conflict is interesting but we donÕt actually need to teach competitive behaviour; the students will already be expert at it, and itÕs important that we donÕt exploit the actorsÕ conflicts. Even in what seems to be a tremendous argument, the actors should still be co-operating, and coolly developing the action. The improviser has to understand that his first skill lies in releasing his partnerÕs imagination. What happens in my classes, if the actors stay with me long enough, is that they learn how their ÔnormalÕ procedures destroy other peopleÕs talent. Then, one day they have a flash of satoriÑthey suddenly understand that all the weapons they were using against other people they also use inwardly, against themselves. 

ÔWorkingÕ Someone 
Bill Gaskill used to make one actor responsible for the content and development of the scene, while his partner just ÔassistedÕ. 
ÔHave you got it?Õ 
ÔHere it is, Sir.Õ 
ÔWell, unwrap it.Õ 
ÔHere you are, Sir.Õ 
ÔWell, help me put it on.Õ 
ÔThere, Sir. I think itÕs a good fit.Õ 
ÔAnd the helmet.Õ 
ÔHowÕs that, Sir?Õ 
ÔExcellent. Now close the faceplate and start pumping. I shall give three tugs on the rope when I find the wreck. CanÕt be more than twenty fathoms.Õ 
If you concentrate on the task of involving your assistant in some action, then a scene evolves automatically. In my view the game is most elegant when the audience have no idea that one actor is working the other. 
ÔGood morning.Õ 
ÔGood morning.Õ 
ÔYes . . . shall I sit here?Õ 
ÔOh, yes, Sir.Õ 
The first actor sits at a slant in the chair and opens his mouth. The second actor Ôcatches onÕ and mimes pumping the chair higher, like a dentist. 
ÔHaving some trouble, Sir?Õ 
ÔYes. ItÕs one of these molars.Õ 
ÔHmm. LetÕs see now. Upper two occlusal . . .Õ 
ÔAaaauuuggghh!Õ 
ÔMy goodness, that is sensitive.Õ 
The trick is not to think of getting the assistant to do things, but of ways of getting each other into trouble. 
ÔThe regular dentist is on holiday, is he?Õ 
ÔYes, Sir.Õ 
ÔI must say, you seem rather young.Õ 
ÔJust out of dental school, Sir.Õ 
ÔWill you have to extract it? I mean, is it urgent?Õ 
ÔIÕll say itÕs urgent, Sir. Another day or so and that would have exploded.Õ 
The audience will be convinced that itÕs the dentist who is controlling the scene. When improvisers are anxious, each person tries to ÔcarryÕ the whole scene by himself. Putting the responsibility all on to one person helps them work more calmly.4 


Blocking and Accepting 
Blocking is a form of aggression. I say this because if I set up a scene in which two students are to say ÔI love youÕ to each other, they almost always accept each otherÕs ideas. Many students do their first interesting, unforced improvisations during ÔI love youÕ scenes. 
If I say Ôstart somethingÕ to two inexperienced improvisers, theyÕll probably talk, because speech feels safer than action. And theyÕll block any possibility of action developing. 
ÔHallo, how are you.Õ 
ÔOh, same as usual. Nice day, isnÕt it.Õ 
ÔOh I donÕt think so.Õ 
If one actor yawns his partner will probably say ÔI do feel fit today.Õ Each actor tends to resist the invention of the other actor, playing for time, until he can think up a ÔgoodÕ idea, and then heÕll try to make his partner follow it. The motto of scared improvisers is Ôwhen in doubt, say ÒNOÓ.Õ We use this in life as a way of blocking action. Then we go to the theatre, and at all points where we would say ÔNoÕ in life, we want to see the actors yield, and say ÔYesÕ. Then the action we would suppress if it happened in life begins to develop on the stage. 
If youÕll stop reading for a moment and think of something you wouldnÕt want to happen to you, or to someone you love, then youÕll have thought of something worth staging or filming. We donÕt want to walk into a restaurant and be hit in the face by a custard pie, and we donÕt want to suddenly glimpse GrannieÕs wheelchair racing towards the edge of the cliff, but weÕll pay money to attend enactments of such events. In life, most of us are high-skilled at suppressing action. All the improvisation teacher has to do is reverse this skill and he creates very ÔgiftedÕ improvisers. Bad improvisers block action, often with a high degree of skill. Good improvisers develop action: ÔSit down, Smith.Õ 
ÔThank you, Sir.Õ 
ÔItÕs about the wife, Smith.Õ 
ÔShe told you about it has she, Sir?Õ 
ÔYes, yes, sheÕs made a clean breast of it.Õ 
Neither actor is quite sure what the scene is about but heÕs willing to play along, and see what emerges. 
At first students donÕt realise when theyÕre blocking or yielding, and theyÕre not very good at recognising when itÕs happening with other students. Some students prefer to yield (these are ÔcharmingÕ people) but most prefer to block, even though they may have no idea exactly what they are doing. I often stop an improvisation to explain how the blocking is preventing the action from developing. Videotape is a great help: you replay the transaction, and itÕs obvious to everyone. 
A: Augh! 
B: WhatÕs the matter? 
A: IÕve got my trousers on back to front. 
B: IÕll take them off. 
A: No! The scene immediately fizzles out. A blocked because he didnÕt want to get involved in miming having his trousers taken off, and having to pretend embarrassment, so he preferred to disappoint the audience. 
I ask them to start a similar scene, and to avoid blocking if possible. 
A: Augh! 
B: (Holding him) Steady! 
A: My back hurts. 
B: No, it doesnÕt . . . Yes, youÕre right. B has noticed his error in blocking, which resulted from his wishing to stick to the trouser idea. A then blocks his own idea by shifting to another. 
A: IÕm having trouble with my leg. 
B: IÕm afraid IÕll have to amputate. 
A: You canÕt do that, Doctor. 
B: Why not? 
A: Because IÕm rather attached to it. 
B: (Losing heart) Come, man. 
A: IÕve got this growth on my arm too, Doctor. 
During this scene B gets increasingly fed up. Both actors experience the other as rather difficult to work with. They can say ÔThe scene isnÕt workingÕ, but they still donÕt consciously realise why. IÕve written down the dialogue while they were playing the scene, and I go through it, and explain exactly how they were interacting, and why B was looking more and more depressed. 
I get them to start the scene again, and this time theyÕve understood. 
A: Augh! 
B: Whatever is it, man? 
A: ItÕs my leg, Doctor. 
B: This looks nasty. I shall have to amputate. 
A: ItÕs the one you amputated last time, Doctor. (This is not a block because heÕs accepted the amputation.) 
B: You mean youÕve got a pain in your wooden leg? 
A: Yes, Doctor. 
B: You know what this means? 
A: Not woodworm, Doctor! 
B: Yes. WeÕll have to remove it before it spreads to the rest of you. (AÕs chair collapses) B: My God! ItÕs spreading to the furniture! (And so on.) The interest to the audience lies in their admiration and delight in the actorsÕ attitude to each other. We so seldom see people working together with such joy and precision. 
HereÕs another scene I noted down. 
A: Is your name Smith? 
B: Yes. 
A: IÕve brought the . . . car. I interrupt and ask him why he hesitated. A says he doesnÕt know, so I ask him what he was going to say. He says ÔElephantÕ. 
ÔYou didnÕt want to say ÒelephantÓ because there was one mentioned in the last scene.Õ 
ÔThatÕs right.Õ 
ÔStop trying to be original.Õ 
I make them restart the scene. 
A: IÕve brought the elephant. 
B: For the gelding? 
A: (Loudly) No! The audience groan and cry out with disappointment. They were enthralled with the possibilities latent in a scene about gelding an elephant, the elephant suddenly fizzing down to nothing at the first cut, or cutting the trunk off by mistake, or a severed penis chasing the actors about the room. But of course this is why A felt impelled to block. He didnÕt want to be involved in anything so obscene or psychotic. He resisted the very thing that the audience longed to see. 
I call anything that an actor does an ÔofferÕ. Each offer can either be accepted, or blocked. If you yawn, your partner can yawn too, and therefore accept your offer. 
A block is anything that prevents the action from developing, or that wipes out your partnerÕs premise.5 
If it develops the action it isnÕt a block. For example: ÔYour name Smith?Õ ÔWhat if it is, you horrible little man!Õ This is not a block, even though the answer is antagonistic. Again: ÔIÕve had enough of your incompetence, Perkins! Please leave.Õ ÔNo, Sir!Õ This isnÕt a block either. The second speaker has accepted that heÕs a servant, and he accepts the situation, one of annoyance between himself and his employer. If a scene were to start with someone saying ÔUnhand me, Sir Jasper, let me goÕ, and her partner said ÔAll right, do what you like, thenÕ, this is probably a block. It would get a laugh but it would create bad feeling. Once you have established the categories of ÔofferÕ, ÔblockÕ and ÔacceptÕ you can give some very interesting instructions. For example, you can ask an actor to make dull offers, or interesting offers, or to ÔoveracceptÕ, or to Ôaccept and blockÕ and so on. You can programme two actors so that A offers and accepts, and B offers and blocks. 
A: Hallo, are you a new member? 
B: No, IÕve come to fix the pipes. You got a leak somewhere? 
A: Yes, oh thank goodness. ThereÕs three feet of water in the basement. 
B: Basement? You ainÕt got a basement. 
A: No, well, er, the boiler-room. ItÕs just down a few steps. YouÕve not brought your tools. 
B: Yes I have. IÕm miming them. 
A: Oh, silly of me. IÕll leave you to it then. 
B: Oh no. I need an assistant. Hand me that pipe wrench. (And so on.) Sometimes both actors can block as well as offer. Bad improvisers do this all the time, of course, but when you tell people to block each other their morale doesnÕt collapse so easily. This again suggests to me that blocking is aggressive. If the order comes from me, the actors donÕt take it personally. 
A: Are you nervous? 
B: Not at all. I can see that you are. 
A: Nonsense. IÕm just warming my fingers up. YouÕre taking the piano exam, are you? 
B: IÕm here for my flying lesson. 
A: In a bathing costume? 
B: I always wear a bathing costume. 
Me: YouÕve accepted the bathing costume. (laughter.) An interesting offer can be ÔThe house is on fire!Õ, or ÔMy heart! Quick, my pills!Õ but it can also be something non.specific. ÔAll right, whereÕs the parcel?Õ or ÔShall I sit here, Doctor?Õ are interesting offers, because we want to know what will happen next. Even ÔAll right, beginÕ is OK. Your partner can beat you on the head with a balloon, and you thank him, and the audience are delighted. 
HereÕs an example in which A makes dull offers, while B makes interesting offers. 
A: (Dull offer.) Good morning! 
B: (Accepts.) Good morning. (Makes interesting offer.) Great heavens! Frank! Did they let you out? Have you escaped? 
A: (Accepts.) I hid in the laundry van. (Makes dull offer.) I see youÕve had the place redecorated. 
B: (Accepts, makes interesting offer.) Yes . . . but . . . look . . . about the money. YouÕll get your share. It wasnÕt my idea to cut you out. IÕve . . . IÕve got a good business here . . . 
A: (Accepts, makes dull offer.) Yes, itÕs a step up in the world. 
B: (Accepts, makes interesting offer.) It was different in the 
old days . . . I . . . I didnÕt mean to rat on you Charlie . . . The actors have automatically become involved in some sort of gangster scene, but all they actually worry about is the category the offers fit into. The scene Ôlooks after itselfÕ. 
Scenes spontaneously generate themselves if both actors offer and accept alternately. 
ÔHavenÕt we met before?Õ 
ÔYes, wasnÕt it at the yacht club?Õ 
ÔIÕm not a member.Õ 
(Accepts the yacht club. A bad improviser would say Ôwhat yacht club?Õ) ÔAh, IÕm sorry.Õ 
ÔSchool!Õ 
ÔThatÕs right. I was in the first form and you were one of the school leavers.Õ 
ÔPomeroy!Õ 
ÔSnodgrass!Õ 
ÔAfter all these years!Õ 
ÔWhat do you mean, after all these years? It seems only yesterday that you were beating me up every lunchtime.Õ 
ÔOh well . . . boys will be boys. Was it you we held out of the window by your feet?Õ 
ÔButterfingers.Õ 
ÔI see youÕre still wearing the brace.Õ 
Good improvisers seem telepathic; everything looks prearranged. This is 
because they accept all offers madeÑwhich is something no ÔnormalÕ person would do. Also they may accept offers which werenÕt really intended. I tell my actors never to think up an offer, but instead to assume that one has already been made. Groucho Marx understood this: a contestant at his quiz game ÔfrozeÕ so he took the manÕs pulse and said, ÔEither this manÕs dead or my watch has stopped.Õ If you notice that you are shorter than your partner you can say ÔSimpkins! DidnÕt I forbid you ever to be taller than me?ÕÑwhich can lead on to a scene in which the servant plays on all fours, or a scene in which the master is starting to shrink, or a scene in which the servant has been replaced by his elder brother, or whatever. If your partner is sweating, fan yourself. If he yawns, say ÔLate, isnÕt it?Õ 
Once you learn to accept offers, then accidents can no longer interrupt the action. When someoneÕs chair collapsed Stanislavsky berated him for not continuing, for not apologising to the character whose house he was in. This attitude makes for something really amazing in the theatre. The actor who will accept anything that happens seems supernatural; itÕs the most marvellous thing about improvisation: you are suddenly in contact with people who are unbounded, whose imagination seems to function without limit. 
By analysing everything into blocks and acceptances, the students get insight into the forces that shape the scenes, and they understand why certain people seem difficult to work with. 
These Ôoffer-block-acceptÕ games have a use quite apart from actor training. People with dull lives often think that their lives are dull by chance. In reality everyone chooses more or less what kind of events will happen to them by their conscious patterns of blocking and yielding. A student objected to this view by saying, ÔBut you donÕt choose your life. Sometimes you are at the mercy of people who push you around.Õ I said, ÔDo you avoid such people?Õ ÔOh!Õ she said, ÔI see what you mean.Õ 
6 
Here are some games IÕve used with my students. 

ÔTwo PlacesÕ 
You can play very funny scenes in which one character plays, for example, waiting at a bus stop, while another character claims that the stage is his living-room, and so on. Such scenes exploit blocking very successfully. (This game comes from the Royal Court WritersÕ Group, circa 1959.) ÔPresentsÕ 
I invented a rather childish game, which is now often used with small children, but works really well with grown-ups, if you coax them through their initial resistance. 
I divide people into pairs and call them A and B. A gives a present to B who receives it. B then gives a present back, and so on. At first each person thinks of giving an interesting present, but then I stop them and suggest that they can just hold their hands out, and see what the other person chooses to take. If you hold out both hands about three feet apart, then obviously it will be a larger present, but you donÕt have to determine what your gift is. The trick is to make the thing you are given as interesting as possible. You want to ÔoveracceptÕ the offer. Everything you are given delights you. Maybe you wind it up and let it walk about the floor, or you sit it on your arm and let it fly off after a small bird, or maybe you put it on and turn into a gorilla. 
An important change of thinking is involved here. When the actor concentrates on making the thing he gives interesting, each actor seems in competition, and feels it. When they concentrate on making the gift they receive interesting, then they generate warmth between them. We have strong resistances to being overwhelmed by gifts, even when theyÕre just being mimed. You have to get the class enthusiastic enough to go over the ÔhumpÕ. Then suddenly great joy and energy are released. Playing in gibberish helps. 

ÔBlind OffersÕ 
An inexperienced improviser gets annoyed because his partners misunderstand him. He holds out his hand to see if itÕs raining, and his partner shakes it and says ÔPleased to meet you.Õ ÔWhat an idiotÕ, thinks the first actor, and begins to sulk. When you make a blind offer, you have no intention to communicate at all. Your partner accepts the offer, and you say ÔThank you.Õ Then he makes an intentionless gesture, and you accept that, and he says ÔThank youÕ and so on. 
A strikes a pose. 
B photographs him. 
A says ÔThank you.Õ 
B stands on one leg, and bends the other. 
A straddles the bent leg and Ônails a horseshoe on itÕ. 
B thanks him and lies on the ground. 
A mimes shovelling earth over him. 
B thanks him . . . And so on. 
DonÕt underestimate the value of this game. ItÕs a way of interacting that the audience love to see. They will watch fascinated, and every time someone says ÔThank youÕ, they laugh! 
ItÕs best to offer a gesture which moves away from the body. When youÕve made a gesture, you then freeze in the position until your partner reacts. 
Once the basic technique has been mastered, the next step is to get the actors to play the game while discussing some quite different subject. 
ÔA touch of autumn in the air today, James,Õ says A, stretching his hand out. ÔYes, it is a little brisk,Õ says B, peeling a glove off AÕs hand. B then lies on the floor. ÔIs the Mistress at home?Õ says A, wiping his feet on B . . . and so on. The effect is startling, because each actor seems to have a telepathic understanding of the otherÕs intentions. 

ÔItÕs TuesdayÕ 
This game is based on ÔoveracceptingÕ. We called it ÔItÕs TuesdayÕ because thatÕs how we started the game. If A says something matter of fact to B, like ÔItÕs TuesdayÕ, then maybe B tears his hair, and says ÔMy God! The BishopÕs coming. WhatÕll he do when he sees the state everythingÕs in?Õ or instead of being upset he can be overcome with love because itÕs his wedding day. All that matters is that an inconsequential remark should produce the maximum possible effect on the person itÕs said to. 
A: ItÕs Tuesday. 
B: No . . . it canÕt be . . . ItÕs the day predicted for my death 
by the old gypsy! (It doesnÕt matter how crummy the idea is, what matters is the intensity of the reaction.) Now B turns white, clutches his throat, staggers into the audience, reels back, bangs his head on the wall, somersaults backwards, and ÔdiesÕ making horrible noises, and saying at his last gasp: B: Feed the goldfish. 
A now plays ÔItÕs TuesdayÕ on the goldfish remark. Maybe he expresses extreme jealousy: A: ThatÕs all he ever thought about, that goldfish. What am I to do now? HavenÕt I served him faithfully all these years? (Weeps on knee of audience member.) HeÕs always preferred that goldfish to me. Do forgive me, Madam. Does . . . does anyone have a Kleenex? Fifty yearsÕ 
supply of antsÕ eggs, and what did he leave to meÑnot a penny. (Throws spectacular temper tantrum.) I shall write to Mother. This last remark introduces new material, so that B now plays ÔItÕs TuesdayÕ on that. 
B: (Recovering) Your mother! You mean Milly is still alive? He then plays passionate yearning, until he canÕt take the emotion any further and throws in another ÔordinaryÕ remark. Any remark will do. ÔForgive me Jenkins, I got rather carried away.Õ Maybe Jenkins can then do a five-minute ÔhateÕ tirade: ÔForgive you? After the way you hounded her? Turning her out into the snow that Christmas Eve . . .Õ and so on. 
Three or four sentences can easily last ten minutes, when expanded a little, and the audience are astounded and delighted. They donÕt expect improvisers, or actors for that matter, to take things to such extremes. 
I would classify ÔItÕs TuesdayÕ as a Ômake boring offers, and overacceptÕ game. 

ÔYes, But . . .Õ 
This is a well known Ôaccept-and-blockÕ game (described in Viola Spolin, Improvisation for the Theatre). (Its twin game ÔYes, and . . .Õ is an Ôaccept-and-offerÕ game.) IÕll describe it because there are two ways of playing which produce opposite results, and which tell one a lot about the nature of spontaneity. 
A asks questions that B can say ÔYesÕ to. B then says ÔBut . . .Õ and then whatever occurs to him. To play the game badly, B should think of his reply before he begins to speak. 
ÔExcuse me, is that your dog?Õ 
ÔYes, but IÕm thinking of selling him.Õ 
ÔWill you sell him to me?Õ 
ÔYes, but heÕs expensive.Õ 
ÔIs he healthy?Õ 
ÔYes, but you can take him to a vet to check him out if you like.Õ (And so on.) Probably the audience do not laugh, and probably the actors donÕt enjoy the experience much. This is because the more logical, rational part of the mind is in control. 
If you reply ÔYes, but . . .Õ with enthusiasm, as soon as the question is put to you, and then say whatever comes into your head, the scenes are quite different. IÕll play it with myself now, typing as quickly as possible. 
ÔDonÕt I know you?Õ 
ÔYes, but IÕm going.Õ 
ÔYou took my money!Õ 
ÔYes, but IÕve spent it.Õ 
ÔYouÕre a swine.Õ 
ÔYes, but everyone knows that.Õ 
This time an audience would probably laugh. ItÕs worth teaching both ways of playing the game. It can demonstrate to uptight people exactly how cautious they usually are. Also itÕs funny to launch out strongly on ÔYes, but . . .Õ, and then have to complete the sentence off the top of your head. 

Verse 
If the students are in a really happy mood, I might ask them to improvise in verse. At first theyÕre appalled. IÕll already have made them play scenes in gibberish, and as impromptu operas, but theyÕll have been turned off verse by school, while at the same time retaining an exaggerated respect for it. 
To me the most enjoyable thing about verse is its spontaneity. You can Ôfake upÕ verse by deciding what to write, and then thinking up the rhymes, but if youÕre asked to improvise it you just have to abandon conscious control, and let the words come of their own accord. 
I start to talk in verse, and explain that it doesnÕt matter whether the verse is good or bad, and that anyway weÕre going to start with the worst possible verse: ÔTom and Else take your places, 
A happy smile on your faces, 
DonÕt start wondering what to say 
Or we will never start today! 
WeÕll have Tom come in and propose 
ÕCause ElseÕs pregnant, I suppose . . .Õ 
The worse the verse I speak, the more encouraged the actors are. I get them to stop thinking ahead, and just say a line, and trust to luck that thereÕll be something to rhyme with it. If theyÕre in trouble and canÕt think what to say, theyÕre not to rack their brain, and try and force their inspiration. I get them to say ÔpromptÕ and then either I shout something out, or one of the audience does. 
Once a scene starts, the verse has to control the content and the action. Someone says: ÔAt last IÕve got you in my clutches: 
IÕll keep you here and take you crutches.Õ He wonÕt make any attempt to mime taking crutches away from his partner though, until I yell ÔTake the crutches!Õ Then his partner falls over and says: ÔOh, please Sir Jasper, let me go! 
You must not treat a cripple so . . .Õ 
ÔOh no! IÕll not be robbed of my revenge! 
IÕll sacrifice you here in old Stonehenge.Õ He makes no attempt to sacrifice her, I have to tell him: 
ÔDo itÑitÕs what you said! 
Everyone wants to see her dead.Õ 
ÔLie down on yonder block and pray . . . prompt?Õ 
ÔIÕll kill her at the break of day . . .Õ suggests someone in the audience. 
No one in their right senses would think up a scene about sacrificing a cripple at Stonehenge, but the verse precipitates it. My job is to get the actors to go where the verse takes them. If you donÕt care what you say, and you go with the verse, the exercise is exhilarating. But if an actor suddenly produces a really witty couplet, youÕll see him suddenly ÔdryÕ as his standard rises, and he tries to produce ÔbetterÕ verse. 
7 
Reading about spontaneity wonÕt make you more spontaneous, but it may at least stop you heading off in the opposite direction; and if you play the exercises with your friends in a good spirit, then soon all your thinking will be transformed. Rousseau began an essay on education by saying that if we did the opposite of what our own teachers did weÕd be on the right track, and this still holds good. 
The stages I try to take students through involve the realisation (1) that we struggle against our imaginations, especially when we try to be imaginative; (2) that we are not responsible for the content of our imaginations; and (3) that we are not, as we are taught to think, our ÔpersonalitiesÕ, but that the imagination is our true self. 
NOTES 
1. 

I donÕt know who originated the rubber glove gag, but in his book King of Comedy (Peter Davies, 1955) Mark Sennet attributes it to Felix Adler. 

2. 

Teachers are obliged to impose a censorship on their pupils, and in consequence schools provide an anti-therapeutic environment. In Interacting with Patients (Macmillian, New York, 1963), a work intended for nurses, Joyce Samhammer Hays and Kenneth Larson describe therapeutic and non-therapeutic ways of interacting. Here are their first ten Ôtherapeutic techniquesÕ. 


Therapeutic techniques Examples 
Using silence: 
Accepting: Yes. Uh Hmm. I follow what you said. Nodding. 
Giving recognition: Good morning, Mr S. YouÕve tooled a leather wallet. I notice that youÕve combed your hair. 
Offering self: IÕll sit with you a while. IÕll stay here with you. IÕm interested in your comfort. 
Giving broad openings: Is there something youÕd like to talk about? What are you thinking about? Where would you like to begin? 
Offering general leads: Go on. And then? Tell me about it. 
Placing the event in time or What seemed to lead up to . . .? in sequence: Was this before or after . . .? 
When did this happen? 
Making observations: You appear tense. Are you uncomfortable when you . . . I notice you are biting your lips. It makes me uncomfortable when you . 
. . Encouraging description of Tell me when you feel anxious. perceptions: What is happening? 
What does the voice seem to be saying? Encouraging comparison: Was this something like . . .? Have you had similar experiences? 
Obviously the book has psychiatric nurses in mind, but itÕs interesting to compare it to teacher-pupil interactions. Here are the first ten Ônon.
therapeutic techniquesÕ.  
Non-therapeutic  Examples  
techniques  
Reassuring:  I wouldnÕt worry about . . .  
Everything will be all right.  
YouÕre coming along fine.  
Giving approval:  ThatÕs good.  
IÕm glad that you . . .  
Rejecting:  LetÕs not discuss . . .  
I donÕt want to hear about . . .  
Disapproving:  ThatÕs bad.  
IÕd rather you wouldnÕt . . .  
Agreeing:  ThatÕs right.  
I agree.  
Disagreeing:  ThatÕs wrong.  
I definitely disagree with . . .  
I donÕt believe that.  
Advising:  I thing you should . . .  
Why donÕt you . . .?  
Probing:  Now tell me about . . .  
Tell me your life history.  
Challenging:  But how can you be President of the  
United  
States?  
If youÕre dead, why is your heart beating?  
Testing:  What day is this?  
Do you know what kind of a hospital this  
is?  
Do you still have the idea that . . .?  

IÕm doing the book an injustice by quoting out of context, but itÕs widely available, and it analyses many interactions. Schools make it difficult for teachers to interact therapeutically. Thinking back to my own schooling, I remember how isolated the teachers were, how there were only certain areas in which you could communicate with them at all. If teachers were allowed to interact in a therapeutic manner, then the adjective Ôschool-teacheryÕ would not be disparaging. 
3. 
When I meet a new group of students they will usually be ÔnaysayersÕ. This term and its opposite, ÔyeasayersÕ, come from a paper by Arthur Couch and Kenneth Kenison, who were investigating the tendency of people answering questionnaires to be generally affirmative, or generally negative in attitude. They wrote in Freudian terms: ÔWe have arrived at a fairly consistent picture of the variables that differentiate yeasayers from naysayers. Yeasayers seem to be Òid-dominatedÓ personalities, with little concern about or positive evaluation of an integrated control of their impulses. They say they express themselves freely and quickly. Their Òpsychological inertiaÓ is very low, that is, very few secondary processes intervene as a screen between underlying wish and overt behavioural response. The yeasayers desire and actively search for emotional excitement in their environment. Novelty, movement, change, adventureÑ these provide the external stimuli for their emotionalism. They see the world as a stage where the main theme is Ôacting outÕ libidinal desires. In the same way, they seek and respond quickly to internal stimuli: their inner impulses are allowed ready expression . . . the yeasayerÕs general attitude is one of stimulus acceptance, by which we mean a pervasive readiness to respond affirmatively or yield willingly to both outer and inner forces demanding expression. 
ÔThe ÒdisagreeingÓ naysayers have the opposite orientation. For them, impulses are seen as forces requiring control, and perhaps in some sense as threats to general personality stability. The naysayer wants to maintain inner equilibrium; his secondary processes are extremely impulsive and value maintaining forces. We might describe this as a state of high psychological inertiaÑimpulses undergo a series of delays, censorships, and transformations before they are permitted expression. Both internal and external stimuli that demand response are carefully scrutinised and evaluated: these forces appear as unwelcome intruders into a subjective world of ÒclassicalÓ balance. Thus, as opposed to the yeasayers, the naysayersÕ general attitude is one of stimulus rejectionÑa pervasive unwillingness to respond to impulsive or environmental forces.Õ (ÔYeasayers and NaysayersÕ, Journal of Abnormal and Social Psychology, Vol. 160, No. 2, 1960.) 4. 
My impro group used to set up a Ôsay ÒYesÓÕ game using a tape recorder. We would record a one-sided dialogue, and then play the tape during a show, and get an actor who did not know what was on the tape to improvise with it. It means you have to accept the tape or fail totally, since the tape canÕt adapt to you. One tape ran like this: ÔHallo. (Pause.) No, no, me, IÕm down here. On the footpath. (Pause.) IÕm an ant. (Pause.) Pick me up, will you? (Pause.) Go careful. (Pause.) We want to surrender. (Pause.) WeÕre fed up with being stepped on, bloody great things. Dictate your terms. (Pause.) Excuse me interrupting. Can you see what IÕm holding? (Pause.) Hold me up to your eye. (Pause.) Closer. (Pause.) Now. Pick up Willy. Put your hand down and heÕll climb on. Feel him. (Pause.) Put him on your shoulder. (Pause.) You may feel him climbing up into your ear. WhatÕs that Willy? He says thereÕs a lot of wax here. (Pause.) Right now you may hear a sort of crinkling noise. (Pause.) ThatÕs Willy blowing up a paper bag. Any trouble from you and heÕll burst it against your eardrum. (Pause.) (Huge explosion.) Do it again, Willy, just to show him. (Explosion. Pause.) Well? What have you got to say, ant-murderer? (Pause.) WeÕll talk later. Get moving. Walk. Left, right, left, right . . .Õ 
5. 
A Japanese text compares, two actors who block each other to Ôtwo mantids eating each other. They fight with each other; if one puts out a hand it is eaten off; if one puts out a leg, it is eaten off, so that it is natural that in the end they destroy each other.Õ (The ActorÕs Analects, translated by Charles J. Dunn and Bunzo Torigoe, Columbia University Press, 1969.) A problem for the improviser is that the audience are likely to reward blocking at the moment it first appears. 
ÔYour name Smith?Õ ÔNo!Õ (Laughter.) They laugh because they enjoy seeing the actors frustrated, just as theyÕll laugh if the 
actors start to joke. Jokey TV or radio programmes usually stop for a song, or some animation, every few minutes. The improviser, who is committed to performing for longer periods, gags or blocks at his peril, although the immediacy, of the audienceÕs laughter is likely to condition him to do just this. 
Once the performers have been lured into gagging or blocking, the audience is already on the way towards irritation and boredom. More than laughter they want action. 


Narrative Skills 

1 
Playboy: Knife in the Water was an original, and unusual screenplay. Where did you get the idea for it? 
Polanski: It was the sum of several desires in me. I loved the lake area in Poland and I thought it would make a great setting for a film. I was thinking of a film with a limited number of people in it as a form of challenge. I hadnÕt ever seen a film with only three characters, where no one else even appeared in the background. The challenge was to make it in a way that the audience wouldnÕt be aware of the fact that no one else had appeared even in the background. As for the idea, all I had in mind when I began the script was a scene where two men were on a sailboat and one fell overboard. But that was a starting-point, wouldnÕt you agree? 
Playboy: Certainly, but a strange one. Why were you thinking about a man falling out of a sailboat? 
Polanski: There you go, asking me how to shrink my head again. I donÕt know why. I was interested in creating a mood, an atmosphere, and after the film came out, a lot of critics found all sorts of symbols and hidden meanings in it that I hadnÕt even thought of. It made me sick. (Playboy, December, 1971.) I started my work on narrative by trying to make the improvisers 
conscious of the implications of the scenes they played. I felt that an artist 
ought to be ÔcommittedÕ, and that he should be held responsible for the 
effects of his workÑit seemed only common sense. I got my students to analyse the content of Red Riding Hood and The Sleeping Beauty and Moby Dick and The Birthday Party, but this made them even more inhibited. I didnÕt realise that if the people who thought up Red Riding Hood had been aware of the implications, then they might never have written the story. This was at a time when I had no inspiration as a writer at all, but I didnÕt twig that the more I tried to understand the ÔrealÕ meaning, the less I wrote. When Pinter directs his own plays he may say ÔWe may assume that what the author intended here is . . .ÕÑand this is a sensible attitude: the playwright is one person and the director another, even when they share the same skull. 
When I ran the Royal CourtÕs script department, I used to read about fifty plays a week, and many of them seemed to betray their authorÕs conscious intention. At one time there was a glut of plays about homosexual lovers whose happiness, or even lives, are destroyed by the opposition of ignorant bigots. I didnÕt see these as pro-homosexual although IÕm sure their authors did. If I wrote such a play my homosexuals would live happily ever after, just as my Goldilocks would end up living in a commune with the bears. Recent films in which the good lawman comes to grief when he tries to fight the system (Walking Tall, Serpico) have the moral ÔDonÕt stick your neck outÕ, but this may not be what their directors intend. In the old days the honest sheriff was triumphant; nowadays heÕs crippled, or dead. Content lies in the structure, in what happens, not in what the characters say. 
Even at the level of geometrical signs ÔmeaningÕ is ambiguous. A cross, a circle, and a swastika contain a ÔcontentÕ quite apart from those which we assign to them. The swastika is symmetrical but unbalanced: itÕs a good sign for power, it has a clawiness about it (cartoonists drew swastika spiders scrabbling over the face of Europe). The circle is stiller, is a much better sign for eternity, for completeness. The cross can stand for many things, for a meeting-place, for a crossroads, for a kiss, for a reed reflected in a lake, for a mast, for a swordÑbut it isnÕt meaningless just because the interpretations arenÕt one-for-one. Whatever a cross suggests to us it wonÕt have the same associations as a circle, which makes a much better sign for a moon, for example, or for pregnancy. Moby Dick may be a symbol for the Ôlife-forceÕ, or for ÔevilÕ and we can add anything it suggests to us, but the area of legitimate association is limited. There are things the white whale doesnÕt symbolise, as well as things it does, and once you start combining signs together in a narrative the whole thing becomes too complex. A story is as difficult to interpret as a dream, and the interpretation of a dream depends on whoÕs doing the interpreting. When King Lear really gets going Ñthe mad King, the man pretending to be mad, the fool paid to be mad, and the whole mass of overlapping and contradictory associationsÑwhat can the spectator sensibly do but be swept away on the flood, and experience the play, instead of trying to think what it ÔmeansÕ. 
My decision was that content should be ignored. This wasnÕt a conclusion I wished to reach, because it contradicted my political thinking. I hadnÕt realised that every play makes a political statement, and that the artist only needs to worry about content if heÕs trying to fake up a personality he doesnÕt actually have, or to express views he really isnÕt in accord with. I tell improvisers to follow the rules and see what happens, and not to feel in any way responsible for the material that emerges. If you improvise spontaneously in front of an audience you have to accept that your innermost self will be revealed. The same is true of any artist. If you want to write a Ôworking-class playÕ then youÕd better be working class. If you want your play to be religious, then be religious. An artist has to accept what his imagination gives him, or screw up his talent. 
Alex Comfort once filmed some of my work, and he seemed surprised when I told him that my students never attacked me physically. HeÕd been explaining that I was really operating as a therapist, that I was coaxing students into areas that would normally be ÔforbiddenÕ, and that spontaneity means abandoning some of your defences. 
I didnÕt have an answer at the time, except to say ÔWell, they donÕtÕ, but my refusal to attribute any importance to content may be the answer. If my students produce disturbing material I link it with ideas of my own, or with something someone else has produced, and I stop them feeling isolated or ÔpeculiarÕ. Whatever dredges up from their unconscious IÕll accept, and treat as ÔnormalÕ. If I seized on the content of scenes as revealing secrets about the student, then IÕd be perceived as a threat. TheyÕd have to ÔloveÕ me, or ÔhateÕ me. IÕd have negative and positive transference states to contend withÑwhich would be a hindrance. 
Once you decide to ignore content it becomes possible to understand exactly what a narrative is, because you can concentrate on structure. 
My dictionary says a story is Ôa sequence of events that have, or are alleged to have happened, a series of events that are or might be narrated . . . a personÕs account of his life or some portion of it . . . a narrative of real or, more usually, fictitious events, designed for the entertainment of the hearer . . .Õ and so on. Even a small child knows that a story isnÕt just a series of events, because he says ÔAnd is that the end?Õ If we say ÔA story is a series of events that might be narratedÕ then we beg the question, which is: ÔWhy do we narrate one series of events but not another?Õ 
I had to decide what a story was, and present a theory that an improviser could use on the spur of the moment in any situation. Obviously, the Ôseventeen basic plotsÕ approach would be too limiting. I needed a way to handle anything that cropped up. 
Suppose I make up a story about meeting a bear in the forest. It chases me until I come to a lake. I leap into a boat and row across to an island. On the island is a hut. In the hut is a beautiful girl spinning golden thread. I make passionate love to the girl . . . 
I am now ÔstorytellingÕ but I havenÕt told a story. Everyone knows it isnÕt finished. I could continue forever in the same way: Next morning I am walking around the island when an eagle seizes me and carries me high into the sky. I land on a cloud and find a path leading to Heaven. To one side of the path I notice a lake with three swans. One of the swans suddenly disappears, and an old man stands in his place . . . 
The trouble with such a sequence is that thereÕs no place where it can stop, or rather, that I can stop anywhere; you are unconsciously waiting for another activity to start, not free association, but reincorporation.
