LetÕs begin the story again: I escape from a bear by rowing across to an island. Inside a hut on the island is a beautiful girl bathing in a wooden tub. IÕm making passionate love to her when I happen to glance out of the window. If I now see the bear rowing across in a second boat, then there was some point in mentioning him in the first place. If the girl screams ÔMy lover!Õ and hides me under the bed, then this is better storytelling, since IÕve not only reintroduced the bear, but IÕve also linked him to the girl. The bear enters the hut, unzips his skin, and emerges as the grey old man who makes love to the girl. I creep out of the hut taking the skin with me so that he canÕt change back into a bear. I run down to the shore and row back to the mainland, towing the second boat behind me (reintroducing the boats). Then I see the old man paddling after me in the tub. He seems incredibly strong and thereÕs no escape from him. I wait for him among the trees, and pull the bearskin around myself. I become a bear and tear him to piecesÑ thus IÕve reincorporated both the man and the skin. I row back to the island and find the girl has vanished. The hut has become very old and the roof is sagging in, and trees that were young saplings are now very tall. Then I try to remove the skin and I find itÕs sealed up around me. 
At this point a child would probably say ÔAnd is that the end?Õ because clearly some sort of pattern has been completed. Yet at no time have I thought about the content of the scene. I presume itÕs about sexual anxieties and fear of old age, or whatever. Had I ÔknownÕ this, then I wouldnÕt have constructed that particular story, but as usual the content has looked after itself, and anyway is only of interest to critics or psychologists. What matters to me is the ease with which I Ôfree-associateÕ and the skill with which I reincorporate. 
HereÕs a Ôgood nightÕ story made up by me and Dorcas (age six). 
ÔWhat do you want a story about?Õ I asked. 
ÔA little bird,Õ she said. 
ÔThatÕs right. And where did this little bird live?Õ 
ÔWith Mummy and Daddy bird.Õ 
ÔMummy and Daddy looked out of the nest one day and saw a man coming through the trees. What did he have in his hand?Õ 
ÔAn axe.Õ 
ÔAnd he took the axe and started chopping down all the trees with a white mark on. So Daddy bird flew out of the nest, and do you know what he saw on the bark of his tree?Õ 
ÔA white mark.Õ 
ÔWhich meant?Õ 
ÔThe man was going to cut down their tree.Õ 
ÔSo the birds all flew down to the river. Who did they meet?Õ 
ÔMr Elephant.Õ 
ÔYes. And Mr Elephant filled his trunk with water and washed the white mark away from the tree. And what did he do with the water left in his trunk?Õ 
ÔHe squirted it over the man.Õ 
ÔThatÕs right. And he chased the man right out of the forest and the man never came back.Õ 
ÔAnd is that the end of the story?Õ 
ÔIt is.Õ 
At the age of six she has a better understanding of storytelling than many university students. She links the man to the birds by giving him an axe. 
She links up the water left in the trunk with the woodcutter, whom she remembers weÕd shelved. She isnÕt concerned with content but any narrative will have some (about insecurity, I suppose). 
I say to an actress, ÔMake up a story.Õ She looks desperate, and says, ÔI canÕt think of one.Õ 
ÔAny story,Õ I say. ÔMake up a silly one.Õ 
ÔI canÕt,Õ she despairs. 
ÔSuppose I think of one and you guess what it is.Õ 
At once she relaxes, and itÕs obvious how very tense she was. 
ÔIÕve thought of one,Õ I say, Ôbut IÕll only answer ÒYesÓ, ÒNoÓ, or ÒMaybeÓ.Õ 
She likes this idea and agrees, having no idea that IÕm planning to say ÔYesÕ to any question that ends in a vowel, ÔNoÕ to any question that ends in a consonant, and ÔMaybeÕ to any question that ends with the letter ÔYÕ. 
For example, should she ask me ÔIs it about a horse?Õ IÕll answer ÔYesÕ since ÔhorseÕ ends in an ÔEÕ. 
ÔDoes the horse have a bad leg?Õ 
ÔNo.Õ 
ÔDoes it run away?Õ 
ÔMaybe . . .Õ 
She can now invent a story easily, but she doesnÕt feel obliged to be ÔcreativeÕ, or ÔsensitiveÕ or whatever, because she believes the story is my invention. She no longer feels wary, and open to hostile criticism, as of course we all are in this culture whenever we do anything spontaneously. Her first question is: ÔHas the story got any people in it?Õ 
ÔNo.Õ 
ÔHas it got animals in it?Õ 
ÔNo.Õ 
ÔHas it got buildings in it?Õ 
ÔYes.Õ (IÕm having to drop my rule about consonants, or sheÕd get too discouraged.) ÔDoes the building have anything to do with the story?Õ 
ÔMaybe.Õ 
ÔDoes it have aeroplanes in it?Õ 
ÔNo.Õ 
ÔFish?Õ 
ÔNo.Õ 
ÔInsects?Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔDo the insects play a large part in the story?Õ 
ÔMaybe.Õ 
ÔDo they live underground?Õ 
ÔNo.Õ 
ÔDo they start out as harmless?Õ 
ÔNo.Õ 
ÔDo the insects take over the world?Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔAre they as big as elephants?Õ 
ÔNo.Õ 
ÔDo they take any poison?Õ 
ÔNo!Õ 
ÔIs it a gradual process, this taking over of the world?Õ 
ÔNo.Õ 
ÔWere there many insects?Õ 
ÔNo.Õ 
ÔDo the insects gain anything by destroying the world?Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔDo they reign utterly alone?Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔDo they destroy the world in a foul manner?Õ 
ÔNo.Õ 
ÔDoes the story begin with their existing?Õ 
ÔNo.Õ 
ÔBut there arenÕt any people in this bloody story. So it must start with the insects. Have the insects been reigning alone in the world for a long time?Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔDo they live in the buildings that used to be the peopleÕs buildings?Õ ÔYes.Õ 
ÔAnd then they suddenly decide to destroy the world?Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔAnd they donÕt die. And when they eat everything in sight they become larger?Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔAnd then they canÕt fit into the buildings again? 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔAnd is that the end of the story?Õ 
ÔIt is.Õ 
If she got more than two ÔNosÕ in a row I sometimes said ÔYesÕ to encourage her, and in the end I said ÔyesÕ all the time because she was getting discouraged. We used to play this game at parties, and people who claim to be unimaginative would think up the most astounding stories, so long as they remained convinced that they werenÕt responsible for them. The great joke was to lure somebody into inventing a story about a midget dentist sexually assaulting Siamese twins, or whatever, wait until he accused you of having really perverted minds, and then explain triumphantly that he had created the story himself. Faubion Bowers once wrote an article on this game, in, I think, Playboy. 
To some extent such stories are due to chance, but you can see in the last example that a story is struggling to get out. She doesnÕt ask ÔAre the insects harmless?Õ, she says ÔDo they start out as harmless?Õ so that you know she has the intention of creating some destructive force. She also wants them to be big. She says ÔAre they as big as elephants?Õ and gets the answer ÔNo,Õ but she still ends up getting them gigantic, since they eat so much that they canÕt fit into the buildings. SheÕs been lured into constructing one of the basic myths of our culture, the apparently harmless force that destroys the environmentÑand itself. Notice how she shapes the story by recapitulation. She links the buildings and the insects, and she reintroduces the buildings again at the end. She says, ÔIs that the end?Õ because she knows sheÕs linked up the story. It must be obvious that when someone insists that they ÔcanÕt think up a storyÕ, they really mean that they ÔwonÕt think up a storyÕÑwhich is OK by me, so long as they understand itÕs a refusal, rather than a Ôlack of talentÕ. 
2 
The improviser has to be like a man walking backwards. He sees where he has been, but he pays no attention to the future. His story can take him anywhere, but he must still ÔbalanceÕ it, and give it shape, by remembering incidents that have been shelved and reincorporating them. Very often an audience will applaud when earlier material is brought back into the story. They couldnÕt tell you why they applaud, but the reincorporation does give them pleasure. Sometimes they even cheer! They admire the improviserÕs grasp, since he not only generates new material, but remembers and makes use of earlier events that the audience itself may have temporarily forgotten. 
It seems obvious to teach storytelling as two separate activities. I get the actors to work in pairs, with Actor A telling a story for thirty seconds, and then with Actor B finishing it for thirty seconds. Actor A is to provide disconnected material, and Actor B is somehow to connect it. 
A: It was a cold winterÕs night. The wolves howled in the trees. The concert pianist adjusted his sleeves and began to play. An old lady was shovelling snow from her door . . . 
B: . . . When she heard the piano the little old lady began shovelling at fantastic speed. When she reached the concert hall she cried, ÔThat pianist is my son!Õ Wolves appeared at all the windows, and the pianist sprang on to the piano, thick fur growing visibly from under his clothes. 
Or again: 
A: An old lady sits in her lighthouse very worried because the sea has dried up and there are no ships for her light to warn. In the middle of the desert a tap has been dripping since the beginning of time. In the heart of the jungle, in a little hut, an old man sits cross-legged . . . 
B: . . . ÔI canÕt stand that dripping sound,Õ he cries, leaping up and making a great journey to the centre of the desert. Nothing he can do will stop the tap dripping. ÔAt least I can turn it on,Õ he cries. Immediately the desert flourishes, the seas fill up again, and the old lady is very happy. She travels to the jungle to thank the old man, and ever afterwards keeps a picture of him and his hut above the mantelpiece in the lighthouse. 
And again: 
A: A man sits in a cave surrounded by pieces of bicycles. There is a fire outside the cave, and a woman is sending up smoke signals. Some children are playing in the river. An aeroplane passes over the valley and breaks the sound barrier . . . 
B: . . . The sonic boom makes the children look up. They see the smoke signals. ÔDaddy mended the bicycles,Õ they shout. When they run back to the cave a strange sight meets their eyes: not bicycles, but a flying-machine made out of all the pieces. Leaping on, they all pedal into the air, and fly around the valley all day. 
Sometimes Actor A will try to make it ÔeasyÕ for Actor B. This actually makes it more difficult. 
A: There was a little old lady in Putney who ran a fish-and.chip shop. All the people liked her, especially the local cats, because she used to give them scraps of fish. Also she didnÕt charge much, not to poor people, so they saved up and bought her a birthday present . . . 
B: ThereÕs nothing for me to do. SheÕs joined it all up herself. Me: True! 
IÕm not saying that this method produces great literature, but you can get people inventing stories who previously claimed they could never think of any. 
Once people have learned to play each stage of this game with no effort or anxiety, I let them play both halves themselves. I say ÔFree-associateÕ, and then when theyÕve produced unconnected material, I say ÔConnectÕ, or ÔReincorporateÕ. 
A knowledge of this game is very useful to a writer. First of all it encourages you to write whatever you feel like; it also means that you look back when you get stuck, instead of searching forwards. You look for things youÕve shelved, and then reinclude them. 
If I want people to free-associate, then I have to create an environment in which they arenÕt going to be punished, or in any way held responsible for the things their imagination gives them. I devise techniques for taking the responsibility away from the personality. Some of these games are very enjoyable and others, at first encounter, are rather frightening; people who play them alter their view of themselves. I protect the students, encourage them and reassure them that theyÕll come to no harm, and then coax them or trick them into letting the imagination off its leash. 
One way to bypass the censor who holds our spontaneity in check is to distract him, or overload him. I might ask someone to write out a paragraph on paper (without premeditation) while counting backwards aloud from a hundred. IÕll try it now as IÕm typing: ÔExtra. I fall through the first storey of the car park. The driver throughout the night thought the soft concrete slit his genitals thoughtfully. Nurse Grimshaw fell further . . .Õ 
I got to sixty until I felt my brain was going to explode. ItÕs like trying to write after a severe concussion. Try it. ItÕs very surprising to see what something in you ÔwantsÕ to write when it gets the chance. 
You might try drawing a picture with two hands at once. The trick is to keep your attention equally divided, rather than switching quickly from hand to hand. Also you shouldnÕt decide what to draw; just sit down with a blank mind and draw as quickly as possible. This regresses your mind to about five years of age. Curiously, each hand seems to draw with the same level of skill. 
3 
Lists 
If I tell a student, ÔSay a wordÕ, heÕll probably gawp. He wants a context in which his answer will be ÔrightÕ. He wants his answer to bring credit to him, thatÕs what heÕs been taught answers are for. 
ÔWhy canÕt you just say whatever comes into your head?Õ 
ÔYes, well, I donÕt want to speak nonsense.Õ 
ÔAny word would have done. A spontaneous reply is never nonsense.Õ This puzzles him. 
ÔAll right,Õ I say, Ôjust name me a list of objects, but as quick as you can.Õ 
ÔEr . . . cat, dog, mouse, trap, dark cellar . . .Õ 
He trails off, because he feels that the list is somehow revealing something about himself. He wants to keep his defences up. When you act or speak spontaneously, you reveal your real self, as opposed to the self youÕve been trained to present. 
Nonsense results from a scrambling process, and takes time. You have to consider your thought, decide whether it gives you away, and then distort it, or replace it with something else. The studentÕs ÔtrapÕ and Ôdark cellarÕ were threatening to release some anxiety in him. If heÕd continued with the list, speaking as quickly as possible, heÕd have revealed himself as not quite so sane and secure as he pretends. IÕll try typing out some nonsense as fast as I can and see what I get. 
ÔThe lobster bites the foot. Freda leaps skyward, back falling prone on to the long breakwater. Archie Pellingoe the geologist leaping up around down and upon her lovingly chews her alabaster sandwich . . .Õ 
This is still partly scrambled, because I canÕt type quickly enough. I managed to censor some of it, but I wasnÕt able to remove all the sexual content. I veered away from the lobster suspecting the image to be vaguely erotic, but it got worse. The only way I could have made it meaningless would be to type more slowly, and to substitute other images. This is what my students do all the time. I ask them for an idea and they say Ô. . . oh . . . aahh . . . um . . .Õ as if they couldnÕt think of one. The brain constructs the universe for us, so how is it possible to be ÔstuckÕ for an idea? The student hesitates not because he doesnÕt have an idea, but to conceal the inappropriate ones that arrive uninvited. 
I make my students improvise lists of objects to make them understand that there are two processes they can use. You can make rational jumps from one object to the next: ÔDog, cat, milk, saucer, spoon, fork . . .Õ or you can improvise a non-associative list. IÕll type out one as quickly as I can. ÔDuck, rhomboid, platypus, elephantÕs egg, cactus, Johnnie Ray, clock face, East Acton . . .Õ ItÕs like emptying all sorts of garbage from your mind that you didnÕt know was there. Try it. ItÕs more difficult than you think, but it stops people caring what comes out of their minds. IÕll try again. ÔDead nun, postbox, cat-o-nine-tails, cement hopper, mouse-juice, Pope Urban the Eighth, a blob, giant opera singer, piece of lettuce, a kazoo, a vivisected clown, a lump of interstellar dust, limpet shell, moving lava, red minibus, stamen, sickle-cell anaemia . . .Õ 
A sequential list feels like one you Ôthink upÕ. A non-sequential list seems to arrive by itself. One day IÕm sure thereÕll be an explanation for the two processes. Students choose the first way, and have to be coaxed into attempting the second. They feel as if theyÕre being bombarded with the thoughts of someone else. They canÕt understand why such bizarre lists should occur to them. I tell them that itÕs perfectly natural, and that hypnagogic images come in the same way. 

Associating Images 
One of the earliest games we played at the studio involved associating images. We developed it from word-association games and we found that if someone gives an image suddenly, this will automatically trigger off another image in his partnerÕs mind. Someone says ÔA lobster . . .Õ and someone answers ÔWith a flower in its clawÕ, and the juxtaposition does imply a content: ÔA torn photograph . . .Õ Ô. . . An empty roomÕ; ÔBasket of eggs . . .Õ Ô. . . Cement mixerÕ. Afterwards you can see that a lobster with a flower in its claw is a good symbol of insensitivity, for someone locked out of the world of feeling, and so on, yet none of these associations were conscious at the moment that the pairing image jerked out. 
HereÕs some genuine nonsense assembled by C. E. Shannon and based on word-probabilities: ÔThe head and frontal attack on an English writer that the character of this point is therefore another method for the letters that the time of whoever told the problem for an unexpected . . .Õ and so on. This is not the sort of thing spewed out by the unconscious. 

ÔCharactersÕ 
One way to trigger off narrative material is to put the students in groups of three, and have them invent a name for a character, and see if they can agree on what heÕs like. For example. 
ÔBetty Plum.Õ 
ÔBig breasts.Õ 
ÔYes. A barmaid.Õ 
ÔEr . . .Õ 
ÔWell, she has worked as a barmaid . . .Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔLives in a room with blue curtains.Õ 
ÔA stuffed toy dog on the dresser . . .Õ 
ÔWhich she keeps her nightdress in.Õ 
ÔNylon.Õ 
The group continue until they know who she lives with, her taste in music, her secret ambition, the sorrow in her life, etc. The important thing is that the students should really agree, they shouldnÕt just make compromises. As soon as one person disagrees they wipe the character out, and start on another. Soon they learn to develop a character much further, and in a way that satisfies all of them. ÔGeorge HoneywellÑ keeps beesÑ smokes a pipeÑmarriedÑwas marriedÑin love with the daughter of the tobacconistÑwears a soft capÑheÕs a voyeurÑlikes dogs . . .ÕAnd so on. 
Automatic Writing 
Automatic writing is one way of getting students to understand that there is Ôsomething inside them besides themselvesÕ. Normally this skill is rare, but I have invented a method that works for most people; actually I suppose I should call it Ôautomatic readingÕ. HereÕs how I coaxed a poem from a volunteer at a public lecture. 
ÔMime taking a book from a shelf,Õ I said. 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔWhat colour is it?Õ 
ÔBlue.Õ 
ÔDid you have to think up the colour or did you see it?Õ 
ÔIt was blue.Õ 
ÔOpen it at the flyleaf. Can you see the name of the publisher?Õ 
ÔItÕs faint.Õ 
ÔSpell it.Õ 
ÔH . . . o . . . d . . . Hodson.Õ 
ÔAnd the name of the book?Õ 
ÔIn . . .Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔThe . . .Õ 
ÔYes . . . try to spell it.Õ 
ÔC . . . country.Õ 
ÔIn the Country . . . author?Õ 
ÔAlex . . . ander Pope.Õ 
ÔOpen it till you come to a page of verse. WhatÕs the page number?Õ 
ÔThirty-nine.Õ 
ÔFind me a line of verse.Õ 
ÔSo that we . . .Õ 
ÔAre you seeing it or inventing it?Õ 
ÔSeeing it.Õ 
ÔNext word.Õ 
ÔItÕs blurred.Õ 
ÔIÕve given you a magnifying-glass . . .Õ 
I continued drawing the poem out of her, until sheÕd ÔreadÕ two verses. Then I stopped because she was finding the experience frightening. So did the audience, because it really didnÕt seem to be a poem she was inventing, yet someone was inventing it. 
So that we can be happy 
Together in our loves 
Since you were away I have been alone. 
Having been so close 
I cannot live again 
Many years will pass 
Till I live again. 
The personality will often try to resist this method by saying ÔItÕs in RussianÕ, or ÔItÕs too tiny to read the printÕ, and so on. I say ÔThereÕs something written in English in the marginÕ or ÔIÕm shrinking you down to the size of the bookÕ, or something suitable. ItÕs easy to switch from Ôautomatic readingÕ to my form of Ôautomatic writingÕ. You just look at a blank sheet of paper, and ÔseeÕ a word, and then write it where you ÔsawÕ it. IÕve filled many exercise books using this method, partly to see where it led me, and partly to know what happens if you go past the point where you feel impelled to stop. IÕve learned a lot about myself this way. Again thereÕs a great gap between what I would choose to write, and what actually emerges. HereÕs a bit that sounds like a statement about the imagination. 
The great dragon dare not stir The trainer watches kindly but 
At the slightest movement taps it on the nose 
The eyes glint fire and yet the muscles dare 
Not exert themselves nor let the flame burst forth 
Which would engulf the city in one flash. 
The trainer speaks of kindness and consoles 
And says he is the dragonÕs only friend 
Sometimes the dragon purrs but oh the pain 
Of never moving those enormous limbs. 
HereÕs another one, not at all like ÔmyÕ writing. 
Windmills 
The vanes split apart 
All the mechanism rusts 
Growing children talk Words turn to dust. 
Look where the ocean 
Clogged with oil stands 
Still struggling seabird Below on black sand. 
Where on the headland Does a lighthouse blaze now? 
The endless waves mount Desire fails below. 

ÔDreamsÕ 
A game we got from America uses relaxation to bypass the censor. ItÕs used by psychologists, and IÕve seen dire warnings about other people using it. Most psychologists who use games rely very heavily on the discoveries of people working in the theatre, and my guess is that the Ôguided dreamÕ came from the theatre in the first place. (Frederick Perls says he was once a pupil of Max Reinhardt!) Anyway, I think the warnings are due to the same kind of fear that Mask work and hypnosis inspire. ItÕs true that if someone is hovering on the edge of insanity one little push may topple him over, but a bus trip can be just as disturbing as anything that happens in a theatre class. 
If I get you to lie down, close you eyes and relax, and report what your imagination gives you, then youÕll probably go into a deep state of absorption, and instead of Ôthinking things upÕ the experiences will seem to be really happening to you. Afterwards, if I ask ÔDid you feel the floor?Õ then youÕll probably say, ÔThere wasnÕt any floor.Õ If I say, Ôdid you experience your body?Õ youÕll probably answer, ÔI wasnÕt in my bodyÕ or ÔI was in the body I had in the story.Õ 
I begin by suggesting something like ÔYouÕre on a beach?Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔIs it sandy or stony?Õ 
ÔSandy.Õ 
ÔDid you think that up?Õ 
ÔNo, I just knew.Õ 
ItÕs very likely that the student will want to stay on the beach and not be moved. I ask if he can see anything, or anyone, but heÕll usually be alone. I tell him heÕs lain on the beach a long time, and then I suggest that he moves to the water, or away from it. If I donÕt tell him heÕs been there a long time, heÕll probably refuse to Ôget upÕ. The sort of story IÕll expect to get may involve him walking along the shore, passing a cave. I may suggest he looks in the cave, or wades into the ocean, but probably heÕll prefer to go on walking. Maybe he walks up to the top of the cliffs and looks down. Then I stop him. 
Most people will have a good experience with this game, and sometimes itÕs like paradise. It can also be pretty hellish. I watch their breathing, and if they seem alarmed I take them out, or steer them towards something less alarming. I coax them near to threatening areas: IÕll suggest they enter the cave or swim in the ocean, but I wonÕt push them. 
Once the basic technique is mastered, I let students try it again. This time theyÕll be bolder. TheyÕll encounter other people, theyÕll have adventures, but IÕll still guide them away from Ôbad tripsÕ. IÕm using the game to demonstrate to the student that he can be effortlessly creative, not to teach him that his imagination is terrifying and should be suppressed! People can get upset playing the game, but if they weep you can cuddle them, which makes them feel better. When people abreact I always establish that (1) itÕs good for them; (2) theyÕll feel marvellous in half an hour; (3) it Ôhappens to everybodyÕ. 
Advanced students, whom you know well, may want to set off on deeper and more fearful journeys. ThatÕs all right when they know what theyÕre doing. One way is to have them cuddled by other students while they play the game. If they start to express great alarm, take them out, be calm, tell them to open their eyes, rock them if necessary. Two people can go on a journey together, each trying to have the same fantasy. The essential thing is not that the student should abreact, but that he should have the experience of imagining something ÔeffortlesslyÕ, and ÔchoicelesslyÕ. He should understand through this game that he doesnÕt have to do anything in order to imagine, any more than he needs to do anything in order to relax or perceive. 
HereÕs a dream in which I was the questioner, and the ÔvictimÕ a drama student. 
ÔWhat sort of stories do you like?Õ 
ÔScience fiction. Well . . . Tolkien, Stories like The Hobbit.Õ 
ÔOK. Imagine a lake surrounded by mountains.Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔYou are swimming in the lake.Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔCan you see any fish?Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔLarge ones?Õ 
ÔNo.Õ 
ÔShoals of little ones turning and darting?Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔThere is one particular fish. What do you do with it?Õ 
ÔI catch it.Õ 
ÔYou swim back to the shore and three hooded figures are waiting for you. What do you give them?Õ 
ÔThe fish.Õ 
ÔAnd what do they give you in exchange?Õ 
ÔA stick.Õ 
ÔWhat do you do with it?Õ 
ÔI point it at an oak tree and it vanishes.Õ 
ÔAnd then?Õ 
ÔI point it at the three hooded figures and they vanish too.Õ 
ÔYou set out through the woods. Does the path lead up or down?Õ 
ÔUp.Õ 
ÔWhat do you hear? Is it from your right or your left?Õ 
ÔLeft. Someone crying.Õ 
ÔYou look down into a clearing and see a woman surrounded by . . .?Õ 
ÔLittle men.Õ 
ÔWhatÕs she wearing? Anything?Õ 
ÔSheÕs naked.Õ 
ÔThe little men see you?Õ 
ÔTheyÕre coming at me waving sticks.Õ 
ÔThe woman calls to them?Õ 
ÔShe says itÕs not me who did it.Õ 
ÔWas it someone from the castle?Õ 
ÔYes, he threw her out naked into the forest.Õ 
ÔDo you help her?Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔDo you go up the path?Õ 
ÔI put my cloak round her and we set off to the castle.Õ 
ÔIt gets dark?Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔAnd you are going to sleep?Õ 
ÔWe cover ourselves with leaves and we lie about eight feet apart.Õ 
ÔYouÕre fast asleep when you wake up to feel her touching you.Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔWhatÕs she after?Õ 
ÔThe stick.Õ 
ÔDoes she get it?Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔShe points it at you and what happens?Õ 
ÔIt goes all grey and wintry.Õ 
ÔWhat do you see in the mist?Õ 
ÔA huge oak tree, and three hooded figures leaping about and shouting.Õ 
At this point the story has obviously ended (because of a brilliant reincorporation), and we roll about on the floor roaring with laughter. WeÕre very pleased to have co-operated so effortlessly. 
YouÕll notice that my suggestions are mostly in the form of questions. He said ÔYesÕ to most of them, because we had a good rapport, and I knew what to ask. Such ÔdreamsÕ are intensely real to the person lying down, and pretty vivid to the questioner. This happened years ago, but I still have the ÔvisionÕ of the story sharp in my mind. I could easily draw illustrations to every part of it. To be a good questioner you have to enter something like the same trance state as the person answering. 

ÔExpertsÕ 
When Vahktangov, one of StanislavskyÕs favourite pupils, was directing Turandot he asked the wise men to set themselves impossible problems. When they were onstage they were always to be secretly trying to solve problems like ÔHow do you make a fly the size of an elephant?Õ 
I adapted this idea to use in ÔinterviewsÕ. One actor plays a TV interviewer, and his partner becomes an ÔexpertÕ who has to convince us that heÕs an authority on his subject. 
The best way to think up the questions is to start a sentence without knowing how itÕs going to end. You say: ÔGood evening . . . We are fortunate enough to have Professor Trout in the studio with us, who has just returned from Africa where he has been teaching hippopotamuses to . . .Õ You have no idea what to say next, but almost anything will do: Ô. . . to do handstandsÕ or Ô. . . to yodelÕ. If you try to Ôthink upÕ impossible questions, itÕs very difficult. Once you start the sentence ÔHow do you turn a pig into . . .Õ itÕs very easy to conclude it Ô. . . a fire station.Õ 
If you are asked, ÔHow do you teach hippopotamuses to knit?Õ you are likely to hedge: ÔWell now, we have, as you know, a large number of these hippopotamuses which the Government has assembled in the hope that they will eventually boost KenyaÕs export trade. WeÕre hoping to sell about ten thousand pullovers a year soon.Õ You waffle on like that, hoping that a nice idea will occur to you, but this isnÕt a good way of really amazing an audience. ItÕs much better to give any answer. The interviewerÕs job is to hold the ÔexpertÕ to the problem of answering. 
ÔYes, but how exactly did you teach them?Õ 
ÔIt was the carrot and the whip really.Õ 
ÔBut what techniques?Õ 
The ÔexpertÕ has agreed to answer the problem as part of the game, and he understands that the interviewer is trying to help him in demanding an immediate answer. Once he Ôjumps inÕ, and stops hedging, the game is simple. 
ÒWell I demonstrated the stitches. Then I gave them sharpened telegraph poles and about a mile of barbed wire.Õ 
ÔDidnÕt they have trouble holding on to the poles?Õ 
ÔYes, well they would. They lack the opposed thumb. They do have quite good co-ordination though, and are very suited to activities of a repetitive nature.Õ 
ÔBut how exactly did they hold the poles?Õ 
ÔAh! Leather pole-holders strapped on to the forearms, or, er, in common parlance, feet.Õ 
ÔBut the pullovers, werenÕt they rather uncomfortable?Õ 
ÔTerrible. We were starting with barbed wire and telegraph poles just to give them the general idea. If you try with wool right away, they keep snapping it.Õ 
ÔQuite.Õ 
ÔItÕs all a matter of grading. You get Õem on to rope, and then string, and finally theyÕll be doing crochet.Õ 
ÔDo you have any examples of their work?Õ 
ÔIÕm wearing it. Every article of my clothing was knitted by the Kilimanjaro Hippo Co-operative.Õ (And so on.) ItÕs a little difficult on the printed page to show how pleasurable the game is. ItÕs not so much what is said, but the expertÕs eagerness to supply instant answers. The audience know that theyÕd hedge, and beat about the bush, and they have a great respect for a performer who doesnÕt try any evasions. Sometimes such interviews are hysterically funny. ItÕs very good if the interview refers to ÔchartsÕ that he imagines on the wall, and asks the ÔexpertÕ to explain them, or if the activity can be demonstrated. If youÕve been teaching mushrooms to yodel, the interviewer can say, ÔI believe youÕve brought some of your soloists with you this evening.Õ Anyone from the audience would hastily deny this. ItÕs so nice when the expert says ÔYesÕ and calls them in, or mimes taking them out of his pocket. 

ÔVerbal ChaseÕ 
Students can become better at playing ÔExpertsÕ if they play a Ôverbal chaseÕ game first. 
For example: Suppose I say ÔImagine a box.Õ A student can predict that the next thing I will say is something like ÔWhatÕs in it?Õ Instead I say ÔWho put you in there?Õ ÔMy father,Õ he says, anticipating a further question like ÔWhy did he put you there?Õ Instead I say ÔWhat have you got in there with you?Õ He replies ÔA toiletÕ. I donÕt know what he anticipates now, but certainly not what I do say, which is ÔWhatÕs written on the outside of the box?Õ ÔLadies!Õ he says, collapsing with laughter. 
We all laugh, I suppose because of the implied homosexuality. If I were to point this out, then the student would feel the need to guard against me. Instead, I ignore the content, and concentrate on trying to jerk the answers out of the student as quickly as possible. 
I say to another student: ÔYouÕre in a street. What street are you in?Õ ÔMain street.Õ 
ÔWhatÕs the shop?Õ 
ÔA fishmongerÕs.Õ 
ÔWhat does the fishmonger point at you?Õ 
ÔA pistol.Õ 
ÔWhat comes out?Õ 
ÔVinegar.Õ 
Again everyone laughs and is very pleased. Answering such questions is easy. Asking them is very difficult, because you have to change the ÔsetÕ of the questions each time. HereÕs a sequence recorded in connection with a TV show. I was working with a girl student IÕd just met for the first time. 
ÔWhere are you?Õ 
ÔHere!Õ 
ÔYouÕre not. Where are you?Õ 
ÔIn a box.Õ 
ÔWho put you there?Õ 
ÔMummy.Õ 
ÔSheÕs not really your mummy. Who is she?Õ 
ÔSheÕs my aunt.Õ 
ÔWhatÕs her secret plan?Õ 
ÔTo kill me.Õ 
ÔWhat with?Õ 
ÔA knife.Õ 
ÔShe sticks the knife where?Õ 
This question freaks her, because itÕs so sexual. 
ÔIn . . . in . . . in my stomach.Õ 
ÔShe cuts it open and takes out a handful of papery . . .Õ 
ÔBoxes.Õ 
ÔOn the boxes is written . . .?Õ 
Ô ÒHelp!Ó Õ 
ÔWho wrote it?Õ 
ÔI did.Õ 
ÔWhoÕs in the box? Crawling out?Õ 
ÔA spider.Õ 
ÔA spider marked . . .?Õ 
Ô ÒYESÓ.Õ 
ÔThe spider does what?Õ 
ÔIt eats me.Õ 
ÔInside the spider you meet?Õ 
ÔMy father.Õ 
ÔHolding?Õ 
ÔA . . . a . . . a . . . elephant.Õ 
ÔBy the . . .Õ 
ÔTail.Õ 
Everyone falls about with laughter, as if weÕd been telling jokes, and they understand that some of sequence has come to an end. A student who becomes an expert questioner, that is, who becomes very ingenious at changing the ÔsetÕ of the questions, becomes a better improviser. Speed is important, so that the questions and answers are a little too fast for ÔnormalÕ thought. 
Some questioners start doing all the work. For example: ÔYouÕre walking along a road.Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔYou meet a giant.Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔYou fall into a pool and are eaten by crocodiles.Õ 
ÔYes.Õ 
ÔEr . . . er . . .Õ 
This could be rephrased, and would then work. 
ÔYouÕre walking alongÑwhat?Õ 
ÔA road.Õ 
ÔA giant does what to you?Õ 
ÔThrows me into a pond.Õ 
ÔWhat do the crocodiles bite?Õ 
The more ÔinsaneÕ the questions, the better in jerking spontaneous answers from the ÔvictimÕ. 

ÔWord at a TimeÕ 
If I ask someone to invent the first line of a short story, heÕll unconsciously rephrase the question. HeÕll tense up, and probably say ÔI canÕt think of one.Õ HeÕll really act as if heÕs been asked for a good first line. Any first line is really as good as any other, but the student imagines that heÕs been asked to think up dozens of first lines, then imagine the type of stories they might give rise to, and then assess the stories to find the best one. This is why he looks appalled and mumbles Ô. . . oh . . . dor . . . um . . .Õ 
Even if I ask some people for the first word of a short story theyÕll panic and claim that they ÔcanÕt think of oneÕ, which is really amazing. The question baffles them because they canÕt see how to use it to display their ÔoriginalityÕ . . . A word like ÔtheÕ or ÔonceÕ isnÕt good enough for them. 
If I ask one student for the first word of a story, and another for the second word, and another for the third word, and so on, then we could compose a story in this way: ÔThereÕ . . . ÔwasÕ . . . ÔaÕ . . . ÔmanÕ . . . ÔwhoÕ . . . ÔlovedÕ . . . ÔmakingÕ . . . ÔpeopleÕ . . . ÔhappyÕ. 
One version of the gameÑwhich I still play occasionallyÑinvolved telling a story around a circle as quickly as possible. Sometimes we did it to a beat. Anyone who ÔblockedÕ we threw out until only two people were left. You can make the game tougher by having each person who speaks point to the person who is to say the next word, thereÕs no way to anticipate when your turn will come. 
Anyone who tries to control the future of the story can only succeed in ruining it. Every time you add a word, you know what word you would like to follow. Unless you can continually wipe your ideas out of your mind youÕre paralysed. You canÕt adapt to the words said by other people. 
ÔWe . . . (went for a walk) . . .Õ 
ÔAre . . . (nice people) . . .Õ 
ÔGoing . . . (to the circus) . . .Õ 
ÔAway . . . (for a holiday) . . .Õ 
ÔTo . . . (the country) . . .Õ 
ÔExplore . . . (the amazon) . . .Õ 
ÔA . . . (cave) . . .Õ 
ÔGiant!Õ 
Once you say whatever comes to mind, then itÕs as if the story is being told by some outside force. I wouldnÕt be surprised to find that there are cultures which use the method as a form of divination. The group learn that this method of storytelling wonÕt work unless they relax, stop worrying about being ÔobviousÕ and remain attentive. I have played it in darkened rooms with the group lying on their backs with their heads at the centre of a circle. I remember at RADA we once pulled curtains over ourselves and lay there like a huge pudding. After the group has played the game with their eyes shut, get them to walk about and observe any perceptual changes. (Colours become brighter, people and spaces seem of a different size, focus is sharper.) Our normal thinking dulls perception, but the word-at-a-time game can shut some of the normal screening off. (ItÕs not a good game for German speakers because of the rules about verbs coming at the end!) I divide students into groups of four and get them to compose ÔlettersÕ a word at a time. They all relax and one of the players writes the letter down. I was describing this technique to an Eng. Lit. graduate. 
ÔI donÕt think I could ever learn such a game,Õ she said. 
ÔTry it,Õ I said, and wrote Ô89Õ at the top of a sheet of paper. 
ÔWhatÕs that?Õ she asked. 
ÔThe beginning of the address.Õ 
ÔI donÕt know what to put.Õ 
This intelligent girl was suffering. She was claiming to be ÔuncreativeÕ but was really just terrified sheÕd give something away. 
ÔYou know how addresses start on letters.Õ 
ÔWell . . . all right. ÒTheÓ.Õ 
ÔElmsÕ, I wrote. 
Ô89 The Elms canÕt be an address.Õ 
ÔItÕll do.Õ 
ÔI canÕt think of anything else. IÕve got a block.Õ 
I wrote ÔblockÕ down. Then I wrote ÔJanÕ. 
ÔMarchÕ she said, looking helpful. 
I put an oblique stroke between ÔJanÕ and ÔMarchÕ. She looked as if she was under great stress. She wanted to fail but didnÕt know how to. She was afraid that the game might make her reveal secret things about herself. 
ÔDearÕ, I wrote. 
ÔHenryÕ, she said after a long pause. 
ÔI.Õ 
ÔHope.Õ 
ÔMrs.Õ 
ÔI donÕt know who to put.Õ 
ÔAny name. There isnÕt any way to choose a name thatÕs wrong.Õ 
ÔExeterÕ, she said, and seemed suddenly to realise that the game could be fun. The completed ÔletterÕ read like this: 


89 The Elms Block 
Jan/March 
Dear Henry, 
I hope Mrs Exeter has been behaving well. Mum hopes that you will take off your bra. You will not proceed to any other perversions. The Vicar says Mrs White is a cow. Do you allow Mrs White to help you go to the bathroom? 
Yours sincerely, 
Arthur PS I hate you. 
Not an inspired letter, but once she got over her initial resistance she became fascinated by the game, and played it many times. 
Word-at-a-time letters usually go though four stages: (1) the letters are usually cautious or nonsensical and full of concealed sexual references; (2) the letters are obscene and psychotic; (3) they are full of religious feeling; 
(4) finally, they express vulnerability and loneliness. 
Improvisations go through similar stages if you donÕt censor them, and if you work with the same group day after day. Here is a sequence of ÔlettersÕ which were written late one night, by three drama students (two boys and a girl). They said it took them a couple of hours, what with talking, and opening more beers, and so on. IÕd told them that the stories changed if they persisted in writing them, but I hadnÕt told them what to expect. They stopped when they were too scared to write any more. You can see the ÔarmourÕ peeling off letter by letter. Some of the paragraphs have titles, which I think were arrived at by spelling them out a letter at a time. 
1. 
ÔHow did he walk on the water when it was raining? I donÕt think that God exists (in garbage cans). Polacks began to fix their dynamite to the end of their tools which shuddered and vibrated radically. ÒJohn is a prick,Ó said Mary, Òwhy canÕt he fuck my arse, the bastard!Ó Jeremy and Fiona lay in a compromising position with green and yellow forceps plucking their pubic hair which rustled like reeds in a storm which was raging then. Tomorrow we must go with Jane to old mansions and buy all the paraphernalia required for our happy transactions in the nuptial bed. Why did Mary pull JeremyÕs trousers off his legs and burn with green fingers? She stroked his beard and began fondling his nosebag. It began to get warm in the greenhouse, plants wilted and detumesced. If the rain couldnÕt get into the trough all the plants would die. How will Mother walk when it begins raining?Õ 

2. 
ÔBecause Mary felt ill, she went to the doctors. Did he feel reluctant to examine her? She couldnÕt pass water and fart when asked. ÒYou may leave the basin on the table if water is spilling down your legs.Ó I thought that we can perhaps catch ourselves in bed. Basins frighten ghosts and mice, but spiders walk around chairs and breathe softly. Can I hear myself breathe? Only God can produce ChristÕs image on church walls without seeming to 

characterise. If water falls gently on to the spider it will die. Tomorrow is ChristÕs birthday and we must celebrate with balloons and razors. Should we allow Christ to die? Perhaps he can save us, perhaps he can obliterate us. Fear is always present with me. God is dead. Big tits can make me feel happy, and saved. Mary and I are not related and can only marry if God permits. Why canÕt we live by ourselves?Õ 

3. 
ÔPurgation. Lightning strikes trees but only when it rains hard. Water runs along green branches carrying specks of bird-shit. Clouds follow the sun which shines only on holidays. Thunder is loud but soft in rain. Why can trees blow their leaves towards the houses? Why does rain trickle down my trees? I like rain when it splatters against my house and face. Should ghosts haunt my house? I would like it if God left me alone. Ghosts like butter, mice like ghosts, butter likes me. Poetry destroys all images and reincarnation. Why, why canÕt I live without people and Jesus and poetry? If it destroys me it destroys everything. Bombs destroy people. God and me. Are bombs created by God or are poets Gods, or is love a bomb which destroys rain?Õ 

4. 
ÔAutumn. He walked through the trees carrying a body which bumped gently against the ground, which was hard and frozen. She held his hand, softly whispering ÒDead!Ó CanÕt leaves drift under the bodies without breaking? Is Mother dying or has she died without screaming ÒDead!Ó whispered Mary. He shook the leaves off Mother and began sprinkling dirt over her grave. After death will God see her face? Will Mother laugh at God or cry ÒDead!Ó? Should we mourn her parting? Leaves tremble and fall swiftly. Time carries her scythe tenderly without cutting her throat. Leaves cut my heart, but Jesus cuts my mother. Between them their relationship seems brittle and lifeless like dead leaves.Õ 

5. 
ÔLove lost. Sunshine brightens my life. Yesterday, today, tomorrow; all my loves have flown towards oblivion. Death approaches from lost loves. Is death the answer? Can Christ save lost lovers or do angels meet flying shadows under sunlit gardens? Black night frightens angels. Dark alleys frighten lovers. Only lovers know love and see nothing but sunshine. Perhaps death hides people from the heaven-sent sunshine, or love hides from death. When you travel through darkness, hold love tightly, for you will need all the strength of your love to be unafraid of death. Why should we not love again, though we may lose our lives from lost loves? Death cannot change us, or destroy us, while God loves lovers.Õ 

6. 
ÔShipwrecks are dangerous to people on ships. Waves cause shipwrecks and are beautiful. How can beautiful waves destroy people without turning ugly? Jesus walks; untroubled footsteps sound across vast oceans of beautiful waves. Shipwrecks begin when love disappears. Jesus hears no footsteps, only the screams of the dying waves which patter his feet. Why do mermaids not hear God? Is death inevitable? Do shipwrecks begin when ears are hearing nothing but footsteps? Can I hear waves beating on nothing? Only if I make footsteps heard. Christmas comes when God is deaf to our screams, and waves become destructive and silent like JesusÕs feet.Õ 

7. 
ÔSabbat. Seven dwarfs stood silently watching. Six leprechauns, being present at the funeral, began dancing. Five Jews scrabbled in mud for money which the angels took. Three dwarfs raped two leprechauns, who said that one was enough.Õ 

8. 
ÔHells I view. Black shines brightly under white silk curtains, light filters through black windows but fades colours anyway. Windows shine at people from afar. Darkness surrounds me as I gaze at people in the street. Is my body there or am I looking at it through dark windows?Õ 

9. 
ÔSeagulls. Look at the seagulls circling above this place, like shadows falling at noon. Wings are made for flying higher and higher and swifter and stronger than anything crawling below. How do birds know what it is like to be earthbound? Perhaps they envy creatures who crawl and swim. If I could fly, like them, at the end of autumn where leaves lie brown and decaying, then I would know that God is a being who flies.Õ 

10. 
ÔSnow is gentle and cold. It falls from above us. Death is only snow. It falls to cover our lives. But we cannot melt away death like sunshine melts snow. Coldness comes, only once the snow has fallen. If I can melt my snow will also melt, but because I am never fully warm I cannot live. Terror is cold. Fear is cold and only I can depend on heat. Love is warm. Ah! If only we had love always, we could conquer, and live forever. The people who love themselves cannot melt their snow. Only you can melt my snow, for our love can never fail, for however cold it becomes, we shall love each other and therefore melt each otherÕs snows and live forever.Õ 

11. 
ÔHappiness is always transient. Perhaps we should try to be happier and better with our friends. Friendship is transient but transcience meets often with lasting friendship. Why canÕt we meet other people and make friendships last? Am I ever going to meet my friends in honest friendship? 

Or will we ever see our own friendships die through lack of love? Which is the better? I donÕt like to leave friends behind but friendship will come again. Love is permanent only when friendship and trust exists. If friendship is transient, trust must be the permanent basis of love.Õ 

12. 
ÔStrings vibrate when they feel varying pressures upon them. Sounds echo through empty buildings. Light shines brightly, but only enters through open spaces in walls. My room vibrates silently and darkly. No light enters my room. It feels no emotion, like a static building where strings never vibrate. I cannot live alone, listening to silence and seeing nothing but walls and darkness. Why does light not enter my room? Must I feel deaf to vibrating strings and see nothing? Where am I, where are you? Where are the people who play music and vibrate strings? When will I hear and see music? I canÕt tell. Only you can help me see and hear. I only live, hoping that you love me. Give me your hand, and take away my darkness and silence.Õ 

13. 
ÔSummer roses die when winter strangles the ground. Weeds flourish when roses die. I lived in a thorn bush until roses began to die, then I left my thorn bush and ran towards the sun. I felt it warm my body as I had no clothes on. Approaching dusk saw me shiver, but I still ran towards the sun, and finally dropped towards the end of life. Roses covered by body. Dawn came and warmed the roses and me. Then at noon I burned. My body could not feel pain. I stood among the flourishing roses. They did not burn. Midnight came. I tried to return to my thorn bush but I was cold. If I cannot grow into a rose, I must die, and become a weed.Õ 

14. 
ÔWalls encircle me. My heart has walls which surround my blood, beating steadily and relentlessly it pushes through my veins because I am so alive. Talk to me please. The walls are thin and crumbling. Life is being drained from within my body. Stop the current. I must break through the walls which hold my body. Death will soon release the aching heart, but I am not afraid. Here is my heart, now take a piece and smash down my walls.Õ 


If you play this game with children, then itÕs important not to insist on leading. HereÕs a ÔstoryÕ I improvised with a ÔdisturbedÕ nine-year-old boy. His words are in italics, and you can see how little I contributed. 
ÔA year ago strangely enough dead people strangled my mother. How did they do this? ÒHelp!Ó cried Thing-a-me-boober, ÒI went over Heaven and Hell, where did you stop?Ó Hell is the ugliest place I have ever seen. 
Devil George swims through waves of flame to strengthen his bones. Mother screamed when she saw George holding a stick called a pitchfork. She fainted when my friend hit her over the head with a bucket of molten metal. Meanwhile, back in my own Casbah I got very drunk.Õ 
A game can stare you in the face for years before you ÔseeÕ it. It wasnÕt until IÕd left the studio that I though of asking students to act the stories out as they told them. 
I get the actors to work in pairs, with their arms round each other, to say ÔWeÕ instead of ÔIÕ, and to use the present tense. I discourage them from putting in adjectives, or saying ÔButÕ. ItÕs normal for them to encounter something unpleasant, and to hold it off with adjectives by saying ÔWe . . . met . . . a . . . big . . . huge . . . terrifying . . . angry . . . black . . . monster . . . but . . . we . . . escaped.Õ Once theyÕve mastered the basic technique of the game (which is very easy) then I forbid them to escape from the monsters. ÔKill it or be killed,Õ I say, Ôor make friends with it, outwit it.Õ I remind them that there isnÕt really a monster, so what does it matter if they allow themselves to be torn apart? If they get eaten or killed I say ÔGo on, donÕt stop the game.Õ Then they can fight their way out of the monster, or continue in heaven, or whatever. They can mime sitting astride enormous turds and paddle through the intestines. If they get to heaven they can find God is missing and take over the place, or arm-wrestle him, or anything. 
The audience can hardly believe that itÕs possible to improvise scenes in this way, and theyÕre delighted to see actors working in such sympathy. I used to ask the audience for titles first, and I usually combined two titles to make one; the actors would then improvise Dracula and the Bald Lighthouse Keeper, or Rin- Tin-Tin and the Fall of the Roman Empire. 
Some people avoid getting involved in action. All theyÕll produce is stories like ÔWe-are-going-to-the-market-where-we-buy-bread-and-now-we.walk-to-the-beach-where-we-watch-the-seagulls . . .Õ ItÕs a good idea to start such people off inside a womb, or on another planet, or being hunted for murder, or some other dramatic situation. 
The game can be intensified by having one partner close his eyes, while the other partner stops him from bumping into the furniture. In another version both partners close their eyes, while the group stand round them and protect them. If the group is in a good state, that is to say warm and friendly, then theyÕll begin to add things to the story. If a wind is mentioned the group will spontaneously make wind noises, or perhaps flap coats around them to make a draught. If the storytellers are in the forest, then bird sounds will be made, or rustlings. Soon the group begin to dictate parts of the action, providing encounters with animals or monsters. An extraordinary energy is released, an almost sinister excitement sweeps over the group, and all sorts of sensitivity exercises are discovered. The group will ÔflyÕ the story tellers, and bury them in heaps of bodies or be ÔspidersÕ crawling all over their skins. 
ItÕs amazing to be one of the ÔstorytellersÕ because everything becomes so real for you. Once your eyes are shut, and youÕre involved in the story, and people begin to supply even very approximate effects, the brain suddenly links it all up, and fills in the gaps. If someone touches your face with a wet leaf you hallucinate a whole forest, you know what kind of trees are there, the type of animals, and so on. 
One extraordinary way to play word-at-a-time games is to ask a whole group to tell the story, all speaking together. I donÕt know how to convince you that this is possible, but most groups can succeed at it, if you approach them at the right moment. Start with everyone pressed together, and say ÔStart with ÒWeÓ and all speak at the same time.Õ I suppose it works because many people say the same words, and the minority who go ÔwrongÕ are swamped out by the majority. 
4 
ÔPlaywritingÕ 
An improviser can study status transactions, and advancing, and ÔreincorporatingÕ, and can learn to free-associate, and to generate narrative spontaneously, and yet still find it difficult to compose stories. This is really for aesthetic reasons, or conceptual reasons. He shouldnÕt really think of making up stories, but of interrupting routines. 
If I say ÔMake up a storyÕ, then most people are paralysed. If I say Ôdescribe a routine and then interrupt itÕ, people see no problem. A film like The Last Detail is based on the routine of two sailors travelling across America with a prisoner whom they have to deliver to a prison. The routine is interrupted by their decision to give him a good time. The story I fantasised earlier about the bear who chased me was presumably an interruption of the routine. ÔWalking through the forestÕ. Red Riding Hood presents an interruption of the routine ÔTaking a basket of goodies to GrandmaÕ. 
Many people think of finding more interesting routines, which doesnÕt solve the problem. It may be interesting to have a vet rectally examining an elephant, or to show brain surgeons doing a particularly delicate operation, but these activities remain routines. If two lavatory attendants break a routine by starting a brain operation, or if a window cleaner begins to examine the elephant, then this is likely to generate a narrative. Conversely, two brain surgeons working as lavatory cleaners immediately sounds like part of a story. If I describe mountaineers climbing a mountain, then the routine says that they first climb it, and then they climb down, which isnÕt much of a story. A film of a mountain climb isnÕt necessarily anything more than a documentary. If we interrupt the routine of mountain-climbing by having them discover a crashed plane, or if we snow them up and have them start eating each other, or whatever, then we begin story-telling. As a story progresses it begins to establish other routines and these in their turn have to be broken. In the story about the bear I escaped to an island and began making love to a beautiful girl. This can also be considered as a routine that itÕs necessary to interrupt. I interrupted it with the bear, but I could have chosen one of an infinity of other ways. I could have found that she was wearing a wig to hide her complete baldness, or that I was impotent, or that my penis was growing so long that it had made its way to the shore of the lake where it was being attacked by the bear. I could have discovered that she was my sisterÑMaupassant set such a story in a brothel. 
It doesnÕt matter how stupidly you interrupt a routine, you will be automatically creating a narrative, and people will listen. The scene in The Tempest where Caliban hears the clown coming works marvellously, but itÕs ludicrous. The first routine suggests that Caliban will defend himself, or leave. He crawls under a sheet. When the clown enters he sees this monster hiding under the sheet. If we treat this as a routine, then itÕs obvious that the clown runs away. What he does is incredibleÑthe very last thing anyone would do is to crawl under the sheet beside the monster. ItÕs actually the best thing to do, since it spectacularly breaks the routine. 
We could introduce this concept by getting each actor in a scene to prearrange something thatÕll surprise his partner. In a scene where a couple are about to go to bed, maybe the husband suddenly turns into a boot fetishist, or maybe the wife will suddenly start to laugh hysterically, or find sheÕs growing feathers. If you set out to do something in a scene that your partner canÕt anticipate, you automatically generate a narrative. 
Sometimes stories themselves become so predictable that they become routines. Nowadays if your princess kisses the frog, itÕs probably better if she becomes a frog herself, or if the frog she kissed just becomes six feet higher. ItÕs no good the knight killing the dragon and deflowering the virgin any more. Killing the virgin and deflowering the dragon is more likely to hold the audienceÕs attention. 
One way that storytellers wreck their talent is by cancelling. A student of mine wrote a scene in which a girl friend messed up her ex-boy friendÕs apartment in an act of revenge. He arrived and they had a row. Once the row was over and she had left, the playwright had a sensation of ÔfailureÕ, or having done nothingÑwhich was true. When I told the writer to consider the row as a routine which needed to be broken, she wrote a scene in which at the height of the row the girl suddenly injected the ex-boy friend with a syringe, and locked herself in the bathroom. One moment there was a row going on, and the next the man was suddenly terrified of what she might have done to him. 
Many students dry up at the moment they realise that the routine theyÕre describing is nearing its completion. They absolutely understand that a routine needs to be broken, or they wouldnÕt feel so unimaginative. Their problem is that they havenÕt realised whatÕs wrong consciously. Once they understand the concept of Ôinterrupting routinesÕ, then they arenÕt stuck for ideas any more. 
Another way that improvisers screw themselves up is by moving the action elsewhere. An improvisation starts with a girl asking a boy for the time. He says itÕs four oÕclock. She says that the others are late, and they begin talking about these imaginary others, and what happened last time, and the scene fizzles out. I tell them that they got diverted into a discussion of events that happened another time, and that there was nothing for the audience to see. I start them again with the opening dialogue. She asks what time it is. He says, ÔFour oÕclock,Õ I shout out: ÔSay itÕs time to begin.Õ ÔItÕs time to begin,Õ he says. ÔMust we?Õ she asks. He says, ÔWell you know how strict he isÕ, and again they begin talking about something outside the scene. I tell them I donÕt care what they do so long as the action remains onstage. ÔGet a bucket,Õ I say, and the actor mimes carrying on a bucket. ÔIs it really necessary?Õ implores the actress. ÔYes,Õ he replies, Ôopen your mouth, IÕll put the funnel in.Õ ÔIÕve put on twenty pounds in the last week,Õ she complains. ÔHe likes them fat,Õ he says, pouring the ÔcontentsÕ of the ÔbucketÕ into the ÔfunnelÕ while she pretends to be swelling up. The scene now seems inspired, and the audience are fascinated. (Speke found this scene in reality: a tribe where the kingÕs wives were forcibly fed, and sprawled about like great seals.) One of the first games I used at the studio involved getting Actor A to order Actor B about: ÔSit down. Stand up. Go to the wall. Yawn. Say ÒIÕm tired.Ó Look around. Walk to the door . . .Õ and so on. We werenÕt trying to create narratives; we only wanted the actors to get used to obeying each other, and to ordering each other around. This game (if you can call it a game) exposed them to an ÔaudienceÕ without their having to think about success or failure. 
Now that IÕm teaching ÔplaywritingÕ in a Canadian university, IÕve adapted this early game into a way of teaching narrative skills. Two students obey a third who tells them what to say and do. The third student, the ÔplaywrightÕ, will be under a certain amount of stress, but if he blocks I tell him to say ÔpromptÕ, and then someone tells him what to say next. We donÕt play the game in order to get Ôgood storiesÕ, although Ôgood storiesÕ may emerge; the important thing is to investigate exactly why the playwright ÔblocksÕ. 
A playwright who gets his two students to wash up soon stops and says ÔI canÕt think of anything.Õ If I say ÔBreak the routineÕ he has one student break a plate on purpose. He now has a quarrel which he can develop for a while, but which is also a routine. They decide to put the plate together, and find a piece is missing. They investigate and find a hold in the floor. They peer through the hole and start talking about what they see underneath. The playwright then gets stuck again. What heÕs done is to move the action offstage, so I tell him heÕs been deflected, and that heÕs to get the action onstage again. He tells them to tear up the floorboards, and the ÔblockÕ dissolves. 
An audience will remain interested if the story is advancing in some sort of organised manner, but they want to see routines interrupted, and the action continuing between the actors. When a Greek messenger comes in with some ghastly story about events that have happened somewhere else, the important thing is the effect the revelation produces on the other characters. Otherwise it stops being theatre, and becomes ÔliteratureÕ. 
A ÔplaywrightÕ begins a story by saying: ÔDennis, sit on the chair, and look ill. Betty, say ÒAre you feeling well?Ó Dennis, say ÒNo, could you get me a glass of water.Ó Betty, get Dennis a glass of water. Drink it, Dennis. Betty, say ÒHow do you feel?Ó. Dennis, say ÒMuch better nowÓ . . .Õ 
At this point the ÔplaywrightÕ becomes confused, so I stop him and explain that heÕs cancelled everything out. He introduces the idea of sickness, and then he removes it. I take the story back to when Dennis drinks the glass of water. ÔDennis, find that the water goes right though you, and is splashing on the floor under the chair. Betty, get him another glass of water. Dennis, examine yourself to try to work out what happened. Betty, give him the water, and put the glass under the chair to catch it when it runs through again. Dennis, say ÒCan you help me?Ó. Betty, say ÒYouÕll have to take your clothes off, then.Ó Dennis, mime undressing . . .Õ And so on. The level of invention is no higher, but the story is no longer being cancelled, and it holds the attention. 
ThereÕs nothing very profound about such stories, and they donÕt require much imagination, but people are very happy to watch them. The rules are: 
(1) interrupt a routine; (2) keep the action onstageÑdonÕt get diverted on to an action that has happened elsewhere, or at some other time; (3) donÕt cancel the story. 
5 
I began this essay by saying that an improviser shouldnÕt be concerned with content, because the content arrives automatically. This is true, and also not true. The best improvisers do, at some level, know what their work is about. They may have trouble expressing it to you, but they do understand the implications of what they are doing; and so do the audience. 
I think of an improvisation we did years ago: Anthony Trent played being a prisoner in a cell. Lucy Fleming arrived, I donÕt remember how, and he endowed her with invisibility. At first he was terrified, but she calmed him down, and said she had come to rescue him. She led him out of the prison and as he stepped free he fell dead. It had the same kind of effect as Ambrose BierceÕs story Incident at Owl Creek Bridge. 
I remember Richardson Morgan playing a scene in which I said he was to be fired, and in which he said he was failing at his work because he had cancer. I think Ben Benison was the boss and he treated Ric with amazing harshness. It was about the cruellest scene IÕve ever seen and the audience were hysterical with laughter. IÕve never heard people laugh more. The actors seemed to be dragging all the audienceÕs greatest fears into the open, laying out all their insecurities, and the anxiety was releasing itself in waves of roaring, tearing laughter, and the actors absolutely knew what they were doing, and just how slowly to turn the screw. 
You have to trick students into believing that content isnÕt important and that it looks after itself, or they never get anywhere. ItÕs the same kind of trick you use when you tell them that they are not their imaginations, that their imaginations have nothing to do with them, and that theyÕre in no way responsible for what their ÔmindÕ gives them. In the end they learn how to abandon control while at the same time they exercise control. They begin to understand that everything is just a shell. You have to misdirect people to absolve them of responsibility. Then, much later, they become strong enough to resume the responsibility themselves. By that time they have a more truthful concept of what they are. 


Masks and Trance 

1 
George Devine 
George Devine gave a Mask class to the Royal Court writersÕ group in 1958. He arrived with a box full of dusty Masks that had last been used some years before at the Old Vic Theatre School. I didnÕt like the look of them: they reminded me of surgical prostheses, and I didnÕt like what he was saying either. Already I was feeling threatened. George talked to us for about forty minutes, and then gave us a demonstration. He retired to the far end of the long, shadowy room, put a Mask on, looked in a mirror, and turned to face usÑor rather ÔitÕ, Ôthe MaskÕ, turned to face us. We saw a Ôtoad-godÕ who laughed and laughed as if we were funny and despicable. I donÕt know how long the ÔsceneÕ lasted, it was timeless. Then George removed the Mask and suggested that we try. 
Next day he was despondent. He thought the class had been a failure and that this had been his fault. He said that none of the Masks had been ÔinhabitedÕ, by which he meant that none of us had been possessed by the Masks. I tried to explain how amazed weÕd been, but he insisted that the class had been a poor one, and that I was wrong to be so enthusiastic. 
William Gaskill borrowed the Masks, and began to give Mask classes along the lines laid down by George. He collected some old clothes, and some props, and developed the theory that the actor should shock himself with the MaskÕs reflection. The time at the mirror was to be kept short, and the student was to be pushed into acting on whatever impulse came to him. GeorgeÕs ideas related to Oriental theatre (Noguchi had designed his King Lear) but we had seen the toad-god, and thought more in terms of voodoo than the Noh Theatre. Gaskill persuaded me to give Mask classes as well: ÔIt shouldnÕt all be left to me,Õ he said, and we both gave classes to groups who visited the theatre. 
ItÕs true that an actor can wear a Mask casually, and just pretend to be another person, but Gaskill and myself were absolutely clear that we were trying to induce trance states. The reason why one automatically talks and writes of Masks with a capital ÔMÕ is that one really feels that the genuine Mask actor is inhabited by a spirit. Nonsense perhaps, but thatÕs what the experience is like, and has always been like. To understand the Mask itÕs also necessary to understand the nature of trance itself. 
One day Devine invited me to lunch, which he never did unless he wanted to discuss something. He was embarrassed (he was actually a shy man), and finally when we were almost through coffee and the restaurant was practically empty, he said that he thought Bill and I had misunderstood the nature of the Mask. At this time George was giving comedy classes at the Studio, so I suggested a swop: I would give his comedy classes, and he would give my Mask classes. 
George allowed his students to work in a very casual way. Bill and I had tried to condition a response to the wearing of a Mask by insisting that whenever one was on the face, the actor should attempt to enter the ÔMask stateÕ. This led to Masks being handled as if sacred. George shocked me by allowing actors to talk as themselves while actually wearing the Masks. TheyÕd choose clothes or wander about with the Masks on without any attempt to be in character. I think George was overreacting to the way weÕd been teaching, because even in performance these Masks often spoke with the wearerÕs voice, although George had explained that theyÕd need speech lessons before they could speak Ôas the MasksÕ. Eventually, George said that the students who had worked first with William Gaskill and myself were usually the better ones, so that our method must have something to recommend it. I think this was because we used to hurl the students into the work, whereas George was much gentler. He was very good at explaining exactly when a Mask was ÔinhabitedÕ, but it was really up to the actors. Many of his students played safe, and kept to their preferred areas acting with Masks on, rather than being possessed. GeorgeÕs attitude was really very different from mine, and possibly GaskillÕs; George was primarily interested in developing characters that could be used without the Mask when the actor was cast in plays. I saw the Masks as astounding performers, as offering a new form of theatre, and I didnÕt care what Mask creatures arrived, so long as they were possessed. The Masks we were using covered the top half of the face, leaving the mouth and lower half of the cheeks exposed. George had learnt the technique from Michel Saint-Denis in the 1930s, and Michel had been taught by Jacques Copeau (his uncle). These half masks are usually called Ôcomic masksÕ but George called them ÔCharacter MasksÕ. He thought it important to hand on the tradition unchanged, and he was shocked when he found that I was mixing Character Masks with Tragic Masks (Tragic Masks work by a quite different techniqueÑsee page 184). My students showed him one of the mixed scenes they had prepared with me, and they reported him as saying that it did work, but that he still didnÕt like it! When I visited him during his last illness, almost the last thing he said to me was ÔI still donÕt think that Mask work was right.Õ 
George cited ChaplinÕs Tramp as a Mask, since the character had come from the clothes and the make-up. HereÕs ChaplinÕs own account (from his autobiography). 
ÔOn the way to the wardrobe I thought I would dress in baggy pants, big shoes, and a cane and a derby hat. I wanted everything to be a contradiction; the pants baggy, the coat tight, the hat small and the shoes large. I was undecided whether to look young or old, but remembering Sennett had expected me to be a much older man, I added a small moustache which, I reasoned, would add age without hiding my expression . . . 
Ô. . . I had no idea of the character. But the moment I was dressed, the clothes and make-up made me feel the kind of person he was. I began to know him, and by the time I walked on the stage he was fully born. When I confronted Sennett I assumed the character and strutted about, swinging my cane and parading before him. Gags and comedy ideas went racing through my mind . . . 
Ô. . . My character was different and unfamiliar to the Americans. But with the clothes on I felt he was a reality, a living person. In fact he ignited all sorts of crazy ideas that I would never have dreamt of until I was dressed and made-up as the Tramp.Õ 
Elsewhere Chaplin has said, ÔI realised I would have to spend the rest of my life finding out about the creature. For me he was fixed, complete, the moment I looked in the mirror and saw him for the first time, yet even now I donÕt know all the things that are to be known about him.Õ1 
(Isabel Quigly, Charlie ChaplinÑEarly Comedies, Studio Vista, 1968.) 2 
Russians 
At first I thought that Mask work was completely unlike StanislavskyÕs concept of actor training, but this isnÕt true. HereÕs Stanislavsky describing the Mask state in Building a Character. Kostya, a drama student, has been told to put on a character make-up, but nothing satisfies him. He creams his face to remove the greasepaint and then, unexpectedly . . . 
ÔAll the other colours blurred . . . It was difficult to distinguish where my nose was, or my eyes, or my lips. I smeared some of the same cream on my beard and moustache and then finally all over my wig. Some of the hair clotted into lumps . . . and then, almost as if I were in some delirium, I trembled, my heart pounded, I did away with my eyebrows, powdered myself at random, smeared the back of my hands with a greenish colour and the palms with light pink. I did all this with a quick, sure touch, for this time I knew who I was representing, and what kind of fellow he was!Õ 
He then paced the room feeling Ôhow all the parts of my body, features, facial lines, fell into their proper places and established themselves . . . I glanced in the mirror and did not recognise myself. Since I had looked into it the last time a fresh transformation had taken place in me. ÒIt is he, it is he!Ó I exclaimed . . .Õ 
He presents himself to the director (Tortsov), introducing himself as Ôthe criticÕ. HeÕs surprised to find his body doing things by itself, things he hadnÕt intended. 
ÔQuite unexpectedly my twisted leg came out in advance of me and threw my body more to the right. I removed my top hat with careful exaggeration and executed a polite bow . . .Õ 
He then played a scene with the director, having no difficulty in sustaining this weird character he had become, and knowing always exactly what to say. Later Kostya reflects: ÔCan I really say that this creature is not part of me? I derived him from my own nature. I divided myself, as it were, into two personalities. One continued as an actor, the other as an observer.Õ 
At the time when Copeau was working with Masks in France, StanislavskyÕs favourite pupil Vakhtangov was working with them in Russia. Nikolai Gorchakov has left an account of those rehearsals. Vakhtangov set up a circus in which the Masks were to be auditioned as clowns. They were to do things that would make the spectators Ô. . . applaud wildly, rush on the stage and hug and kiss you! Or at least roll on the floor with laughter. Go ahead, start! . . .Õ 
Vakhtangov threw an incredible number of instructions to the Masks until they were lost and confused. Someone played circus music and they had no choice but to perform. They tried imaginary gymnastics, and ice-skated, and pretended to juggle, and finally succeeded in getting warm applause from the onlookers. ÔDo you really think youÕve hit on the ÒgrainÓ of the Masks merely by doing a few exercises in front of the audience?Õ said Vakhtangov. ÔYou havenÕt even started to act as Masks! . . . You must vie with one another in captivating the audience by every possible meansÑtalk, act, dance, sing, do acrobatics, do anything. Understand?Õ 
Things got worse until finally Vahktangov left, the Masks continuing without him. Suddenly two of them became genuine Masks in the characters of Tartaglia and Pantalone. Tartaglia was eating a cake, and Pantalone was starving. Tartaglia spoke with a stutter (which was unexpected) and said that Pantalone would have to earn it, but he Ôgraciously allowed Pantalone to eat the crumbs remaining on his palm each time he swallowed a bit. This made Pantalone very happy, and it was fun watching him pick up these crumbs while Tartaglia lectured him on the necessity of work . . . 
ÔThe episode was fairly long, but we were so enchanted we did not notice it. We were not much interested in their chatter, but were fascinated by their naive seriousness, the kind one sees only in two children when one is sucking a toffee and the other cannot tear his eyes away from this sweet process, looking enviously at the happy owner . . . 
ÔTartaglia was so carried away that he began to tease Pantalone, passing the cake under his nose every time he nipped a bit off. Suddenly, Pantalone opened wide his mouth and snapped up about three-quarters of what remained. Tartaglia burst into tears while Pantalone, his mouth full, gestured that it served him rightÑhe should not have teased him.Õ 
Vakhtangov had been watching from a doorway. He immediately set up another scene with the same characters. Pantalone was to be a dentist, and Tartaglia his patient. Tartaglia panicked, stepped out of character and said he didnÕt want to do it. 
Ô ÒAnd what do the dentists say to that?Ó Vakhangov turned gravely to his partners. 
Ô ÒThere have been many cases in history of medicine of patients refusing to be treated,Ó Kudryavtsev replies seriously, without forgetting that he was Pantalone, the learned secretary of King AltoumÕs court. ÒSuch refusal is a sure sign of illness. In this particular case I presume weÕll have to extract the aching teeth not only through the mouth, but also through other apertures, ears and nostrils included . . .Ó Õ 
Afterwards Vakhtangov commented that they had at first tried to Ôthink upÕ what to do. ÔI donÕt deny the importance of thinking, inventing or planning, but if you have to improvise on the spot (and thatÕs exactly what we have to do), you must act and not think. ItÕs action we must haveÑwise, foolish or naive, simple or complicated, but action.Õ (Nikolai Gorchakov, The Vakhtangov School of Stage Art, Foreign Languages Publishing House, Moscow.) Vakhangov forced his students to act spontaneously. This produces a light trance state in which the actors feel as if something else is controlling them. They ÔknowÕ what to do, whereas normally they ÔchooseÕ what to do. The state is regressive, but they experience no self-consciousness. 
3 

Destroying the Mask 
Masks seem exotic when you first learn about them, but to my mind Mask acting is no stranger than any other kind: no more weird than the fact that an actor can blush when his character is embarrassed, or turn white with fear, or that a cold will stop for the duration of the performance, and then start streaming again as soon as the curtain falls. ÔWhatÕs Hecuba to him?Õ asks Hamlet, and the mystery remains. Actors can be possessed by the character they play just as they can be possessed by Masks. Many actors have been unable to really ÔfindÕ a character until they put on the make-up, or until they try on the wig, or the costume. We find the Mask strange because we donÕt understand how irrational our responses to the face are anyway, and we donÕt realise that much of our lives is spent in some form of trance, i.e. absorbed. What we assume to be Ônormal consciousnessÕ is comparatively rare, itÕs like the light in the refrigerator: when you look in, there you are ON but whatÕs happening when you donÕt look in? 
ItÕs difficult to understand the power of the Mask if youÕve only seen it an illustrations, or in museums. The Mask in the showcase may have been intended as an ornament on the top of a vibrating, swishing haystack. Exhibited without its costume, and without a film, or even photograph, of the Mask in use, we respond to it only as an aesthetic object. Many Masks are beautiful or striking, but thatÕs not the point. A Mask is a device for driving the personality out of the body and allowing a spirit to take possession of it. A very beautiful Mask may be completely dead, while a piece of old sacking with a mouth and eye-holes torn in it may have tremendous vitality.
